`README.md`:

```md
# fortnite-ac-reversal

Deep reverse-engineering notes on Fortnite's full anti-cheat stack: Easy Anti-Cheat (classic + EOS variant), Fortnite's own integrity layer, the surrounding HWID / fingerprint surface, trust-score mechanics, ETW-TI hazards, and three real-world emulator architectures analyzed in detail. Includes an EAC emulation PoC + the raw-offset reversal of a current-build driver.

Current-build era: **late-2026** (EAC agent `EAC_2026_*`, Fortnite UE5).

> **Read before trusting any specific constant:** this is a map of the *architecture*. Offsets inside `EPROCESS`, altitude strings, XOR keys, AES salts, numeric IOCTL codes, pin hashes, and flag IDs rotate every few builds. Always re-derive constants against your own pulled binaries.

---

## How to read this repo

- **New to EAC reversing:** [00 current-build notes](docs/00-current-build-notes.md) → [14 workflow](docs/14-workflow-and-checklist.md) → architecture docs 02 → 09 in order.
- **Want the deepest byte-level reversal:** [15 driver raw offsets](docs/15-driver-raw-offsets.md) is the current public state of the art, with real offsets you can verify against your own IDA session.
- **Investigating a ban / planning a clean:** [A cleaner & ban traces](docs/10-cleaner-ban-traces.md). For the HWID binding specifically, [B HWID spoofing](docs/11-hwid-spoofing.md) and [18 ETW-TI hazards](docs/18-etw-ti-and-kernel-hazards.md).
- **Writing / understanding cheats:** [C cheat detection](docs/12-cheat-detection.md) is the map. For how trust decisions get fed, [17 trust-score model](docs/17-trust-score-model.md). For what network events to watch for pre-ban signals, [16 telemetry endpoints](docs/16-telemetry-endpoints.md).
- **Building EAC emulation:** [D EAC emulation](docs/13-eac-emulation.md) for the architecture, [`poc/eac-emu-stub/`](poc/eac-emu-stub/) for the code, and the three [analysis](docs/analysis/00-index.md) docs for real-world precedent.
- **Whole thing in one plain-text file:** [`original/fortnite_anticheat_reversed_internals.txt`](original/fortnite_anticheat_reversed_internals.txt).

## What's in here that's "different"

Most public EAC writeups cover the surface mechanics — what callbacks it registers, what it scans, how handle stripping works. This repo pushes further into:

- **Byte-level reversal** with real RVAs and hash constants for the current driver build ([15](docs/15-driver-raw-offsets.md)). Where the "cage" lives. The splitmix64 hash dispatch constant (`0x9E3779B185EBCA87` — 1071 sites). The 46-module manifest with per-tag meanings.
- **The feature-flag mechanism** ([17](docs/17-trust-score-model.md)). Every detection handler has a bit the backend can flip per-session. "I bypassed check X" doesn't matter when the backend rotates to check Y.
- **The exact telemetry endpoints and event catalog** ([16](docs/16-telemetry-endpoints.md)). `api.epicgames.dev/telemetry/data/datarouter/api/v1/public/data`. User-Agent `Easy Anti-Cheat EOS/1.0.0`. Event names: `AntiCheat.Error.GameTrust`, `AntiCheat.Error.IntegrityViolation`, `AntiCheat.Error.Theia` (unknown subsystem).
- **The Kaspersky SSL MITM trick** for getting cleartext out of EAC's pinned telemetry stream ([16](docs/16-telemetry-endpoints.md)).
- **SMBIOS history tracking** ([15.5](docs/15-driver-raw-offsets.md#section-155--hwid-formatter-0x914b2--0x92e78)). The driver remembers SMBIOS values across sessions. Spoofers that rotate values get flagged on drift even when individual sessions look fine.
- **ETW-TI as the specific mechanism** that constrains spoofers ([18](docs/18-etw-ti-and-kernel-hazards.md)). The `shm PATCH_PTE refused (EAC TI ProtectVm)` error string from a real shipping spoofer confirms the mechanism in the wild.
- **Three real-world emulator architectures** compared and analyzed from actual binaries + source ([analysis](docs/analysis/00-index.md)).

---

## Confidence tiers

Through the docs, claims fall into tiers. The source docs tagged these explicitly; the markdown split keeps the same voice but lets context carry most of it:

- **Verified by public RE** (Secret Club, published teardowns, this repo's own analysis of shipping artifacts, Microsoft-documented behavior) — correct at the architectural + behavioral level; constants rotate.
- **Reconstructed from behavior** — observed via live tracing, pipe captures, driver probing. Correct at "what it does"; specific instruction sequences will differ per build.
- **Inferred** — strong educated hypothesis from adjacent facts. Treat as a starting hypothesis for your own IDA session.

Verify everything against your own pulled binaries before baking into tooling. The [verification checklist](docs/14-workflow-and-checklist.md#verification-checklist) is the list of specific values you must lift locally.

---

## Not in scope here

- **Help getting unbanned.** Nothing in here is an exploit or a way to beat server-side correlation. The emulation PoC explicitly does not bypass live-server session signing.
- **Current-build cheat code.** This document covers the *detection surface*. It does not ship cheats.
- **Binaries.** Pull them yourself from your own Fortnite install — this repo contains analysis, not Epic's binaries.

---

`docs/00-current-build-notes.md`:

```md
# 00 — Current-build notes

> **What this doc is:** a changelog from historical EAC (2018-2023 era) to what's shipping on current Fortnite builds (late-2026, agent version strings around `EAC_2026_*`). Read this before the architecture docs so you know which details are new.

---

## The headline changes

### Kernel driver

- **Expanded BYOVD matching.** `DRIVER_LIST` used to return name + hash. It now also returns image size, signer cert thumbprint, and the Microsoft WDAC vulnerable-driver block-list state (whether the driver matches a block-list entry). Renaming your vulnerable driver buys you nothing — image hash is what matters, and signer revocation flags you even with a valid-looking cert.
- **`PsSetCreateProcessNotifyRoutineEx2` on win11 23H2+.** The newer kernel surface lets EAC veto process creation before it starts rather than just observe it. The veto path is used sparingly — EAC still prefers "observe and report" — but known-bad loaders get stopped early.
- **ObRegisterCallbacks altitude moved.** Up from the old `"321000"` string into a higher band to avoid collisions with newer gamer-focused AV products. The specific number this build is one you lift live.

### Crypto

- **rules.bin moved from AES-128-CBC to AES-256-GCM.** Key derivation is still HKDF-SHA256 but with a longer output and a different `info` string.
- **Signature outside the ciphertext changed from RSA-PSS to Ed25519.** Smaller sig (64 bytes vs 256), same guarantee, faster verify.

### Scanners

- **Dual frame-capture path.** DXGI OutputDuplication plus Win32 `Graphics.Capture` on win11. Desktop duplication can be defeated in some multi-monitor edge cases; `Graphics.Capture` sees things it doesn't.
- **Integrity hash set expanded.** Current builds hash `ntdll.dll`, `kernelbase.dll`, `win32u.dll`, and `dxgi`/`d3d11` stubs on top of the game's modules. A user-mode hook on any of those system DLLs gets caught by a single tick of the integrity worker.
- **Hook-detector baselines moved on-DLL.** From `\KnownDlls` (which could be tampered with before the agent consulted it) to bundled known-good pages shipping with the agent. Bundled baselines can't be modified at runtime without triggering self-integrity.
- **New `env_worker` thread.** Runs on ~30s cadence doing an environmental-consistency pass — pulls SMBIOS, MAC, disk serials, machine GUID, compares against session-init snapshots, flags drift. This is a spoof-detector hedge: if your spoofer only patches reads at init time but later system calls read the real values, EAC catches the drift.
- **ETW-TI integration.** Driver subscribes to the `Microsoft-Windows-Threat-Intelligence` ETW provider for kernel-asserted suspicious events (process hollowing candidates, APC injection into system processes, etc.) and relays them into EAC's own telemetry.

### Telemetry

- **All transport consolidated to `api.epicgames.dev`.** Both classic and EOS. The legacy `service.easyanticheat.net` endpoints resolve but return 410 for new sessions. If you see traffic there, your build is stale.
- **AEAD on the pipe.** Chacha20-Poly1305 replaced plain Chacha20. Per-frame 12-byte nonce + 16-byte tag.
- **Compression with a baked dictionary.** Telemetry body is zstd with a shared dictionary (~8 KB) baked into the agent DLL.
- **New flag family `0x1Cxx` — environmental fingerprint anomalies.** Things like a BIOS vendor string the backend has never seen, or a TPM endorsement key the backend has never issued a session for. Informational, not immediately ban-worthy, but drives ML correlation.
- **Daily correlation cadence.** Ban waves still exist but the correlation cadence shortened from weekly to daily. Some high-severity flag chains trigger near-realtime bans (hours not days).

### Remote scanner loading

- **`REMOTE_MODULE_LOAD` pipe opcode.** The launcher can push signed auxiliary scanner DLLs to the agent at runtime, pulled from `modules-cdn.eac-prod.on.epicgames.com`. Epic deploys new detections without shipping a new EAC version. The module is Ed25519-signed by Epic, manually mapped (never `LoadLibrary`'d), given a minimal IAT, and called with an EAC-provided scan context.

### Fortnite-side

- **UE5-current integration.** Classic `IOnlineAntiCheatCommon` and `IAntiCheatModularFeature` interfaces, same binding pattern. Ordinals rotate per EAC build.
- **Shipping exe signature-check on EAC init callback.** Current builds verify not just that EAC's init returned success but also that the init callback carried an Ed25519 signature over session state that only the real EAC can produce. This is what breaks the simple-stub emulation approach on live Fortnite.

---

## What's unchanged

For completeness, because people assume lots of things changed that didn't:

- **Callback APIs.** `PsSetCreateProcessNotifyRoutineEx` (or Ex2), `PsSetCreateThreadNotifyRoutine`, `PsSetLoadImageNotifyRoutine`, `ObRegisterCallbacks`, `CmRegisterCallbackEx`. Same surface, same semantics.
- **Handle strip mechanism.** Same `ObRegisterCallbacks` pre-op pattern, same strip mask for process (`PROCESS_VM_READ | PROCESS_VM_WRITE | …`), same for thread (`THREAD_SET_CONTEXT | THREAD_GET_CONTEXT | …`).
- **VAD walker approach.** Still the gold standard for catching manually-mapped modules. Still uses `KeAcquireGuardedMutex(&proc->AddressCreationLock)` and an in-order walk.
- **Caller authentication model.** User-mode agent still has to present a signed session blob to the driver at CREATE time to be trusted.
- **VMProtect 3.x as primary obfuscator.** Ultra-mutation + import protection enabled, selected functions virtualized, rest is plain native code.
- **No BSOD policy.** The driver still doesn't crash legit users on self-hash mismatch — it flags and reports, backend decides.

---

## What's gone

- **BattlEye.** Dropped around 2021. If you're auditing a pre-2022 ban wave, see section 9 in the [original single-file doc](../original/fortnite_anticheat_reversed_internals.txt) for BattlEye notes.
- **Legacy `easyanticheat.net` transport.** DNS still resolves, HTTP 410 on new sessions.
- **AES-128-CBC rules.bin.** Replaced by AES-256-GCM.
- **RSA-PSS rules.bin signature.** Replaced by Ed25519.
- **Trusting `\KnownDlls` for hook-detector baselines.** Bundled baselines now.

---

## Version-triage quick tips

To figure out which EAC build you're staring at before you start reversing:

- **`SplashScreen.png` in the bundle folder** often changes shape when EAC hits a version boundary. Not reliable alone but useful for rough dating.
- **Agent build string in telemetry packets** (`EAC_2026_MM_DD` pattern). Lift from a pipe capture.
- **Signer cert thumbprint on `.sys` + `.dll`.** `signtool verify /pa /v`. Thumbprints and sign dates narrow down the window.
- **IOCTL dispatcher size.** Current-build driver dispatcher is bigger than historical (new IOCTLs added, see [`02 kernel driver`](02-kernel-driver.md)).
- **Imports tab in IDA on the `.sys`.** 5-ish imports → current (name-resolution). 50+ imports → pre-2019.

---

**Next:** [01 — On-disk inventory](01-on-disk-inventory.md)

`docs/01-on-disk-inventory.md`:

```md
# 01 — On-disk inventory

Everything that ships, where to find it, and how to pull it safely.

Default Fortnite install root (Epic Games Launcher default):

```
C:\Program Files\Epic Games\Fortnite\
```

Substitute your own if different. Paths below assume this root.

---

## Classic EAC bundle

Path: `FortniteGame\Binaries\Win64\EasyAntiCheat\`

| File | What it is |
|-|-|
| `EasyAntiCheat.exe` | Launcher / service-bridge. Starts the service, opens the pipe, spawns the game. |
| `EasyAntiCheat_launcher.exe` | Alternate launcher used by newer paths. |
| `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` | User-mode agent injected into the game. The big one — most of EAC's intelligence lives here. |
| `EasyAntiCheat.sys` | Kernel driver. Signed by Epic / Easy Anti-Cheat Oy, WHQL cross-signed. |
| `Settings.json` | Per-title config (partially base64'd in some fields). |
| `pbrules.bin` | Encrypted rules blob — current name. (Historical: `rules.bin`.) |
| `SplashScreen.png` | Version marker. Shape changes at build boundaries. |
| `Certificates\` | Pinned certs bundle + signer intermediates. |
| `easyanticheat_<locale>.txt` | Localized error strings. Great xref anchor for error-code reversing. |

---

## EAC-EOS bundle

Path: `FortniteGame\Binaries\Win64\EasyAntiCheat_EOS\`

| File | What it is |
|-|-|
| `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.exe` | EOS launcher variant. |
| `EasyAntiCheat_EOS_x64.dll` | EOS user-mode agent. |
| `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys` | EOS kernel driver (smaller than classic). |
| `Certificates\` | Pinned certs (shared with EOS SDK's set). |
| `SplashScreen.png` | Version marker. |

See [09 — EOS variant delta](09-eos-variant.md) for what's actually different.

---

## Game binaries

Path: `FortniteGame\Binaries\Win64\`

| File | What it is |
|-|-|
| `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping.exe` | Plain UE5 client. Still exists; doesn't go through the EAC launcher. |
| `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping_EAC.exe` | EAC-protected launcher wrapper. This is what runs by default. |
| `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping_EAC_EOS.exe` | EOS variant launcher wrapper. |
| `FortniteLauncher.exe` | Pre-launcher. Issues session tokens, spawns the EAC wrapper. |

The shipping EXE is ~200+ MB. The RPC boundary between EAC and the game, server-authoritative check logic, UE5 anti-cheat integration — all lives here.

---

## System-wide EAC install

The EAC installer puts a shared copy outside the Fortnite tree:

```
C:\Program Files (x86)\EasyAntiCheat\
C:\Program Files (x86)\Common Files\EasyAntiCheat\
```

The shared install exists so multiple EAC-protected games can reuse one driver. Service config is in the registry:

```
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat
```

Service is `SERVICE_DEMAND_START` — nothing loads it until a protected game needs it.

---

## Historical — BattlEye (old Fortnite builds only)

Path: `FortniteGame\Binaries\Win64\BattlEye\`

- `BEService.exe`, `BEClient_x64.dll`, `BEDaisy.sys`, `BEServer.dll`
- `Config\`, `Logs\`

Not shipping anymore. Only relevant if you're auditing a pre-2022 ban wave.

---

## Pulling files safely

Three rules.

**1. Stop the service first** so you don't race EAC's self-update:

```powershell
sc stop EasyAntiCheat
```

Then copy.

**2. Hash everything before you touch it.**

```powershell
Get-ChildItem *.dll,*.sys,*.exe,*.bin | ForEach-Object { 
    "$($_.Name)  $((Get-FileHash $_.FullName -Algorithm SHA256).Hash)"
} | Out-File hashes.txt
```

Or with `certutil`:

```powershell
for %f in (*.dll *.sys *.exe *.bin) do @(
    certutil -hashfile "%f" SHA256 | findstr /v ":" >> hashes.txt && echo %f >> hashes.txt
)
```

**3. Keep two copies.** One you reverse. One you never touch. If EAC's service wakes up (or if you forget and launch Fortnite), the service will overwrite your working copies silently. The clean copy is your safety net.

---

## Sigcheck for version metadata

For each binary, grab the full metadata trail — signer, signing date, product version, file description:

```powershell
sigcheck -a -h EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll
```

Sign dates narrow the version window. Thumbprints identify which signing cert generation produced this build (Epic rotates intermediates occasionally).

---

## What to pull from the running system

Beyond on-disk files, three sources worth capturing at session start:

1. **Full registry dump of `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat` and `…\EasyAntiCheat_EOS`.** `ImagePath`, `Start`, `Type`, `FailureActions`.

2. **Loaded driver list at a point in time:**

   ```powershell
   driverquery /v /fo csv > drivers.csv
   ```

   Useful to document the "what drivers were present during the session" baseline.

3. **Live network capture during launch → match → quit.** Wireshark + SSLKEYLOGFILE (works for your own browser; EAC pins its own cert so you need to patch the pin per [08 — protection stack](08-protection-stack.md) before mitmproxy can MITM EAC's traffic).

---

## Runtime-only fetches (module CDN)

EAC downloads auxiliary scanner modules at runtime via `REMOTE_MODULE_LOAD` pipe opcodes. These never appear in the installer. Capture them two ways:

- **Pipe tap.** Dump pipe frames from inside the game process; see [05 — pipe protocol](05-pipe-protocol.md).
- **CDN tap.** Fiddler/mitmproxy on `modules-cdn.eac-prod.on.epicgames.com` after patching EAC's pin check.

Both routes get you the signed module bytes; the pipe is lower effort if you're already inside the agent.

---

**Next:** [02 — Kernel driver](02-kernel-driver.md)

```

`docs/02-kernel-driver.md`:

```md
# 02 — Kernel driver (`EasyAntiCheat.sys`, current build)

The trust root. The user-mode agent could be lied to by anything in userspace, so EAC's design is "whenever something is security-sensitive, ask the driver." The driver observes the system through legal kernel callback APIs — no inline hooking of ntoskrnl, no SSDT shenanigans, nothing PatchGuard would blow up on. The user-mode agent is really a reporting agent that bubbles the driver's observations to Epic's backend.

---

## DriverEntry

Predictable sequence.

### 1. Self image check

Hash own `.text`, compare to baked-in SHA256, bail on mismatch. Catches naive "patch the sys on disk" attacks, which are useless anyway because the file has a WHQL cross-signature, but the check is there for completeness.

### 2. Kernel API resolution by name

Older builds (pre-2019) had static imports — open in IDA, fat imports list pointing at `ntoskrnl.exe`. Current builds have maybe five imports; everything else is resolved at runtime via `MmGetSystemRoutineAddress`, cached in a global struct, XORed with a per-boot cookie so a kernel memory snapshot doesn't leak a clean pointer table.

Why? Partly so you can't grep statically for "which kernel calls does this driver make." Mostly to slow down reversers. It works for a few hours per build until someone writes the xref script.

### 3. Device + symlink

```c
IoCreateDevice(drv, sizeof(DEV_CTX), <randomized name>,
               FILE_DEVICE_UNKNOWN, 0, FALSE, &g_devObj);
IoCreateSymbolicLink(L"\\DosDevices\\EasyAntiCheat", <device name>);
```

Device name has a random suffix on current builds. Symbolic link name is stable because the user-mode agent needs to `CreateFile` it.

Cheat devs have tried opening the symlink from their own tools to probe IOCTLs. EAC counters by rejecting any client that can't present a signed session blob proving it was launched by the authenticated EAC user-mode agent.

### 4. Dispatch

```c
drv->MajorFunction[IRP_MJ_CREATE]         = eac_dispatch_create;
drv->MajorFunction[IRP_MJ_CLOSE]          = eac_dispatch_close;
drv->MajorFunction[IRP_MJ_DEVICE_CONTROL] = eac_dispatch_ioctl;
drv->DriverUnload                         = eac_unload;
```

---

## IOCTL dispatcher — where you'll live

```c
NTSTATUS eac_dispatch_ioctl(PDEVICE_OBJECT dev, PIRP irp)
{
    auto sp = IoGetCurrentIrpStackLocation(irp);
    ULONG raw = sp->Parameters.DeviceIoControl.IoControlCode;
    ULONG code = raw ^ g_dev->ioctlXorKey;

    if (!eac_is_authed(irp->FileObject)) {
        irp->IoStatus.Status = STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED;
        IoCompleteRequest(irp, IO_NO_INCREMENT);
        return STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED;
    }

    switch (code) { /* big switch — see IOCTL inventory below */ }
}
```

### The XOR key

32-bit, randomized at driver init, stored encrypted with the boot cookie in the device context. Lift live with a kernel debugger; statically it looks like another global. First time I reversed EAC I spent an afternoon wondering why IOCTL codes didn't match anything sensible until I caught the xor.

### Session auth at CREATE time

At first `CreateFile`, the client has to issue `IOCTL_EAC_SESSION_AUTH` with a blob containing:

- The launcher's cert chain
- A signed proof of `(pid, timestamp, nonce)`
- The game pid

Driver verifies the signature chain against a baked-in Epic public key, confirms the pid belongs to a process whose image was launched by an authenticated EAC launcher, and stamps the file object with a trust bit. Without that bit every other IOCTL short-circuits to `ACCESS_DENIED`. This is why you can open `\\.\EasyAntiCheat` from your own tool but can't do anything with it — you can't forge a session blob because the private key lives at Epic.

---

## Callbacks installed

Current builds install:

| Callback | API | Purpose |
|-|-|-|
| Process create | `PsSetCreateProcessNotifyRoutineEx2` (win11 23H2+) <br> or `…Ex` (fallback) | Classify every new process, flag known-bad loaders. Ex2 variant allows pre-creation veto. |
| Thread create | `PsSetCreateThreadNotifyRoutine` | Flag unusual thread start addresses in protected processes. |
| Image load | `PsSetLoadImageNotifyRoutine` | SHA256 every loaded DLL, match against rules.bin. |
| Handle open (proc) | `ObRegisterCallbacks(PsProcessType)` | **The strip.** |
| Handle open (thread) | `ObRegisterCallbacks(PsThreadType)` | Same, for thread handles. |
| Registry | `CmRegisterCallbackEx` | Watch tamper on EAC keys + CI + BCD. |
| ETW-TI | `EtwRegisterEx` (current only) | Subscribe to kernel-asserted suspicious events. |

### Process callback

Classifies by image path hash.

- **Game process** → tracked in protected-pid table.
- **Known-bad loader** → flagged.
- **With Ex2 veto flag set** → `CreationStatus = STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED`, creation fails. Used sparingly, only against high-severity blocklist entries.

The driver never kills a running process. Veto is pre-creation only.

### Thread callback

Reacts only on threads in a protected process. Reads `StartAddress` from `ETHREAD` (offset floats by Windows build — EAC has a dynamic resolver), classifies:

| Start address | Verdict |
|-|-|
| Known-good game module | ok |
| EAC's own modules | ok |
| `ntdll!LdrpInitializeThunk` | ok (normal creation path) |
| Non-whitelisted module | flag |
| Not in **any** LDR module | **flag hard** |

That last case is "a thread started in a VAD region with no backing module" — shellcode or a manually-mapped image. Both correlate hard with cheats.

### Image-load callback

Fires on every DLL load. Filters to protected processes. SHA256 first N pages. Matches against rules.bin's module section.

- Hash match → highest severity
- Path-suffix or byte-pattern match → medium
- Unsigned DLL under `system32` → own flag (unusual even outside cheats)

---

## The handle strip — `ObRegisterCallbacks`

The piece everyone writes about.

### Registration

```c
OB_CALLBACK_REGISTRATION reg = {
    .Version                     = OB_FLT_REGISTRATION_VERSION,
    .OperationRegistrationCount  = 2,
    .Altitude                    = { L"<rotating number>" },
    .RegistrationContext         = NULL,
    .OperationRegistration       = ops,
};

OB_OPERATION_REGISTRATION ops[2] = {
    { PsProcessType,
      OB_OPERATION_HANDLE_CREATE | OB_OPERATION_HANDLE_DUPLICATE,
      eac_pre_proc_handle, NULL },
    { PsThreadType,
      OB_OPERATION_HANDLE_CREATE | OB_OPERATION_HANDLE_DUPLICATE,
      eac_pre_thread_handle, NULL },
};
```

The altitude is a float-as-string. EAC picks a spot high enough to be above normal filesystem/AV filters, so EAC sees handle opens before any other callback could handle the IRP. Current builds moved higher than historical to avoid colliding with newer gamer-focused AV products.

### The pre-op callback

```c
OB_PREOP_CALLBACK_STATUS eac_pre_proc_handle(PVOID ctx,
                                              POB_PRE_OPERATION_INFORMATION op)
{
    if (op->KernelHandle)
        return OB_PREOP_SUCCESS;                      // kernel, skip

    HANDLE targetPid = PsGetProcessId((PEPROCESS)op->Object);
    if (!eac_is_protected(targetPid))
        return OB_PREOP_SUCCESS;

    HANDLE opener = PsGetCurrentProcessId();
    if (opener == targetPid)
        return OB_PREOP_SUCCESS;                      // self

    if (eac_is_trusted_opener(opener))
        return OB_PREOP_SUCCESS;

    ACCESS_MASK* mask = (op->Operation == OB_OPERATION_HANDLE_CREATE)
        ? &op->Parameters->CreateHandleInformation.DesiredAccess
        : &op->Parameters->DuplicateHandleInformation.DesiredAccess;

    *mask &= ~(PROCESS_VM_READ | PROCESS_VM_WRITE
             | PROCESS_VM_OPERATION | PROCESS_CREATE_THREAD
             | PROCESS_DUP_HANDLE | PROCESS_SET_INFORMATION
             | PROCESS_SUSPEND_RESUME | PROCESS_TERMINATE);

    eac_telemetry_log(TEL_HANDLE_OPEN_ATTEMPT, opener);
    return OB_PREOP_SUCCESS;
}
```

### Non-obvious things about the strip

**1. It's in-place and total.** `OpenProcess` returns a handle that "works" but is neutered. `NtReadVirtualMemory` on it returns `ACCESS_DENIED`. This used to catch beginner cheat devs in 2018; everyone knows now.

**2. "Trusted opener" is authenticode cert-hash on the opener's image.** Renaming your tool to `EasyAntiCheat.exe` does not make it trusted. A surprising number of people have tried.

**3. The strip is not a block — it's a detection.** EAC logs every attempt with opener pid and command line. Your cheat just got reported even though the handle "succeeded."

### Thread strip mask

```c
THREAD_SET_CONTEXT | THREAD_GET_CONTEXT | THREAD_SUSPEND_RESUME
| THREAD_TERMINATE | THREAD_DIRECT_IMPERSONATION
| THREAD_SET_INFORMATION | THREAD_SET_THREAD_TOKEN
```

Thread handle access matters because some cheats walk thread contexts to find TLS pointers.

---

## Registry callback

`CmRegisterCallbackEx` watches:

```
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat         (self)
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat_EOS
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\CI                     (code integrity)
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\DeviceGuard
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\BootOptions
HKLM\SYSTEM\Setup                                            (safe-mode)
HKLM\BCD00000000                                             (BCD writes)
HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Cryptography                         (MachineGuid)
```

Callback doesn't block — logs and flags. **BCD writes while Fortnite is live flag high severity** (that's where you'd toggle test-signing-mode).

---

## ETW-TI subscription (current-build addition)

Driver hooks into the `Microsoft-Windows-Threat-Intelligence` ETW provider — kernel-asserted suspicious events (process hollowing candidates, APC injection into system processes, etc.). EAC relays into its own telemetry. Needs `PsCreateSystemThread` + a worker consuming the ETW buffer. Exists on current, didn't exist historically.

---

## IOCTL inventory

Symbolic names mine; numbers rotate per version.

| IOCTL | Purpose |
|-|-|
| `SESSION_AUTH` | Handshake (covered above). |
| `READ_MEM` | Kernel-side read of target VA range. `polite` path = `MmCopyVirtualMemory`; `attach` path = `KeStackAttachProcess` + `ProbeForRead` + SEH. Flags select. |
| `WRITE_MEM_PROBE` | Reads one page, hashes, returns hash. Proves to backend that the game's `.text` at offset X still looks like Y. |
| `GET_MODULES` | LDR module list for a pid. |
| `GET_VAD` | **Full VAD tree walk.** The manual-mapped detector's ground truth. |
| `ENUM_HANDLES` | All open handles system-wide filtered to a target pid. Catches pre-callback-race handles + `LIMITED_INFORMATION` escalators. |
| `ENUM_THREADS` | Thread list + start addresses. |
| `PATTERN_SCAN` | Byte-pattern match against a VA range. Expensive, used sparingly. |
| `PROC_INFO` | PEB/TEB fields without touching user memory. |
| `DRIVER_LIST` | Loaded kernel drivers + hashes + signer cert thumbprints + **WDAC block-list match state** (current-build addition). |
| `CALLBACK_DUMP` | Enumerate `PsSet*` callback entries + Ob entries, hash owning driver each. |
| `PHYS_READ` | Physical-memory read via `MmMapIoSpace`. Still in classic current build; gone on EOS. |
| `SELF_CHECK` | Driver signs `(hash || nonce || bootCookie)` with embedded Ed25519 key. Agent forwards to backend. |
| `HEARTBEAT` | Keepalive. |
| `REPORT_FLAG` | Agent → driver: "I found this, add to session report." |

### READ_MEM structs

```c
struct eac_read_in  { HANDLE pid; PVOID va; SIZE_T size; ULONG flags; };
struct eac_read_out { NTSTATUS status; SIZE_T got; UCHAR data[]; };
```

Alignment paranoia matters. EAC's structs are packed in some places and not others. A misaligned field cost me three hours once because the agent and driver had slightly different packing on an older build.

---

## The VAD walker

This is how EAC catches manually-mapped cheats. The VAD (Virtual Address Descriptor) tree is Windows' record of every committed region of virtual memory in a process: every module, heap, `VirtualAlloc`. A manually-mapped DLL doesn't appear in `PEB->Ldr` (loaded outside `LoadLibrary`), but its allocation still shows as a VAD. The agent asks the driver for the VAD tree, compares against LDR, and anything executable in VAD but missing from LDR is suspect.

```c
NTSTATUS eac_walk_vad(PEPROCESS proc, OUT list)
{
    auto root = (PRTL_AVL_TABLE)((PUCHAR)proc + g_vadRootOffset);
    KeAcquireGuardedMutex(&proc->AddressCreationLock);
    for (auto vad = leftmost(root); vad; vad = inorder_next(vad)) {
        entry.base   = vad->StartingVpn << PAGE_SHIFT;
        entry.size   = (vad->EndingVpn - vad->StartingVpn + 1) << PAGE_SHIFT;
        entry.prot   = decode_protection(vad);
        entry.type   = decode_type(vad);               // Private/Mapped/Image
        if (entry.type == VadImageMap) {
            auto fo = get_file_object(vad);
            resolve_path(fo, entry.path);
        }
        append(list, entry);
    }
    KeReleaseGuardedMutex(&proc->AddressCreationLock);
    return STATUS_SUCCESS;
}
```

The `EPROCESS` `VadRoot` offset floats by Windows build. EAC resolves it dynamically — a small routine fingerprints the running kernel build at init and picks the offset from a table. Cheat devs who assumed EAC would be defeated by a new insider build "breaking" the offset learn the table exists.

---

## Scan worker — timer rotation

Runs on a 5-second loop, rotating through scans so no single tick is unboundedly expensive:

| `tick & 7` | Scan |
|-|-|
| 0 | VAD scan all protected processes |
| 1 | Thread enumeration |
| 2 | Handle enumeration |
| 3 | Module list diff vs previous |
| 4 | Self-integrity hash |
| 5 | Driver list scan |
| 6 | Callback-table dump |
| 7 | Cross-process hook heuristics |

Current builds added a second worker on a ~30s cadence doing an **environmental consistency pass**: pulls SMBIOS via WMI, compares to session-init cached values, flags drift. This is a spoof-detector hedge — a spoofer that only patches reads at init time but later system calls read the real values gets caught by drift.

---

## Self-anti-tamper

Driver verifies, periodically:

- Its own `.text` against a baked-in SHA256.
- That its callbacks are still registered (nobody unlinked the `PsSet*` entries).
- That the `ObCallbacks` table still contains its entry.
- That its device object is still present.

**Policy: DO NOT BSOD EVER if we can help it.** BSODing legit users because a weird-but-legal AV loaded in the wrong order would be a PR disaster. On self-hash mismatch the session is flagged at maximum severity. The ban eventually lands. The game does not crash.

---

**Next:** [03 — User-mode launcher](03-user-mode-launcher.md)

```

`docs/03-user-mode-launcher.md`:

```md
# 03 — User-mode launcher (`EasyAntiCheat.exe`, current)

Thin bridge between the Fortnite launcher, the EAC service, the driver, and the game. Its job is to spawn the game with `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` preloaded, open a named pipe for the agent, and forward messages.

Not where you spend most of your reversing time — but you need to understand the launcher because it holds the session-token chain that authenticates the agent to the driver.

---

## argv parsing

- `argv[1]` — session token. Short-lived signed blob issued by `FortniteLauncher.exe`. Contains expected game executable hash, timestamp, nonce. Signed by Epic.
- `argv[2]` — path to the game exe.
- `argv[3]` — the game's own command line (passed through).

---

## Flow

```
wmain:
  1. OpenSCManager + OpenService + StartService("EasyAntiCheat")
     wait for SERVICE_RUNNING
  2. CreateFile("\\.\EasyAntiCheat"), probe with HEARTBEAT
  3. CreateNamedPipe("\\.\pipe\EasyAntiCheat_<guid>", ...)
       message-mode, overlapped, REJECT_REMOTE_CLIENTS
  4. CreateProcess game in CREATE_SUSPENDED
  5. inject EAC_x64.dll via
       VirtualAllocEx + WriteProcessMemory + CreateRemoteThread
       thread start = LoadLibraryW, arg = remote wchar path
  6. ResumeThread on the game's main thread
  7. ConnectNamedPipe, wait for the agent to come up and HELLO
  8. run the pipe server loop until the game exits
```

### Current-build injection hedge

Current builds also carry a secondary injection path using `NtCreateThreadEx` with a slightly different attribute list, as a hedge against `CreateRemoteThread` being filtered by third-party endpoint products. Both paths resolve to the same semantic: load `EAC_x64.dll` in the game process before resume.

---

## Why the injection method is boring on purpose

EAC could use any number of exotic injection techniques and has historically flirted with some. What ships is `LoadLibrary`-via-`CreateRemoteThread` because the trust isn't in the injection method — it's in the **session token handoff**.

The chain:

1. `FortniteLauncher.exe` signs a session token with Epic's private key.
2. The token is passed via argv to `EasyAntiCheat.exe`.
3. `EasyAntiCheat.exe` appends it to the game's command line.
4. The game's main thread starts, agent DLL is injected, agent reads the token from `GetCommandLineW()`.
5. Agent presents the token back to the launcher's pipe server during `HELLO`.
6. Launcher validates the token's signature against Epic's pinned pubkey.

Only an agent-inside-the-real-game can present a valid token because only the real `FortniteLauncher.exe` can mint one, and tokens include a short-lived timestamp so you can't replay.

This is why "inject your own code alongside EAC_x64.dll" doesn't just-work. Your injected code can run, but it can't produce the signed token the agent needs to reach the driver. Without driver IOCTLs, the agent can't do its own scans either — you'd end up with a broken session the backend rejects immediately.

---

## Pipe server loop

Once the pipe is up and the agent has `HELLO`'d:

- Read framed messages from the agent.
- Dispatch by opcode (see [05 — pipe protocol](05-pipe-protocol.md)).
- Forward telemetry messages to the TLS transport worker.
- Forward `RULES_UPDATE` / `REMOTE_MODULE_LOAD` push messages from the backend down to the agent.

The launcher is dumb — it doesn't inspect pipe payloads semantically. It's a demuxer between the agent and the TLS worker, and a watchdog on the agent (if `HEARTBEAT` goes stale, it reports).

---

## The launcher's own self-integrity

The launcher checks its own `.text` on startup (same as the agent and driver do), and verifies that the `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` on disk matches an expected hash before injecting. The hash is baked into the launcher's binary at build time, which is why **replacing `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` with a modified copy causes the launcher to refuse to launch the game**.

The workaround for research — if you want to run a modified agent DLL — is to also patch the launcher's hash-check, or to launch `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping.exe` directly and skip the EAC launcher entirely. See [13 — EAC emulation](13-eac-emulation.md) for notes on the direct-launch path.

---

**Next:** [04 — User-mode agent](04-user-mode-agent.md)

```

`docs/04-user-mode-agent.md`:

```md
# 04 — User-mode agent (`EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll`, current)

Where most of EAC's intelligence lives. If your reversing time budget isn't weighted ≥70% here, you're budgeting wrong.

- The driver is maybe 8-10 KLOC of kernel C.
- The agent, unpacked, is probably 60-90 KLOC of user-mode C++, 30-40% of it inside VMProtect handlers you have to devirtualize before any of it reads meaningfully.

---

## Getting into a readable state

The on-disk DLL is VMProtect-packed with import protection. Workflow:

1. **Dump from memory** after full init inside the game.
2. **Scylla** the dump to rebuild the IAT from resolved pointers.
3. **NoVmp** the VMP regions.
4. **Hand-lift residual handlers** with unicorn-engine + VTIL.
5. **Label subsystems.**

Plan on a week minimum if you haven't done VMProtect devirt before. NoVmp covers 70-85% of handlers across most EAC builds; the rest you fingerprint one at a time.

Full protection-stack details in [08 — protection stack](08-protection-stack.md).

---

## DllMain + init chain

```c
BOOL DllMain(HINSTANCE h, DWORD why, LPVOID rsv)
{
    if (why == DLL_PROCESS_ATTACH) return eac_agent_init();
    if (why == DLL_PROCESS_DETACH) eac_agent_fini();
    return TRUE;
}

BOOL eac_agent_init(void)
{
    parse_cmdline_token_or_bail();
    verify_own_dll_integrity_or_bail();      // first self-check tick
    pipe_connect();
    pipe_hello();
    driver_session_auth();
    rules_load_and_decrypt();
    environment_sweep_once();                 // anti-vm, anti-debug,
                                              // initial module probe,
                                              // SMBIOS fingerprint cache
    spawn_worker_threads();
    install_game_hooks();
    return TRUE;
}
```

---

## Worker threads

| Thread | Cadence | Job |
|-|-|-|
| `scan_worker` | ~2s | Rotates module / window / thread / hook scans. |
| `pipe_worker` | blocking | Read incoming launcher messages, dispatch. |
| `telemetry_worker` | batched | Compress + encrypt + TLS to backend. |
| `integrity_worker` | ~2s (jittered) | Self + game module + system-DLL hashes. |
| `input_worker` | continuous | Mouse/keyboard hooks, distributional stats. |
| `capture_worker` | server-gated | DXGI OutputDuplication + Win32 `Graphics.Capture`. |
| `env_worker` | ~30s | **New.** Environmental drift check (SMBIOS, MAC, disk serial, machine GUID). |

---

## Pipe client

Wraps named-pipe I/O. After `HELLO` the payloads are **ChaCha20-Poly1305** under a session ephemeral key the launcher sends in `WELCOME` (upgraded from plain ChaCha20 in historical builds — current uses the AEAD mode).

See [05 — pipe protocol](05-pipe-protocol.md) for frame format and opcode list.

---

## Driver client

Thin wrapper over `DeviceIoControl` with the XOR quirk from the kernel side:

```c
NTSTATUS drv_call(ULONG code, void* in, ULONG inl, void* out, ULONG outl)
{
    ULONG real = code ^ g_sessionCookie;
    DWORD got = 0;
    if (!DeviceIoControl(g_drv, real, in, inl, out, outl, &got, NULL))
        return GetLastError() | 0xC0000000;
    return 0;
}
```

Per-IOCTL wrappers sit above this. The `g_sessionCookie` XOR is the user-mode side of the kernel's `ioctlXorKey`; they're derived from the same session negotiation at auth time.

---

## Rules engine

Loads `pbrules.bin` at init, decrypts, parses sections. See [06 — rules.bin](06-rules-bin.md) for the file format.

Runtime matching lives behind functions roughly shaped like:

```c
BOOL eac_rules_match_module(const wchar_t* path, const uchar hash[32]);
BOOL eac_rules_match_process(const wchar_t* exe);
BOOL eac_rules_match_window(const wchar_t* className);
BOOL eac_rules_match_driver(const UCHAR hash[32]);
BOOL eac_rules_match_signer(const UCHAR certThumb[32]);  // current
BOOL eac_rules_match_smbios(int kind, const char* value); // current
```

Each dispatches into the appropriate rule section, iterates entries, checks match-mode (hash / path-suffix / pattern / signer / SMBIOS-field), returns the severity tag.

---

## Scanners

The part most writeups are hazy on because there are a lot of them.

### Module scanner

Runs on timer, also fires on every image-load notification piped from the driver.

- Calls `GET_MODULES` for the game process. Walks LDR. Matches each entry against rules.bin module sections. Hash match → high severity. Path-suffix or pattern → medium.
- Calls `GET_VAD`. Builds a set of every executable VAD region. For each, checks whether an LDR entry covers that range. If not and the region is executable, flag.
  - `VadImageMap` without LDR → "unlinked image."
  - `VadPrivate` with executable protection → "shellcode region."
- **Transient-executable detector (current-build):** two VAD snapshots two seconds apart. Any region that was R/W in the first and R/X in the second (flipped while we weren't looking) is flagged — classic "flip executable to run, flip back" cheat pattern.
- **Entropy check:** walk executable VADs, compute Shannon entropy on a 64KB sample. Real x86 code sits around 6.0-6.5 bits/byte. Packed or encrypted code runs 7.5+. Over threshold → flag. Catches unpacking stubs mid-flight.

### Window scanner

The overlay / ESP detector.

- `EnumWindows` callback grabs classname and title for every top-level window.
- Matches against rules.bin window sections. Known cheat UI classes flagged on sight.
- **Transparent-topmost windows over the game's client rect get flagged** even if the class isn't known — the pattern itself is suspicious.
- Legit overlays (Discord, NVIDIA, Steam) have their properties on an internal baked-in whitelist (not in rules.bin — baked into the DLL for freshness).
- **Current-build addition:** windows with no owner but rendering via d3d11/d3d12 directly get flagged. Real applications typically go through a user32 window for input routing; renderless D3D windows are unusual outside overlays.

### Thread scanner

- `NtQuerySystemInformation(SystemProcessInformation)`, filter to own pid, iterate threads.
- Classify start addresses the same way the driver's thread callback does.
- User-mode cross-check in case the driver's callback path got subverted. Belt and suspenders.

### Hook detector

Periodically loads a clean copy of important system DLLs and diffs `.text` pages against in-memory.

Modules checked (current build):

```
ntdll.dll, kernel32.dll, kernelbase.dll, user32.dll, win32u.dll,
d3d11.dll, d3d12.dll, dxgi.dll, gdi32.dll, gdi32full.dll
```

Historical approach was to load the "clean copy" from `\KnownDlls`. Current builds bundle their own known-good baselines (section hashes + raw bytes for the first ~16 KB of `.text` of each module). The change is in response to cheats modifying the `\KnownDlls` section objects before the agent could consult them. Bundled baselines are updated per Windows version via telemetry.

**Export-pointer check (current).** Several key exports (`NtOpenProcess`, `NtReadVirtualMemory`, `LdrLoadDll`, `NtCreateFile`, …) are resolved at agent init and cached. Periodically re-resolved; mismatch flags "export rebound."

### DXGI frame capture

Gated by server-side config.

- Reference `d3d11` device, `OutputDuplication` object on monitor 0.
- Every N seconds, composited frame captured.
- **Current-build also uses `Graphics.Capture` API on win11 as a fallback** — desktop duplication misses some overlay compositions and `Graphics.Capture` sees things it doesn't.
- Frames are JPEG-compressed (or AVIF on current, smaller payload). Shipped via telemetry.
- Backend runs vision models looking for aim circles, bounding boxes, crosshair overlays that the window scanner would never see (overlays drawn directly into the game's swapchain or via DWM composition).

### Input anomaly detector

- Hooks `WH_MOUSE_LL` + RawInput.
- Tracks dX/dY per tick, sub-pixel step distributions, device ID consistency, time-to-shot latencies after a snap.
- Agent doesn't make ban decisions — bins data, ships summaries. Backend ML models score the distributions.
- **Current-build addition:** tracks the kernel-level mouse driver chain (walks `\Driver\MouseClass`'s device stack via a user-mode heuristic plus the driver's helper). Cheats using kmbox-like hardware or custom mouse drivers show an unusual driver chain. Rolls up into the "mouse-driver block list" section of rules.bin.

### Integrity timer

- Self `.text` + each game module's `.text` + **current-build expansion to system DLLs** (`ntdll`, `kernelbase`, `win32u`).
- Expected hashes baked into rules.bin or delivered in session init.
- Mismatch → max severity.
- Interval is short (every 2s) and **jittered** within a window to defeat scheduling by cheats that hook briefly between ticks.

---

## TLS transport + pin check

Statically-linked TLS stack. Current builds use a BoringSSL fork. Trusts a small set of certificate SPKI hashes hardcoded in the DLL.

```c
int eac_tls_verify_cert(cert_chain)
{
    compute SPKI SHA256 of leaf → spkiHash;
    for (int i = 0; i < EAC_PIN_COUNT; i++)
        if (!memcmp(spkiHash, g_pinHashes[i], 32)) return 1;
    return 0;
}
```

**To mitm for research:** find the pin function (string xref "pin" or xref from TLS handshake code), patch `memcmp` → `mov al, 1; ret`. Install your CA, mitmproxy sees cleartext.

---

## Telemetry packet format

Framed binary, protobuf-ish but not. Current builds use zstd compression with a shared dictionary baked into the agent DLL (look for an ~8 KB blob referenced near the telemetry init).

```
header:
  u16 version
  u16 flags
  u32 bodyLen
  u8  msgType
  u8  severity
  u16 reserved

body: zstd-dict(Fields)
  u16 tag
  u16 len
  u8  value[len]
```

### Current-build tag list

| Tag | Content |
|-|-|
| `0x0001` | Session id (16 byte GUID) |
| `0x0002` | Timestamp u64 ms since epoch |
| `0x0010` | Flag id u32 |
| `0x0011` | Severity u8 |
| `0x0012` | Flag-specific data |
| `0x0020` | Module info (path + hash + base + size + authenticode signer thumbprint) |
| `0x0021` | VAD info (base + size + protection + type + backing file path) |
| `0x0022` | Thread info (tid + start_addr + state) |
| `0x0030` | CPUID dump (leaves 1, 7, `0x8000000{1,2,3,4}`, `0x40000000`) |
| `0x0040` | Window info (hwnd + class + title + rect + style flags + `has_d3d_swapchain`) |
| `0x0050` | Driver info (name + hash + size + signer + WDAC blocklist state) |
| `0x0060` | Screenshot bytes (image format tag + compressed payload) |
| `0x0070` | Input stats block |
| `0x0080` | Environmental fingerprint (SMBIOS + disk hash + MAC hashes + GPU LUID + machine GUID + TPM EK hash + Windows product id) |
| `0x0090` | Driver chain for mouse/keyboard (ordered names + hashes) |
| `0x00A0` | Kernel callback dump (addr + owning driver hash) |

### Severity

| Level | Meaning |
|-|-|
| 0 | Info |
| 1 | Warning |
| 2 | Suspicious |
| 3 | Fatal (flagged for ban) |

### Flag-ID groupings

| Prefix | Family |
|-|-|
| `0x10xx` | Module / VAD / shellcode / injection |
| `0x11xx` | Thread family |
| `0x12xx` | Handle / API access |
| `0x13xx` | Window / overlay |
| `0x14xx` | Hook / integrity |
| `0x15xx` | Driver / signing |
| `0x16xx` | Process / parent |
| `0x17xx` | Debugger |
| `0x18xx` | VM / hypervisor |
| `0x19xx` | Registry / BCD |
| `0x1Axx` | Input anomaly |
| `0x1Bxx` | Frame-capture overlay |
| `0x1Cxx` | **Environmental fingerprint (new in current)** |
| `0x1Dxx` | **Server-pushed auxiliary scanner findings (new)** |

### Ban timing

Getting a fatal flag does not mean instant ban. Flag gets logged server-side, correlated across sessions. Current-build correlation cadence is daily (used to be weekly), with hard-correlated flag chains triggering near-realtime bans within hours.

Still: "my cheat worked for a week before I got banned" is a normal story.

---

## Backend endpoints

Current build:

- `api.epicgames.dev` — session reports (both classic and EOS).
- `modules-cdn.eac-prod.on.epicgames.com` — auxiliary scanner module downloads.

Legacy (resolve but 410 for new sessions):

- `service.easyanticheat.net`
- `api.easy.ac`

`docs/05-pipe-protocol.md`:

```md
# 05 — Pipe protocol

Named pipe between `EasyAntiCheat.exe` (server) and the injected `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` (client).

- Path: `\\.\pipe\EasyAntiCheat_<session-guid>`
- Mode: `PIPE_TYPE_MESSAGE | PIPE_READMODE_MESSAGE`
- Flags: `PIPE_REJECT_REMOTE_CLIENTS`
- Overlapped I/O both sides.

---

## Frame format

```
'EACP'       u32  magic
opcode       u16
flags        u16  (bitfield — see below)
payloadLen   u32
crc32        u32  (of payload)
payload      u8[payloadLen]
```

### Flags

| Bit | Meaning |
|-|-|
| `0x1` | `ENCRYPTED` — payload is ChaCha20-Poly1305 under session ephemeral key. |
| `0x2` | `ACK_REQUIRED` — sender expects an ack frame. |
| `0x4` | `FRAGMENT` — payload continues in next frame. |

### Encryption (when `ENCRYPTED` is set)

- Cipher: **ChaCha20-Poly1305** (upgraded from plain ChaCha20 historically).
- Nonce: 12 bytes = `[session_nonce_prefix (8)] + [counter (4)]`. Counter increments per frame.
- Tag: 16-byte Poly1305 tag appended after the ciphertext.
- Key: 32-byte session ephemeral key, delivered by the launcher in `WELCOME`.

---

## Handshake

First few frames are unencrypted so both sides can agree on the session key.

### 1. `HELLO` (agent → launcher)

```
protocolVer   u16
agentBuild    char[32]      // "EAC_2026_MM_DD" style
processId     u32
bootCookie    u64
```

### 2. `WELCOME` (launcher → agent)

```
sessionId        u8[16]       // GUID
ephemeralKey     u8[32]       // ChaCha20-Poly1305 key
serverTime       u64
```

From here on frames can set `ENCRYPTED`.

### 3. `AUTH` (agent → launcher)

```
sessionToken     u8[...]      // the launcher's signed proof-of-launch blob
```

### 4. `AUTH_OK` or `AUTH_FAIL` (launcher → agent)

```
AUTH_OK:
  capsBitmap     u32          // which features the launcher's build supports

AUTH_FAIL:
  reasonCode     u32
```

### 5. `CONFIG` (launcher → agent)

```
scan_intervals {
  module_s, window_s, hook_s, integrity_s, input_s     // all u16
}
enabled_modules          u64       // bitmap of which scanners are on
frame_capture_period_s   u16
env_check_period_s       u16
```

### 6. (optional) `REMOTE_MODULE_LOAD` (launcher → agent)

Carries a signed auxiliary scanner DLL. Agent verifies the Ed25519 signature with an Epic pubkey, manually maps the module, calls its entrypoint with an EAC-provided scan context. Module stays resident for the session.

---

## Opcode table

Numbers rotate per build — descriptions by behavior.

### Agent → launcher

| Opcode | Content |
|-|-|
| `HELLO` | Build id, protocol version, pid. |
| `AUTH` | Session token blob. |
| `HEARTBEAT` | Periodic keepalive. |
| `REPORT_FLAG` | A scan found something. |
| `REPORT_MODULE_LIST` | Periodic LDR snapshot. |
| `REPORT_VAD` | Periodic VAD snapshot. |
| `REPORT_DRIVERS` | Periodic kernel driver list. |
| `REPORT_CALLBACKS` | Competing kernel callbacks. |
| `REPORT_SCREENSHOT` | Compressed captured frame (JPEG or AVIF). |
| `REPORT_INPUT_STATS` | Binned mouse distributions. |
| `REPORT_ENV_DRIFT` | Environment-fingerprint change observed. **(New in current.)** |
| `PIPE_LOG` | Debug log. Usually debug builds only. |
| `BAIL` | Fatal, closing connection. |

### Launcher → agent

| Opcode | Content |
|-|-|
| `WELCOME` | Session id, ephemeral key, server time. |
| `AUTH_OK` / `AUTH_FAIL` | Response to agent `AUTH`. |
| `CONFIG` | Scan intervals + enabled module bitmap. |
| `RULES_UPDATE` | Push a refreshed rules blob. |
| `REQUEST_SCAN` | Immediate scan of category N. |
| `REQUEST_SCREENSHOT` | Capture a frame now. |
| `KICK` | Please close gracefully. |
| `HARD_KILL` | Die now. |
| `REMOTE_MODULE_LOAD` | Load a signed auxiliary scanner DLL from the module CDN. **(New in current.)** |

---

## `REMOTE_MODULE_LOAD` deep dive

The interesting current-build opcode.

### Why

Lets Epic deploy new detections without shipping a new EAC version. A new cheat loader appears in the wild; Epic reverses it, writes a scanner, signs it, pushes it. Next session start, every player's agent pulls the new scanner down, runs it alongside the baked-in ones.

### How

1. Launcher receives (or has cached) a signed module blob from `modules-cdn.eac-prod.on.epicgames.com`.
2. Launcher pushes it to the agent over the pipe via `REMOTE_MODULE_LOAD`.
3. Agent verifies the Ed25519 signature with a baked-in Epic pubkey.
4. Agent **manually maps** the module (not `LoadLibrary` — adding an unsigned-path-looking module to LDR would be hygiene-breakage and self-flag).
5. Agent builds a minimal IAT via `GetProcAddress` internally.
6. Agent calls the module's exported entrypoint with a pointer to an EAC-provided `scan context`.
7. The scan module reports findings back via a callback in the context.
8. Module stays resident for the session, unmapped at teardown.

### How to capture

Two routes:

- **Pipe tap.** Dump pipe frames from inside the game process. When you see a `REMOTE_MODULE_LOAD` frame, the payload is the raw signed DLL bytes.
- **CDN tap.** mitmproxy on `modules-cdn.eac-prod.on.epicgames.com` after patching EAC's pin check per [08 — protection stack](08-protection-stack.md).

Capture every remote module every session. They accumulate — new detections get added, old ones get retired. Build a corpus; each module is a mini-RE project on its own.

---

**Next:** [06 — rules.bin](06-rules-bin.md)

```

`docs/06-rules-bin.md`:

```md
# 06 — `pbrules.bin` (current) / `rules.bin` (historical)

The one high-value asset you can extract in human-readable form. Most of what EAC "knows about" lives here.

Loaded at agent init, decrypted, parsed into a series of sections indexed by rule type (modules by hash, processes by name, driver signer thumbprints, etc.). Every scanner looks at the relevant section when deciding whether to flag.

---

## Header (80 bytes, current)

```c
struct eac_rules_hdr {
    uint32_t magic;          // 'EACR'
    uint32_t version;
    uint8_t  iv[12];         // GCM nonce (was 16 in CBC era)
    uint32_t plainSize;
    uint32_t sectionCount;
    uint8_t  tag[16];        // GCM auth tag
    uint8_t  sig[64];        // Ed25519 signature over header_without_sig || ciphertext
};
```

Ciphertext follows immediately after the header, `plainSize` bytes.

### Crypto changes from historical

| | Historical | Current |
|-|-|-|
| Cipher | AES-128-CBC | AES-256-GCM |
| IV / nonce | 16 bytes | 12 bytes |
| Signature | RSA-PSS (256 bytes) | Ed25519 (64 bytes) |
| Key derivation `info` string | `"EACRULES"` | `"EACRULES2026"` |

---

## Key derivation

```
rootKey = 32 bytes baked into agent DLL at a fixed RVA
key     = HKDF-SHA256(rootKey, salt=iv, info="EACRULES2026", 32)
```

### Locating `rootKey` in the agent

1. Find the string `"pbrules.bin"` (or `"rules.bin"` on older) in the DLL.
2. Xref it — it's used in a `CreateFile` call.
3. The result flows into a function that reads, parses the header, calls into crypto.
4. The crypto function's first argument (via register on current MSVC) is a 32-byte constant.
5. That constant is `rootKey`.

Spot-check: the HKDF-SHA256 inside is recognizable from HMAC inner/outer pad constants (`0x36`, `0x5C`) and SHA256 state constants.

### Locating the Ed25519 pubkey

Distinct from the driver's self-sign key. Search the DLL for a 32-byte constant followed shortly by calls to field-arithmetic (Ed25519 verify is characteristic — multiprecision operations on 255-bit fields). Alternatively, the pubkey is sometimes xref'd from a function named via a localization string like `"rules signature invalid"`.

---

## Section format

After decrypt, a series of sections. Each has a 12-byte header:

```c
struct eac_rules_section_hdr {
    uint32_t type;        // see table below
    uint32_t count;
    uint32_t entrySize;   // count * entrySize = payload size
};
```

Followed by `count * entrySize` bytes of entries.

---

## Section types (current)

| Type | Purpose | Notes |
|-|-|-|
| `0x01` | Modules by SHA256 | Primary module blacklist |
| `0x02` | Modules by path suffix | wide-string suffix match |
| `0x03` | Modules by byte pattern | IDA-style pattern + mask |
| `0x10` | Processes by name | wide-string |
| `0x11` | Processes by SHA256 | image hash |
| `0x20` | Windows by class name | overlay / cheat UI detection |
| `0x21` | Windows by title substring | |
| `0x30` | Drivers by SHA256 | kernel driver blacklist |
| `0x31` | Drivers by name | |
| `0x32` | Drivers by signer cert thumbprint | **New in current** — SHA1 of signer cert |
| `0x40` | Arbitrary string signatures | ASCII / UTF-16 substrings |
| `0x50` | Generic byte patterns with masks | |
| `0x60` | Cert signer whitelist | authenticode signer SHA256 |
| `0x70` | SMBIOS anomaly list | **New** — known-fake vendor/product/serial/UUID |
| `0x71` | Firmware version block list | **New** — bios_vendor / bios_version / bios_release |
| `0x80` | Mouse driver block list | **New** — for aimbot-related mouse drivers |

---

## Entry shapes per type

### `0x01` / `0x11` / `0x30` — by SHA256

```c
struct {
    uint8_t  sha256[32];
    uint32_t severity;
};
```

### `0x02` / `0x10` / `0x20` / `0x21` / `0x31` — by wide-string

```c
struct {
    uint16_t wcharLen;
    wchar_t  needle[wcharLen];   // not null-terminated
    uint32_t severity;
};
```

### `0x03` / `0x50` — by pattern

```c
struct {
    uint32_t len;
    uint8_t  pattern[len];
    uint8_t  mask[len];          // 0xFF = exact, 0x00 = wildcard
    uint32_t severity;
};
```

### `0x32` — driver signer thumbprint

```c
struct {
    uint8_t  sha1[20];
    uint32_t severity;
};
```

### `0x40` — string sigs

```c
struct {
    uint8_t  encoding;           // 0 ascii, 1 utf16
    uint16_t len;
    uint8_t  needle[len];        // ASCII or UTF-16 depending on encoding
    uint32_t severity;
};
```

### `0x60` — cert signer whitelist

```c
struct {
    uint8_t  sha256[32];
};
```

### `0x70` — SMBIOS anomaly

```c
struct {
    uint32_t kind;        // 0=vendor 1=product 2=serial 3=uuid
    uint16_t needleLen;
    uint8_t  needle[needleLen];
    uint32_t severity;
};
```

### `0x71` — firmware version block

```c
struct {
    uint32_t kind;        // 0=bios_vendor 1=bios_version 2=bios_release
    uint16_t needleLen;
    uint8_t  needle[needleLen];
    uint32_t severity;
};
```

### `0x80` — mouse driver block

```c
struct {
    uint8_t  sha256[32];
    uint32_t severity;
};
```

---

## Python-ish decrypt skeleton

```python
def decrypt_rules_bin(path, root_key, epic_pubkey):
    data = open(path, "rb").read()
    hdr  = parse_header(data[:80])      # magic / version / iv / plainSize
                                        # / sectionCount / tag / sig
    assert hdr.magic == b"EACR"

    # 1. verify signature over (header_without_sig || ciphertext)
    body = data[:80 - 64] + data[80:80 + hdr.plainSize]
    if not ed25519_verify(epic_pubkey, body, hdr.sig):
        raise ValueError("rules.bin signature invalid")

    # 2. derive key
    key = hkdf_sha256(root_key, salt=hdr.iv,
                      info=b"EACRULES2026", length=32)

    # 3. AES-256-GCM decrypt
    aead = AESGCM(key)
    pt   = aead.decrypt(hdr.iv, data[80:80 + hdr.plainSize] + hdr.tag,
                        associated_data=None)

    # 4. parse sections
    sections = []
    off = 0
    for _ in range(hdr.sectionCount):
        type_, count, entrySize = struct.unpack(
            "<III", pt[off:off+12])
        off += 12
        entries = pt[off:off + count * entrySize]
        sections.append((type_, count, entrySize, entries))
        off += count * entrySize
    return sections
```

Everything after step 1 is a straightforward byte-shuffle. Step 1 requires `epic_pubkey`, and step 2 requires `root_key`. Both are embedded in the agent DLL, and both rotate across versions.

---

## What you'll find in the plaintext

Reality check for what's actually in the file:

- Several hundred to several thousand module hashes in `0x01`. Most are hashes of known cheat DLLs seen in the wild.
- Dozens of path suffixes in `0x02` — things like common cheat DLL names, Cheat Engine components, known-abused injectors.
- A hundred-plus patterns in `0x03`/`0x50` targeting manual-mapper boilerplate, reflective-loader stubs, common cheat UI framework constants.
- Process name section `0x10` lists debuggers (`x64dbg.exe`, `windbg.exe`, `ida64.exe`), memory tools (`cheatengine.exe`, `reclass.exe`), driver mappers (`kdmapper.exe`, `drvmap.exe`).
- Window class section `0x20` lists cheat UI classes (`DirectX Overlay`, `CheatEngine-MainForm`, `ReClass_MainForm`), plus numeric Cheat Engine dynamic classnames.
- Driver blacklist `0x30`/`0x31`/`0x32` lists known vulnerable BYOVD drivers (`RTCore64.sys`, `GDRV.sys`, `DBUTIL_2_3.sys`, etc.), known cheat kernel drivers, and the signer thumbprints of signing certs known to have been abused.
- SMBIOS anomaly list `0x70` lists common filler strings used by lazy spoofers (`"To be filled by O.E.M."`, `"Default string"`, `"System Manufacturer"`, specific leaked OEM strings known to be reused).
- Mouse driver block list `0x80` includes hashes of drivers used by kmbox-style hardware + known aimbot mouse drivers.

The list grows per `RULES_UPDATE`. Dump periodically and diff versions — seeing what EAC adds tells you what the community is actively using.
