`docs/07-fortnite-integrity.md`:

```md
# 07 — Fortnite's own integrity layer

This is a separate thing people conflate with EAC. The Fortnite shipping exe has anti-cheat logic that runs **independent** of EAC's DLL. Mostly wiring into UE5's anti-cheat module feature system, plus server-authoritative game logic.

Reversing this layer is worth it because:

- Many bans are delivered via Fortnite's own server checks, not EAC's scans.
- Understanding the client-server trust boundary tells you which "cheats" are visible vs invisible to EAC specifically.
- The EAC emulation PoC in [13](13-eac-emulation.md) needs to interact with this layer correctly.

---

## UE5 anti-cheat interface

Unreal has:

```cpp
class IOnlineAntiCheatCommon { ... };
class IAntiCheatModularFeature { ... };
```

Both abstract. EAC implements concretes. The integration lives in a UE module (`OnlineServicesEAC`, `OnlineAntiCheatEAC`, and friends — names vary per UE version). The game registers users (session attendees) via `RegisterUser`. All anti-cheat network traffic between client and server goes through:

```cpp
SendNetworkMessage(FUniqueNetIdRef, const uint8*, int32)
ReceiveNetworkMessage(FUniqueNetIdRef, const uint8*, int32)
```

on this interface. Which means **EAC's reports tunnel through Fortnite's own game netcode to reach Epic**. You can't block EAC telemetry at the network layer without also breaking matchmaking.

Clever design: EAC's reports reach Epic's infrastructure through a channel that already has to work for the game to function.

---

## Export-ordinal map (EAC_x64 ↔ Shipping)

The game calls `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` **by ordinal, not by name**. Ordinals rotate per EAC build.

Rough shape (names will match the EOS SDK public headers; ordinals are per-build):

| Ord | Name |
|-|-|
| 1 | `Client_Init` |
| 2 | `Client_Shutdown` |
| 3 | `Client_RegisterPeer` |
| 4 | `Client_UnregisterPeer` |
| 5 | `Client_ReceiveMessage` |
| 6 | `Client_PollStatus` |
| 7 | `Client_SetCallback` |
| 8 | `Client_GetProtectedData` |
| 9 | `Client_ReportBehavior` |
| 10 | `Client_AddNetworkToken` |
| 11 | `Client_RemoveNetworkToken` |

Plus a bunch more for session events, message signing, trust-state queries.

### Recovering the map

1. Dump `EAC_x64.dll` exports in CFF Explorer. Exports are by ordinal only; you get numbers.
2. Walk the game's IAT for its `EAC_x64.dll` imports.
3. The correspondence is **positional** — the game's Nth import is EAC's Nth export.
4. Name candidates come from UE5's public anti-cheat module source (in the engine) + the EOS SDK sample for anti-cheat integration (public).

---

## Server-authoritative checks (independent of EAC)

Each of these runs server-side. The client sends intent, the server validates reality. No matter how clean your EAC bypass is, these still catch you if your cheat affects observable game state.

### Movement authority

- Client sends movement intent (`UCharacterMovementComponent` input vector).
- Server simulates physics with authoritative inputs.
- Delta between server-sim and client-reported position checked against tolerance.
- Over-threshold → net correction (teleport you back) + counter increment.
- Sustained over-threshold → flag.

Catches: speed hacks, teleports, infinite jumps, no-clip.

Reverse via `ACharacter` / `APlayerCharacter` replication + the net-correction logic (`ServerMove` RPCs).

### Shot-rate validation

- Each weapon has a server-side minimum fire interval.
- Clients firing faster than that → extra shots discarded, counter incremented.
- Pattern of excessive overshoots → flag.

### Weapon spread validation

- Client cannot reduce spread below server config.
- Server computes **actual** projectile direction using its own spread cone.
- No-spread cheats get the client to display a tight shot but the projectile goes where the server decided.

### FOV validation

- FOV changers above the slider max → caught server-side.
- FOV is replicated for sanity; the server applies its own clamp.

### Build-placement validation

- Illegal placements (through walls, overlapping, through terrain) rejected server-side.
- Client's local build may show up for a frame, then disappear on reject.

### Resource delta validation

- Mats and ammo cross-checked.
- Impossible deltas (gaining mats without a harvest event) → flag.

None of this is EAC. This is Epic's own UE code. You find it in the shipping exe's reflection-tree under paths like `/Game/Athena/Items/…`, `/Script/FortniteGame`.

---

## Kick / soft-ban / hard-ban / HWID-ban taxonomy

Fortnite's enforcement tiers, from lightest to heaviest:

| Tier | What happens | Trigger |
|-|-|-|
| **Kick** | Session-only. No account consequence. | Typically client-side by EAC when the detection is immediate and unambiguous (overlay opened, kernel debugger attached). |
| **Soft ban** | Cooldown after match loss (minutes to hours). | Server heuristics. |
| **Shadow ban** | Matchmaking into a queue of flagged accounts. No notification. | Server correlation. **Epic's cleverest mechanism** — took the cheat community a long time to figure out it existed. |
| **Hard ban** | Account termination. | Server correlation across multiple sessions. |
| **HWID ban** | Hard ban plus hardware fingerprint block. | Repeat offender appears with a fresh account on same hardware. |

### Shadow ban mechanics

Specifically interesting because it's invisible. A shadow-banned account:

- Can still queue for matches.
- Only matches with other shadow-banned accounts (or sometimes bots).
- Doesn't earn competitive progress.
- Doesn't realize anything's wrong unless they play with a non-banned friend.

Server-side only. Nothing on the client distinguishes a shadow-matched session from a normal one.

### HWID ban binding

Covered in depth in [10 — cleaner & ban traces](10-cleaner-ban-traces.md), but the short version: the backend stores a hash over some combination of HWID fields (SMBIOS UUID, motherboard serial, disk serial, TPM EK hash, MAC prefix, machine GUID, …). On session start, agent collects, hashes, sends. Backend matches against ban list.

**Cleaning disk artifacts does not undo HWID bans.** HWID bans are hardware-rooted; the mitigation is spoofing (per [11](11-hwid-spoofing.md)) or new hardware.

---

## What's visible to EAC vs Fortnite's own checks

Useful mental model for understanding where a given cheat gets caught:

| Cheat type | Caught by | How |
|-|-|-|
| DLL injected into game | EAC agent | VAD scan, module scan, handle-open log |
| Kernel driver cheat | EAC driver | Driver list, callback dump, BYOVD matching |
| External memory reader (no injection) | EAC + behavior | Handle strip prevents reads; behavior caught by server checks |
| DMA cheat (external hardware) | Server checks only | EAC can't see it; input ML + behavioral correlation |
| Hypervisor-based cheat | EAC anti-VM | CPUID timing, APIC topology, hypervisor brand |
| Macros / recoil scripts | Server + input ML | Distributional analysis of mouse input |
| FOV changers | Server | Server clamps FOV |
| Server-side bot setup (second PC + capture card) | Server input ML only | Input anomaly + outlier win-rate correlation |

The pattern: EAC catches **client-process anomalies**. Fortnite catches **game-state anomalies**. External cheats evade EAC but can't evade game-state checks because the cheat has to affect game state to be useful.

---

**Next:** [08 — Protection stack](08-protection-stack.md)

```

`docs/08-protection-stack.md`:

```md
# 08 — Protection stack (VMProtect, custom VM, anti-debug, anti-VM, anti-dump, IAT)

The part that will slow your reversing down the most. Everything in this doc exists to make the agent harder to read, dump, hook, or lie to.

---

## VMProtect 3.x

Primary obfuscator on the agent DLL and the driver. VMProtect virtualizes selected functions by compiling them to a bytecode and shipping an interpreter that runs the bytecode.

- **Ultra-mutation enabled** — each handler has 2-8 semantically-equivalent realizations, randomly chosen per build.
- **Import protection enabled** — IAT is scrambled, imports resolved through an intermediate stub.
- **Not everything virtualized** — virtualizing all code would make the DLL unreasonably slow. Only sensitive routines get VMP'd:
  - anti-debug checks
  - anti-VM checks
  - the integrity timer
  - the pin check
  - the session auth
  - SMBIOS fingerprint capture (current build)

The rest is plain native code — obfuscated but tractable.

### Devirt workflow

1. **Dump the DLL from memory** after init. [Scylla](https://github.com/NtQuery/Scylla) to rebuild IAT from the resolved pointers.
2. **Identify virtualized regions** by their entry gadget — VMProtect leaves a characteristic prologue (a push followed by a jump into what looks like a dispatcher).
3. **NoVmp** each virtualized function. VTIL IL dumps out.
   - Repo: [`can1357/NoVmp`](https://github.com/can1357/NoVmp)
   - Expect 70-85% coverage. The uncovered handlers are usually ultra-mutation variants NoVmp hasn't seen yet.
4. **Hand-lift residual handlers** — trace symbolically with triton or by hand with unicorn-engine. Identify the semantic (`vAdd`, `vXor`, `vShl`, `vReadMemQ`, `vCall`, etc.), add to your handler table, re-run.
5. **VTIL has a C exporter.** Output is ugly but readable.

### Handler primitives

Standard VMP vocabulary:

```
vPushReg, vPopReg, vAdd, vSub, vXor, vNor, vMul, vDiv, vShl, vShr,
vReadMemB, vReadMemW, vReadMemD, vReadMemQ, vWriteMem*, vJmp, vJcc,
vCall, vRet, vPushImm, vPopFlag, vCpuid, vRdtsc
```

### Ultra-mutation examples

`vAdd` can be any of:

```
a = a + b
a = -(-a - b)
a = (a ^ b) + 2*(a & b)
a = (a | b) + (a & b)
```

Which makes handler-matching by instruction pattern fragile. Match by semantic.

---

## Custom VM layer (on top of VMProtect)

Current builds wrap 2-4 sensitive routines in a second, simpler custom VM:

- environmental fingerprint capture
- pin check
- session-auth signing path

### Spotting it

- No VMProtect entry gadget.
- Has a classic "fetch byte from table, switch on byte, execute handler" loop.
- Opcode table of 30-60 entries.

### Reversing it

Easier than VMProtect — one layer, no mutation.

1. Identify the opcode table (constants referenced by the loop).
2. Label each handler by behavior.
3. Dump the embedded bytecode and read it like any other disassembly.

---

## Control-flow flattening

On top of both layers. Dispatcher-state machine:

```c
int state = INIT;
while (true) {
    switch (state) {
        case  7: /* block A */ state = 13; break;
        case 13: /* block B */ state =  2; break;
        case  2: /* block C */ state = -1; break;
        case -1: return;
    }
}
```

Unflatten with [`dnpushme/DeFlat`](https://github.com/dnpushme/Deflat) or a triton script. Heuristic: symbolic-execute the dispatcher keeping track of which case transitions to which, reconstruct the natural CFG.

---

## Anti-debug (continuous, not just init)

EAC runs all of these continuously throughout the agent's lifetime.

| Check | Mechanism |
|-|-|
| `PEB.BeingDebugged` | Classic. Still checked. |
| `NtGlobalFlag` | Heap debug flags set → debugger |
| Heap flags | Same idea, different place |
| `ProcessDebugPort` | `NtQueryInformationProcess`; nonzero port → debug |
| `ProcessDebugFlags` | Same; zero → debug |
| `ProcessDebugObjectHandle` | Same; nonzero → debug |
| HWBP | `GetThreadContext` → `Dr0..Dr3` nonzero |
| rdtsc timing | Delta between two nop sequences; too large = being single-stepped |
| Kernel debugger | `NtQuerySystemInformation(SystemKernelDebuggerInformation)` |
| `ThreadHideFromDebugger` consistency | Hidden flag set but debug port still showing → inconsistent debug state |
| `ProcessInstrumentationCallback` | **New in current** — zero means nobody has the kernel-callback instrumentation hook |

Each check nudges a cumulative suspicion score. No single check is a ban on its own.

---

## Anti-VM

| Check | Mechanism |
|-|-|
| CPUID 0x1 ECX bit 31 | Hypervisor-present bit |
| CPUID 0x40000000 brand | `VMwareVMware`, `KVMKVMKVM\0\0\0`, `Microsoft Hv`, `XenVMMXenVMM`, `VBoxVBoxVBox`, `QEMU\0\0\0\0\0\0\0\0`, `TCGTCGTCGTCG`, `bhyve bhyve ` |
| Registry probes | VMware Tools, VBox Guest Additions, Hyper-V Integration Services, Parallels Tools |
| Device probes | `\\.\VBoxMiniRdrDN`, `\\.\pipe\VBoxTrayIPC`, `\\.\vmci`, `\\.\prl_pci` |
| MAC prefix | `00:05:69`, `00:0C:29`, `00:50:56` (VMware); `08:00:27` (VBox); `00:03:FF` (Hyper-V); `00:1C:42` (Parallels) |
| Timing | CPUID cost under virtualization vs native. rdtsc delta across a cpuid, compared against cached native baseline. |
| **APIC ID topology** | **New.** Real hardware APIC IDs have expected topology; many hypervisors return 0 or linear sequences. Anomalous topology flags. |
| **CPUID leaf 0x16 frequency** | **New.** Must return nonzero on real modern hardware. Many hypervisors return zeros. |

None fire immediately. Score accumulates. Backend decides.

---

## Anti-dump

| Technique | Effect |
|-|-|
| Erase PE header after init | Zero `MZ`/`PE` magics in the dumped image. Naive dumpers get garbage at the base. |
| Encrypt `.text` back to packed after sensitive execution | "Snapshot of running agent" is lossy. |
| Continuous self-hash via integrity worker | Paused process with modified `.text` → next tick catches it. |
| `PAGE_EXECUTE_WRITECOPY` on specific regions | **New in current** — CoW breaks naive `.text` patches. |

---

## IAT protection via name hashing

Instead of calling `LoadLibraryA` directly, EAC builds its own import table at init:

```c
typedef struct {
    ULONG hash;      // djb2 or fnv1a of "LoadLibraryA"
    PVOID resolved;
} EAC_IMPORT;

EAC_IMPORT g_imports[128];

void eac_resolve_imports(void) {
    for (int i = 0; i < 128; i++) {
        g_imports[i].resolved = eac_find_by_hash(g_imports[i].hash);
    }
}
```

Calls look like:

```c
((LPVOID(WINAPI*)(LPCWSTR))g_imports[LLW_IDX].resolved)(L"user32");
```

### Reversing it

1. **Find the hash function.** Hash function constants:
   - djb2 init: `5381`
   - fnv1a init: `0x811c9dc5`
2. **Precompute hashes for every export** in `ntdll`, `kernel32`, `kernelbase`, `user32`, `d3d11`, `d3d12`, `dxgi`, `winhttp` (new in current — HTTPS transport), `gdi32`, `gdi32full`.
3. **Script IDA** to replace `g_imports[i]` references with the matching API name based on the stored hash.

Once done, the agent becomes dramatically more readable. Most of the "wtf why is this just calling a global pointer" confusion evaporates.

---

**Next:** [09 — EOS variant delta](09-eos-variant.md)

```

`docs/09-eos-variant.md`:

```md
# 09 — EOS variant delta

`EasyAntiCheat_EOS.*` is architecturally similar to classic EAC — if you've reversed classic, reversing EOS is maybe 30% additional work for the deltas. This doc is the diff.

---

## What changed

### Driver

`EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys` is smaller — maybe 55% the on-disk size of classic. Several IOCTLs moved to user mode or were removed entirely.

| IOCTL | Classic | EOS |
|-|-|-|
| `SESSION_AUTH` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `READ_MEM` | ✓ (polite + attach paths) | ✓ (polite only) |
| `WRITE_MEM_PROBE` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `GET_MODULES` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `GET_VAD` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `ENUM_HANDLES` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `ENUM_THREADS` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `PATTERN_SCAN` | ✓ | **moved to user mode** |
| `PROC_INFO` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `DRIVER_LIST` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `CALLBACK_DUMP` | ✓ | **moved to user mode** (via `NtQuerySystemInformation(SystemBigPoolInformation)` + pool-tag pattern matching) |
| `PHYS_READ` | ✓ | **removed** |
| `SELF_CHECK` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `HEARTBEAT` | ✓ | ✓ |
| `REPORT_FLAG` | ✓ | ✓ |

### Why `PHYS_READ` got dropped

WDAC and attestation pressure on newer Windows made `MmMapIoSpace` calls more flaggable (and WHQL submission harder), so Epic decided user-mode checks with VAD walking cover most of the same use cases. The classic driver still has it because enough older EAC-protected games depend on it.

### No persistent service

- Classic: `EasyAntiCheat` is a persistent `SERVICE_DEMAND_START` service.
- EOS: driver loads on demand when a protected game launches, unloads on exit. No persistent service entry.

Means you can't `sc query EasyAntiCheat_EOS` to see if it's "installed." The driver only exists on disk — loading happens as part of game launch.

### Symbolic link + device name

- Classic: `\\DosDevices\\EasyAntiCheat` → `\\Device\\EasyAntiCheat_<random>`
- EOS: `\\DosDevices\\EasyAntiCheatEOS` → `\\Device\\EasyAntiCheatEOS_<random>`

### ObCallbacks altitude

Different number. Picked to not collide with classic EAC (so both can be loaded simultaneously for games that integrate both).

### Pipe name

- Classic: `\\.\pipe\EasyAntiCheat_<session>`
- EOS: `\\.\pipe\EOS_EAC_<session>`

---

## Transport

Through the EOS SDK.

- **Endpoint:** `api.epicgames.dev`
- **Pin set:** pulled from EOS SDK data section at session init, rather than baked into `EasyAntiCheat_EOS_x64.dll` directly.
- **No legacy `easyanticheat.net` endpoints.**

This matters because the pin check code path is different — the EOS variant locates pin hashes at runtime via EOS SDK internals, so static-finding the pin table requires different xref anchors than classic. The pin-patching technique is the same (`memcmp` → `mov al, 1; ret`), but the location differs.

---

## What stayed the same

For completeness, so you know where to re-use your classic reversing:

- All kernel callbacks + semantics (`PsSetCreateProcessNotifyRoutineEx`, thread, image, `ObRegisterCallbacks`, `CmRegisterCallbackEx`).
- Handle-strip mechanism + strip masks.
- VAD walker approach (same `EPROCESS` offsets, same lock acquisition).
- Module / VAD diff logic.
- Rules engine file format (same `pbrules.bin` layout).
- Window scanner / overlay detection.
- Hook detector (with the same bundled baseline approach).
- Integrity timer + jittered interval.

---

## Things specific to EOS SDK integration

### EOS SDK handle propagation

EOS Anti-Cheat Client is exposed through:

```c
EOS_HAntiCheatClient EOS_Platform_GetAntiCheatClientInterface(EOS_HPlatform);
```

Classic EAC has its own C API that doesn't go through this handle pattern. If the game integrates via EOS SDK, the exports exposed by `EasyAntiCheat_EOS_x64.dll` match the public EOS AntiCheat Client interface signatures — the PoC in [13](13-eac-emulation.md) targets this interface.

### Trust level

EOS has an `AntiCheatTrustLevel` concept exposed via:

```c
EOS_EResult EOS_AntiCheatClient_SetReceiveMessageCallback(
    EOS_HAntiCheatClient, const EOS_AntiCheatClient_AddNotifyMessageToPeerOptions*,
    ...);
```

Peers in a session carry a trust level the local EAC assigns (based on its judgments of them) and that server policy can act on. Reversing the trust propagation is part of understanding why "I got shadow-banned for joining a lobby with a cheater" happens — trust is transitive in some configs.

---

## Running EOS variant in a research VM

Same cautions as classic (see [14 — workflow](14-workflow-and-checklist.md)) but with one addition: because the EOS driver loads on-demand only during game launch, you can't pre-stop it with `sc stop EasyAntiCheat_EOS`. If you want to examine the driver at rest, copy the `.sys` from `FortniteGame\Binaries\Win64\EasyAntiCheat_EOS\` before the game ever launches. Once Fortnite launches the EOS variant, it loads the driver; on game exit the driver unloads but the file on disk may have been swapped if EAC's auto-update ran.

`docs/10-cleaner-ban-traces.md`:

```md
# A — Cleaner & ban traces

The surface involved in **"this machine was flagged / banned"** and **"this hardware is the same hardware that was banned"**. Two overlapping concerns:

| | Concern | Lives in |
|-|-|-|
| **(a)** | On-DISK traces correlating "your machine" to "this account's ban" via log files, caches, registry, event logs. | Filesystem + registry + system event logs |
| **(b)** | Live-queried machine identifiers correlating "your hardware" to "banned hardware" via hardware fingerprinting. | Hardware + firmware + OS install state |

**Both matter.** Cleaning only (a) when (b) is the real binding gets you nowhere. Cleaning only (b) when (a) still shows "this machine has run and been reported by EAC N times" also gets you nowhere.

---

## A.1 — On-disk traces EAC and the system leave

### EAC-specific trace locations

```
C:\ProgramData\EasyAntiCheat\                       — persistent config,
                                                      boot cookies, cached
                                                      session metadata
C:\ProgramData\EasyAntiCheat_EOS\                   — same for EOS variant
C:\ProgramData\Epic\EasyAntiCheat\Logs\             — run logs with session IDs
%LOCALAPPDATA%\EasyAntiCheat\                       — per-user caches
%LOCALAPPDATA%\EasyAntiCheat_EOS\
C:\ProgramData\Epic\EpicGamesLauncher\Data\Logs\   — launcher logs, which
                                                     include EAC service
                                                     start/stop events
                                                     and process spawn
                                                     records
C:\Program Files (x86)\EasyAntiCheat\              — system install dir;
                                                     contains the shared
                                                     driver copy + setup
                                                     log
C:\Windows\System32\LogFiles\EasyAntiCheat\        — present on some
                                                     builds; driver-side
                                                     crash dumps + status
```

### Fortnite-specific

```
%LOCALAPPDATA%\FortniteGame\Saved\Logs\             — client-side game logs
                                                      with session IDs that
                                                      Epic's backend can
                                                      correlate
%LOCALAPPDATA%\FortniteGame\Saved\Config\           — user-ID artifacts +
                                                      session continuation
                                                      data
%LOCALAPPDATA%\FortniteGame\Saved\Crashes\          — crash reports (which
                                                      include hardware
                                                      fingerprints)
%LOCALAPPDATA%\FortniteGame\Saved\WebCache*\        — web cache
%LOCALAPPDATA%\EpicGamesLauncher\Saved\
  DeviceIdCache*                                     — device id cache, per-
                                                      user epic device
                                                      fingerprint
```

### Registry locations EAC writes or reads through

```
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat_EOS
HKLM\SOFTWARE\EasyAntiCheat
HKLM\SOFTWARE\WOW6432Node\EasyAntiCheat
HKCU\Software\EasyAntiCheat                         — per-user cache
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat\Enum
                                                     — PnP enum entry
HKLM\SYSTEM\Setup\LastUserShellFolders               — occasionally touched
                                                     during install/update
```

### Windows forensic artifacts that outlive EAC's own cleanup

#### Prefetch

```
C:\Windows\Prefetch\
  EASYANTICHEAT.EXE-*.pf
  EASYANTICHEAT_LAUNCHER.EXE-*.pf
  FORTNITECLIENT-WIN64-SHIPPING_EAC.EXE-*.pf
  FORTNITECLIENT-WIN64-SHIPPING_EAC_EOS.EXE-*.pf
  FORTNITELAUNCHER.EXE-*.pf
```

Records volume paths, DLLs loaded, run count, first/last run time. **Forensic timeline gold.**

#### Event Logs (`.evtx`)

```
C:\Windows\System32\winevt\Logs\System.evtx
  — service start/stop events for EasyAntiCheat
  — driver load events (Microsoft-Windows-Services)
  — Code Integrity events if the driver had any load drama

C:\Windows\System32\winevt\Logs\Application.evtx
  — any crashes from the game or EAC

C:\Windows\System32\winevt\Logs\
  Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-PnP%4Device*.evtx
  — device enum events for the EAC driver device

C:\Windows\System32\winevt\Logs\
  Microsoft-Windows-TaskScheduler%4Operational.evtx
  — if EAC used a scheduled task
```

#### SRUM database

```
C:\Windows\System32\sru\SRUDB.dat
```

Per-executable network bytes transferred and runtime stats. Shows "this machine ran `EasyAntiCheat.exe` and `FortniteClient-*` and transferred N bytes to Epic endpoints." Aggregated hourly.

#### BAM (Background Activity Moderator)

```
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\bam\UserSettings\<SID>
```

Per-SID list of executables run and their last run time. Includes EAC and Fortnite binaries.

#### MUICache

```
HKCU\Software\Classes\Local Settings\Software\Microsoft\Windows\
  Shell\MuiCache
```

Binary full paths and `FriendlyAppName` values for executables that ran under this user.

#### Shimcache (AppCompat)

```
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Session Manager\AppCompatCache
```

List of executable paths + last-modified times observed. Kept across reboots, flushed to registry only at reboot.

#### Amcache.hve

```
C:\Windows\appcompat\Programs\Amcache.hve
```

**The heaviest forensic witness.** Detailed record of every executable run, with SHA1 hashes of the binaries themselves. Amcache shows not just that a binary ran but the specific version.

#### Jump Lists

```
%APPDATA%\Microsoft\Windows\Recent\AutomaticDestinations\
```

Per-application recent-files lists.

#### NTFS USN Journal

```
$Extend\$UsnJrnl:$J
```

Filesystem change journal. Records creation/modification of EAC files, log files, etc. Huge, but chunks can survive long enough to matter.

#### Paging / hibernation

```
C:\pagefile.sys
C:\hiberfil.sys
C:\swapfile.sys
```

Paged-out memory from any process. Can contain literal cleartext of pipe frames, session IDs, HWID tokens that EAC computed.

#### Volume Shadow Copies

```
vssadmin list shadows
```

Point-in-time snapshots. Any snapshot made while EAC was installed contains the full forensic record above.

#### WER (Windows Error Reporting) queue

```
C:\ProgramData\Microsoft\Windows\WER\
```

Cached crash dumps that haven't been uploaded yet. If Fortnite or EAC ever crashed, a minidump may be here with a full memory snapshot of the crashing process.

### Epic launcher caches

```
C:\ProgramData\Epic\EpicGamesLauncher\
%LOCALAPPDATA%\EpicGamesLauncher\
```

Contain your Epic account ID, device ID cache, sessions, auth tokens. **Tied to your account even if you log out.**

---

## A.2 — Live-queried machine identifiers (the HWID / fingerprint set)

These are the fields EAC reads and reports to the backend as your hardware fingerprint. The backend stores a hash of some combination per session. An HWID ban blacklists that hash.

### SMBIOS

Read via one of:

- `NtQuerySystemInformation(SystemFirmwareTableInformation, 'RSMB')` — current EAC path
- `GetSystemFirmwareTable('RSMB', 0, buffer, size)` — user-mode equivalent
- WMI: `Win32_BIOS` / `Win32_BaseBoard` / `Win32_ComputerSystem` / `Win32_SystemEnclosure`

Fields:

| Table | Field | Weight |
|-|-|-|
| 0 (BIOS) | vendor | low |
| 0 | version | low |
| 0 | release date | low |
| 1 (System) | manufacturer | moderate |
| 1 | product name | moderate |
| 1 | version | low |
| 1 | serial number | **high** |
| 1 | **UUID** | **high (strong identifier)** |
| 2 (Baseboard) | manufacturer | moderate |
| 2 | product name | moderate |
| 2 | **serial number** | **high** |
| 3 (Chassis) | manufacturer | low |
| 3 | serial | moderate |

### Disk

```
per-physical-drive serial number via
  IOCTL_STORAGE_QUERY_PROPERTY with StorageDeviceProperty
per-physical-drive model + firmware via same
NVMe identify-controller via IOCTL_STORAGE_PROTOCOL_COMMAND
  (NVMe EUI64 + NGUID)
per-volume serial number via GetVolumeInformation
  (NTFS boot sector field — lower entropy than physical serial
   but still recorded)
```

### Network

```
per-adapter PERMANENT MAC via NDIS IOCTL
  (NOT the "current" MAC — asks for the burned-in one)
per-adapter description + hardware ID from registry under
  HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Enum\PCI\...
per-adapter IPv6 global prefix (less stable, still captured)
```

### CPU

```
CPUID leaf 1 EAX/EBX/ECX/EDX (signature, feature flags)
CPUID brand string (leaves 0x80000002..0x80000004)
CPUID leaf 0x16 processor frequency (or computed TSC rate fallback)
CPUID leaf 4 cache topology
CPUID leaf 0x1F / 0xB extended topology — NUMA + core layout
microcode revision via RDMSR 0x8B (kernel side)
processor serial number via CPUID leaf 3 if available (deprecated on
  modern CPUs but EAC still probes)
```

### GPU

```
adapter LUID + description via DXGI IDXGIAdapter::GetDesc1
PCI subsystem/vendor/device IDs via setupapi
  HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Enum\PCI\VEN_*\DEV_*
nvidia-specific device UUID via NVAPI (if nvidia driver present)
intel-specific device UUID via IGCL / IGDUMD queries
amd-specific via ADLX / AMDVLK queries
```

### Audio / input

```
audio endpoint device IDs via IMMDeviceEnumerator
mouse/keyboard HID paths via SetupDiGetClassDevs
```

### Display

```
monitor EDID via setupapi registry under
  HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Enum\DISPLAY\*\*\Device Parameters\EDID
```

Contains manufacturer ID, product code, serial number, mfg date.

### TPM (newer builds, when present)

```
TPM endorsement key public — hash strongly stable per-TPM
  (survives Windows reinstall; only replaced if TPM is cleared or
   motherboard swapped)
TPM manufacturer ID + version via TBS APIs
```

### Windows / OS identity

```
HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Cryptography\MachineGuid
HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\ProductId
HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\DigitalProductId
  (4 bytes of installation ID, basically unique per install)
HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\SQMClient\MachineId
current user SID from the user token
Windows build number + revision
```

### Firmware tables beyond SMBIOS

```
ACPI tables via NtQuerySystemInformation(SystemFirmwareTableInformation, 'ACPI')
  — ACPI OEM ID + OEM table ID
  — MSDM table on OEM machines (contains OEM Windows product key)
PCI config space for each device via IoGetDeviceProperty
  — driver-side; EAC's driver can enumerate all PCI devices and hash
```

### Boot / firmware state

```
UEFI firmware vendor + version via GetFirmwareEnvironmentVariable
Secure Boot state
HVCI / VBS state
Windows Memory Integrity (code integrity for drivers) state
DMA Guard / Kernel DMA Protection state
```

### Weight summary

Each field is hashed into contributions to the backend fingerprint. Weighting (reconstructed from observed behavior — exact weights are Epic's secret):

- **Heaviest:** motherboard UUID, TPM EK, disk serials, permanent MAC, machine GUID
- **Moderate:** baseboard serial, chassis serial, system product/manufacturer, CPU brand + topology, GPU LUID
- **Lightest:** monitor EDID, audio endpoints, BIOS vendor/version strings

An HWID ban blacklists a combination hash across some subset. The exact combination is Epic's secret. Probability of getting past an HWID ban by changing one field is low; the ban hash is tolerant to single-field drift up to a point.

---

## A.3 — Comprehensive cleaner operation inventory

The full surface you'd need to touch for a comprehensive clean. This is an **inventory**, not an operational guide. Operational steps are left to the operator. Reinstalling Windows is the "nuclear" option and cleaner than any delete script because it regenerates the Windows identity fields along with the forensic artifacts.

| # | Step |
|-|-|
| 1 | Uninstall EAC and Fortnite via Epic launcher (regular path) |
| 2 | Uninstall system-wide EAC via its uninstaller |
| 3 | Delete `ProgramData` and `LocalAppData` directories listed in A.1 |
| 4 | Delete Epic Games Launcher install directory |
| 5 | Delete Fortnite's saved data |
| 6 | Clear registry keys listed in A.1 |
| 7 | Clear prefetch (files, not the directory) |
| 8 | Clear event logs (requires admin; **creates its own event log entry about the clearing**, which is itself forensically relevant) |
| 9 | Clear BAM via the SID subkey |
| 10 | Clear MUICache |
| 11 | Clear Shimcache — requires a reboot after registry wipe because Shimcache is read at boot and flushed at shutdown |
| 12 | Delete `Amcache.hve` — locked at runtime; needs offline boot or scheduled deletion |
| 13 | Wipe NTFS `$UsnJrnl` |
| 14 | Delete `pagefile.sys`, `hiberfil.sys`, `swapfile.sys` (disable paging, reboot, re-enable to regenerate empty) |
| 15 | Delete all VSS snapshots: `vssadmin delete shadows /all /quiet` |
| 16 | Delete WER queued dumps |
| 17 | Delete jump lists |
| 18 | Clear Windows Search index: `net stop wsearch` then delete `C:\ProgramData\Microsoft\Search\Data\Applications\Windows\` |
| 19 | Clear recycle bin |
| 20 | Clear browser caches and history for any browser used to access Epic accounts |
| 21 | **Recognize that none of this touches the HWID binding — see A.4** |

---

## A.4 — Why cleaning alone doesn't beat HWID bans

**The critical point.** EAC's and Epic's backend don't need your on-disk forensic artifacts to re-recognize banned hardware. At next session start:

1. Agent collects the live-queried identifiers in A.2.
2. Hashes them.
3. Sends the hash.
4. Backend compares against the ban list.
5. Match → ban.

### Cleaning helps

- Prevent a **future** investigation from tying a new identifier to the old ban through log correlation on your disk.
- Make a disputed appeal less open-and-shut.
- Reset local caches that EAC uses for drift detection (future sessions start fresh).

### Cleaning does not

- Unban your hardware.
- Prevent the HWID hash on next launch from matching the ban list.
- Affect Epic's server-side records of your ban at all.

### The one operation that does beat an HWID ban on the same hardware

Spoof the inputs that go into the HWID hash. That's [B — HWID spoofing](11-hwid-spoofing.md).

**Next:** [B — HWID spoofing surface](11-hwid-spoofing.md)

`docs/11-hwid-spoofing.md`:

```md
# B — HWID checks, kernel spoofers, the spoof surface

Covers: every HWID source EAC reads (from [A.2](10-cleaner-ban-traces.md#a2--live-queried-machine-identifiers-the-hwid--fingerprint-set)), the mechanism by which each source is actually read on Windows, and how kernel-level spoofers alter each source.

**"Spoofer"** here means a kernel driver (or kernel-only patch set) that intercepts reads of the HWID fields and returns altered values, aiming to make the hashed fingerprint different from the banned hash.

A map of the attack surface. Useful for both the defender (plug the holes) and the attacker (know what needs patching).

---

## B.1 — SMBIOS

### What it is

SMBIOS (System Management BIOS) is a set of structured tables stored in physical memory by the BIOS/UEFI at boot. Tables are indexed by type:

- Type 0: BIOS
- Type 1: System
- Type 2: Baseboard
- Type 3: Chassis
- ...

Each table is a fixed-layout structure with string references.

### How Windows exposes SMBIOS

```
(a) user-mode via WMI:
      Win32_BIOS                → BIOS info (type 0)
      Win32_ComputerSystemProduct → system info (type 1)
      Win32_BaseBoard           → baseboard (type 2)
      Win32_SystemEnclosure     → chassis (type 3)
    WMI provider ultimately IOCTLs into \Driver\WMIxWDM

(b) user-mode via GetSystemFirmwareTable:
      GetSystemFirmwareTable('RSMB', 0, buffer, size)
    Returns raw SMBIOS table blob. What current EAC uses directly.

(c) kernel-mode via NtQuerySystemInformation:
      NtQuerySystemInformation(SystemFirmwareTableInformation,
                               request, requestSize, &returned)
    With request.Action = SystemFirmwareTable_Get and
         request.ProviderSignature = 'RSMB'
    This is EAC's driver path.
```

### How spoofers alter it

A spoofer that wants to change what EAC sees has to patch **all three paths**. Patching only WMI leaves `GetSystemFirmwareTable` showing real values. Patching only user-mode `GetSystemFirmwareTable` leaves the driver's `NtQuerySystemInformation` call returning real values.

**The real work is at the kernel path**, because the user-mode paths ultimately funnel through kernel-side firmware-table retrieval.

Kernel-side work involves intercepting `ExpQuerySystemFirmwareTable` (the undocumented internal function `NtQuerySystemInformation` ends up calling for `'RSMB'`). Two approaches:

1. **Hook `ExpQuerySystemFirmwareTable` directly.** Painful because PatchGuard protects ntoskrnl `.text`.
2. **Patch the SMBIOS table itself in physical memory** — more durable. The physical SMBIOS table lives at a specific physical address recorded in the UEFI configuration tables; map it via `MmMapIoSpace` and overwrite the string fields.

### The caching problem

Once you alter the table in memory, you have to keep it altered. Windows caches the SMBIOS table at boot and some code paths read from cache rather than re-reading physical memory. A proper spoofer alters the cached copy inside ntoskrnl's data section too.

Find the cache by xrefs from `ExpQuerySystemFirmwareTable` in a kernel disassembly of your Windows build. Apply the alteration there. Now every query is consistent.

### The drift-detector problem

EAC's `env_worker` runs environment-consistency checks every 30s. It re-reads SMBIOS and compares against session-init snapshot. If a lazy spoofer only altered the first read's result but didn't patch durably, env_worker catches drift.

A proper spoofer must either:

- **(a)** Patch durably so every read returns the same spoofed values, OR
- **(b)** Also hook the env_worker's read path specifically (requires hooking the user-mode agent — more fragile).

### Which fields to alter

Most-to-least weight (if trying to beat an HWID ban):

| Table | Field | Priority |
|-|-|-|
| 1 | system UUID (16 bytes, strong identifier) | **must** |
| 1 | system serial number (string) | **must** |
| 2 | baseboard serial (string) | **must** |
| 2 | baseboard manufacturer | should |
| 2 | baseboard product name | should |
| 1 | system manufacturer | should |
| 1 | system product name | should |
| 3 | chassis serial | should |
| 0 | BIOS vendor | optional |
| 0 | BIOS version | optional |

### Anomaly-list hazard

Rules.bin section `0x70` (SMBIOS anomaly list) has block entries for values EAC's backend considers "known fake":

- `"To be filled by O.E.M."`
- `"Default string"`
- `"System Manufacturer"`
- Specific leaked public OEM strings known to be reused by spoofers

Section `0x71` (firmware version block list) blocks known fake BIOS version strings.

A spoofer has to generate **plausible-looking fresh values**, not filler.

---

## B.2 — Disk serials, volume serials, SMART

### Physical disk serial

Read via:

```c
DeviceIoControl(hDisk, IOCTL_STORAGE_QUERY_PROPERTY,
                StorageDeviceProperty, ...);
```

Returns `STORAGE_DEVICE_DESCRIPTOR` with `VendorIdOffset`, `ProductIdOffset`, `SerialNumberOffset`. The serial is what EAC cares about most.

The IOCTL is handled by `\Driver\disk` → `\Driver\storport` → the SCSI/NVMe miniport. A spoofer either hooks `disk.sys`'s dispatcher (more PatchGuard-flaggable) or — more durably — registers a filter driver above `disk.sys`. The filter intercepts the IOCTL return path and rewrites `SerialNumber` offset content.

### NVMe-specific identifiers

```c
DeviceIoControl(hDisk, IOCTL_STORAGE_PROTOCOL_COMMAND, ...);
```

NVMe `identify-controller` returns:

- `EUI64` (8 bytes)
- `NGUID` (16 bytes)

Hardware-rooted. Spoof via the same filter approach, different command.

### Per-volume serial (NTFS)

```c
GetVolumeInformation() → volume serial (32-bit)
```

Stored in the NTFS boot sector. Spoofing requires:

- Altering the boot sector directly (can conflict with Windows' boot-sector protection on current builds — Secure Boot + BitLocker detection).
- Or hooking the user-mode API chain (more fragile, reversible).

### SMART data

Not an HWID field strictly, but `GetSMARTData` can reveal power-on hours and reallocated sector counts. Not EAC-relevant; mentioned for completeness.

### Spoofer considerations

1. **Disk serials are read at various times by various code paths.** The spoofer has to filter every path or EAC's driver will see the real serial via its own IOCTL.
2. **Filter driver above `disk.sys` is the clean approach.** Hooking `disk.sys`'s dispatcher directly works but is more PatchGuard-flaggable.
3. **Current-build EAC compares disk serial via both the user-mode IOCTL path and a WMI query.** A spoofer that only hooks one shows drift.

---

## B.3 — MAC addresses

### Current MAC vs permanent MAC

Two different values the system tracks:

| | Current MAC | Permanent MAC |
|-|-|-|
| What | The active MAC on the adapter | The one the NIC hardware was manufactured with |
| Read via | `OID_802_3_CURRENT_ADDRESS` | `OID_802_3_PERMANENT_ADDRESS` |
| Settable via | Registry `NetworkAddress` under adapter's class key, adapter's advanced properties UI | **Cannot** be changed via registry — it's in the NIC's EEPROM |
| Used for | Actual network traffic | — |

**EAC reads the PERMANENT MAC, not the current one.** Registry-level MAC changer tools do nothing against EAC fingerprinting.

### Registry path for current MAC (for reference)

```
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Class\{4d36e972-e325-11ce-bfc1-08002be10318}\<index>
Value name: NetworkAddress
```

### Spoofing permanent MAC

Three approaches:

1. **Filter driver above the specific miniport.** Rewrites responses to `OID_802_3_PERMANENT_ADDRESS`. Cleanest option.
2. **Hook the NDIS IM (intermediate module) layer.** Rewrite responses before they bubble up.
3. **Some NICs have EEPROM update tools** that change the burned-in MAC. Nontrivial, requires the correct firmware tools, varies per NIC vendor.

### MAC prefix hazard

If your fake permanent MAC uses a prefix that doesn't match any registered OUI, EAC flags. The anomaly list in rules.bin (`0x70` is used for MAC prefix checks too in newer builds) blocks known fake prefixes.

A spoofer generates MACs with real-OUI prefixes.

---

## B.4 — CPU identifiers

CPUID is a CPU instruction. The kernel can intercept it via VT-x / AMD-V by causing vmexits on CPUID — but that's a **hypervisor-level** spoofer, not a driver one. On bare metal, CPUID returns what the silicon returns. **You cannot spoof CPUID via a kernel driver on a non-virtualized system.**

### What EAC reads

```
CPUID 1 (signature, feature flags)
CPUID 0x80000002-4 (brand string, 48 chars)
CPUID 4 (cache topology)
CPUID 0xB / 0x1F (topology)
CPUID 0x16 (frequency)
CPUID 3 (processor serial, deprecated — but still probed)
```

### Spoofer options

| Option | Trade-off |
|-|-|
| Run under a hypervisor that intercepts CPUID and returns altered values | EAC detects hypervisors (see [08 — protection stack](08-protection-stack.md)); triggers anti-VM flags unless the hypervisor is stealthy |
| Hook the user-mode agent's CPUID wrapper to return altered values | Fragile; EAC's agent has multiple CPUID call sites, some VMP'd. Miss one → drift. |
| Hook ntoskrnl's CPUID helpers (`KeGetCpuId` or similar) | Same risk; PatchGuard-adjacent |

**Bottom line:** CPUID spoofing on bare metal requires hypervisor-level control. Most "spoofers" do not spoof CPUID — they rely on CPU fingerprint being a low-weight contributor to the ban hash.

---

## B.5 — GPU and DXGI adapter

### GPU LUID

The LUID is a 64-bit identifier assigned by Windows to the GPU. Stable per boot per adapter, regenerates on driver reinstall or on major Windows updates. Not as stable as SMBIOS UUID but still a fingerprint contributor.

**Spoofing:**

- Hook `IDXGIAdapter::GetDesc1` at user-mode inside the agent.
- Or more durably at `dxgi.dll` system-wide. **But** EAC's integrity timer hashes `dxgi.dll`'s `.text`, so inline-patching in memory is caught.
- Need an API-hook approach that doesn't modify `dxgi`'s `.text` — either EAT/IAT hooking (has its own detection surface via EAC's export-pointer check) or `PAGE_EXECUTE_WRITECOPY` CoW tricks.

### Vendor-specific UUIDs

`NVAPI`, `ADLX`, `IGCL` queries. Hooking these in the vendor driver's user-mode component is possible but requires per-vendor work.

### PCI subsystem/vendor/device IDs

Read from `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Enum\PCI\VEN_*\DEV_*`. Spoofable at the registry layer **but** the driver itself still knows the real IDs via PCI config-space reads. EAC's driver can enumerate PCI config space directly via Hal / pciport. So pure-registry spoofing is caught by driver-side cross-check.

---

## B.6 — Monitor EDID

The monitor's EDID (Extended Display Identification Data) blob is 128 or 256 bytes read from the display via DDC/CI over I2C. Windows caches it in the registry:

```
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Enum\DISPLAY\*\*\Device Parameters\EDID
```

Contains manufacturer ID, product code, serial number, mfg date. Weak fingerprint but contributes.

**Spoofing:** Write the altered EDID into the registry key. Also need to intercept any code path that reads EDID directly from the display (bypassing the registry cache). The display driver does this on hotplug — a filter driver on the display stack can intercept.

---

## B.7 — TPM endorsement key

Newer EAC builds (post-2024) query the TPM endorsement key (EK) via TBS (TPM Base Services) APIs: `Tbsi_Get_TCG_Log`, `Tbsi_Create` / `Tbsi_Submit_Command` with a `TPM2_ReadPublic` for the EK handle.

### Why TPM EK is hard to spoof

- The EK is a per-TPM-chip RSA or ECC key provisioned at manufacture.
- Its public portion is stable until the TPM is cleared.
- Hash of the EK public is **one of the hardest fingerprints to spoof on bare metal.**

### Spoofing TPM EK

| Approach | Trade-off |
|-|-|
| Virtualized TPM (fTPM / dTPM emulation in a hypervisor) | EAC detects hypervisors → triggers anti-VM flags |
| Clearing the physical TPM (changes EK) | **One-shot** — destroys your BitLocker keys among other things |
| Hook TBS at user-mode or driver level to return altered EK public | Fragile; EAC's env_worker re-reads |

**Most current spoofers either:**

- Skip TPM entirely and accept the TPM-hash contribution to their ban hash (hoping the backend weighs it with enough slack), or
- Require you to clear the TPM before use.

There is **no general-purpose durable TPM EK spoofer** that is robust against re-read drift.

---

## B.8 — Windows identity fields

### Machine GUID

```
HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Cryptography\MachineGuid
```

A per-install random GUID generated at Windows install. **Spoofable via registry write.**

### ProductId, DigitalProductId, SQMClient MachineId

All registry writes. Spoofable trivially **but** EAC's env_worker re-reads at the 30s cadence. So long as the spoofer altered the registry before session start AND values remain altered (nothing's regenerating them), fine. Windows itself doesn't regenerate these except at reinstall/sysprep.

### User SID

Tied to the local user account. New user account → new SID. Not spoofable at the SID itself, but the fingerprint reads the SID of the running user token. **"Switch user accounts" is the non-spoof workaround.**

---

## B.9 — Full spoofer architecture

A complete HWID spoofer that beats EAC's current fingerprint surface needs all of the following capabilities. Omitting any one of them creates a detectable gap.

### 1. Kernel-mode presence

Achieved through one of:

**(a) A signed driver.** Unusual — signed driver attack surface is small and usually gets revoked once detected.

**(b) BYOVD (Bring Your Own Vulnerable Driver).** Load a known-vulnerable signed driver and use its arbitrary read/write primitive to map and execute the spoofer's unsigned code. Current popular vulnerable drivers rotate as Microsoft revokes signatures via the WDAC block list. **EAC's `DRIVER_LIST` IOCTL now returns WDAC block-list match state**, so vulnerable drivers with revoked signatures are flagged on load.

**(c) Manual mapping** via `kdmapper` or similar, using a BYOVD primitive. The mapped driver has no backing `\SystemRoot` file but still gets a VAD entry and still registers whatever callbacks it registers. EAC's `CALLBACK_DUMP` catches new callbacks and compares the owning-driver hash against its own whitelist. A manual-mapped driver has no on-disk hash match to anything — itself a flag.

### 2. SMBIOS patching

Both physical memory + ntoskrnl cached copy. See B.1.

### 3. `disk.sys` / `storport` filter

For `IOCTL_STORAGE_QUERY_PROPERTY`, `IOCTL_STORAGE_PROTOCOL_COMMAND`. See B.2.

### 4. NDIS filter or miniport filter

For `OID_802_3_PERMANENT_ADDRESS`. See B.3.

### 5. Display stack filter OR EDID registry overwrite + hotplug interception

See B.6.

### 6. Registry writes

For machine GUID, ProductId, DigitalProductId, SQMClient MachineId. See B.8.

### 7. TPM strategy

Either clear the TPM, run under a hypervisor, or accept the TPM-hash contribution. See B.7.

### 8. GPU

DXGI adapter desc hook (best-effort, not durable), vendor driver API hook (per-vendor work). See B.5.

### 9. Anti-detection hardening

- **Don't register competing kernel callbacks** that EAC would see in `CALLBACK_DUMP` (manual-map and use direct syscall tables for hooking rather than callbacks).
- **Don't leave the driver in loaded-driver lists** where EAC's `DRIVER_LIST` would return it. Manual mapping + removing the `PsLoadedModuleList` entry is standard.
- **Don't be visible in the VAD scan for the game process** (non-issue for a kernel driver — game process doesn't contain the driver's memory).
- **Re-patch on 30s cadence** to defeat EAC's env_worker drift detection — but ideally with a durable patch that doesn't need re-patching.

---

## The arms race, summarized

Three detection axes a spoofer has to stay ahead of:

| If the spoofer… | …it gets caught by |
|-|-|
| Patches once at load but doesn't sustain | `env_worker` drift detection |
| Patches durably but registers detectable kernel callbacks | `CALLBACK_DUMP` |
| Does neither but uses a vulnerable driver with a revoked signature | `DRIVER_LIST` + WDAC state |

The operational problem for spoofers is staying ahead of all three. Commercial spoofers rotate BYOVDs on a cadence matching Microsoft's revocation pace; open-source spoofers tend to lag and get caught faster.

---

`docs/12-cheat-detection.md`:

```md
# C — Cheat detection surface

The specific detection surface EAC maintains for things that live in cheats. Categorized by what the cheat does.

---

## C.1 — Kernel driver indicators

What EAC's driver looks at in other drivers:

### Direct identification

| Check | Section in rules.bin |
|-|-|
| Image hash match | `0x30` |
| Name match | `0x31` |
| Signer cert thumbprint match | `0x32` |

### Environment

| Check | Flag |
|-|-|
| WDAC block-list state (current-build `DRIVER_LIST` returns this) | high severity |
| Authenticode signature validity | moderate |
| Image path — non-standard paths (UNC `\\.\`, temp directories, downloaded content) | moderate |
| Load reason — current-build EAC compares load time to known "bulk boot load" window vs ad-hoc late load | moderate |

### Manual-map indicators

- `PsLoadedModuleList` entry missing when image exists in `PsLoadedModuleList`'s backing allocation (unlink patterns)
- Driver image not present on disk at the recorded path
- Image lacks a valid PE header structure (corruption as anti-analysis)

### The rules list is live

Rules.bin driver sections carry a running list of "we have seen this driver used by cheats" entries. The list updates via `RULES_UPDATE` pipe opcodes without an EAC version update.

### Known-bad signed drivers (BYOVD set)

Drivers with valid signatures that contain exploitable primitives (arbitrary read/write, map physical memory, etc.) that cheat loaders abuse. Microsoft's WDAC block list contains the major ones:

- `RTCore64.sys` (MSI Afterburner)
- `GPC-U64.sys`
- `ATSZIO64.sys`
- `GDRV.sys` (Gigabyte)
- `DBUTIL_2_3.sys` (Dell)
- …many more

EAC's `DRIVER_LIST` IOCTL marks each loaded driver with its WDAC-match state. Match → flag high severity, even if the signature is still technically valid.

### Hypervisor-based cheats

A type-2 or stealth type-1 hypervisor that reads game memory from the host and writes results to a channel the cheat process reads. Detected by:

- Anti-VM signals (from [08](08-protection-stack.md))
- APIC ID topology anomalies
- CPUID timing baseline deviation
- CPUID leaf 0x16 (frequency) returning unexpected values
- rdtsc timing of operations that would cause vmexits under a hypervisor (CPUID, MSR reads, specific port I/O)

A well-written stealth hypervisor (SimpleVisor-derived, commercial offerings) can defeat most of these individually. EAC's answer: raise them to "suspicious" rather than "fatal" and let server-side ML correlate over multiple sessions. A single session of "all VM indicators look fine but the timing is 0.5% off baseline" doesn't ban; 300 sessions with the same tiny offset correlated against win-rates does.

---

## C.2 — Injection indicators

What EAC looks at for cheat DLL injection:

| Indicator | Source |
|-|-|
| **New thread in protected process with start address not in any module** | Shellcode — flag hard |
| **New thread with start address not in whitelisted module** | Flag |
| **VAD entry mapped as Image not in `PEB->Ldr`** | Unlinked module — flag |
| **VAD entry with executable + private protection** | Raw code memory — flag |
| **Transient-executable regions** (R/W at snapshot T, R/X at T+2s) | Runtime unpacking — flag |
| **APC queue entries from non-whitelisted senders** | Current-build inspects `ETHREAD->Tcb.ApcState` for suspicious queue contents. APC injection leaves a queued APC entry at the point of injection |
| **Reflective loader stubs** | Characteristic byte patterns (fnvhash lookups, manual IAT fixups) — rules.bin pattern sections catch them |
| **DLL side-loading / proxy DLLs** | Current-build EAC tracks the dependency tree of game modules and flags if a system DLL is resolved from a non-system path (search-order abuse) |
| **Thread hijacking** | `SetThreadContext` from non-whitelisted caller on a protected process thread → thread strip mask + handle open attempt log |
| **Manual-map shellcode in the game process's own allocations** | Pattern matched |

### The common 2026 cheat pattern

Manual-mapping a cheat DLL into the game process via a kernel-mode writer, bypassing the ObCallback strip by not using `OpenProcess` at all. This leaves:

- A VAD entry (type `Image` if the manual-mapper was polite, type `Private` if not)
- No LDR entry
- Thread(s) with start addresses in the mapped region (if the cheat started its own threads), OR a hijacked existing thread (if not)
- IAT entries resolved manually

**The VAD walker catches this on the first scan tick after the map.**

### Cheat counter-measures and how EAC counters the counter

| Cheat approach | EAC response |
|-|-|
| Map as RW initially, flip to RX momentarily to run | Transient executable detector catches the flip |
| Use existing executable VADs (hollowing a legit module) | Integrity timer catches the `.text` hash change |
| Run cheat code from pure data references with ROP | Expensive, slow, limited in what you can actually do — mostly research territory |

---

## C.3 — Hooking patterns

What EAC detects:

| Hook type | Detection |
|-|-|
| **Inline hooks in system DLL `.text`** | Direct byte diff via hook detector against bundled baselines |
| **IAT hooks in game modules** | Periodic export-pointer re-resolve checks that cached pointers match current `GetProcAddress` |
| **EAT hooks** | Same mechanism from the other direction |
| **`KiUserCallbackDispatcher` hijack** | Current-build checks dispatcher's first few bytes against expected prologue |
| **Vectored exception handlers registered by non-whitelisted callers** | `AddVectoredExceptionHandler` leaves a global list the agent walks |
| **HWBP-based hooks** | `Dr0..Dr3` inspection via `GetThreadContext` (same mechanism as anti-debug) |
| **Instrumentation callback hooks** | `ProcessInstrumentationCallback` nonzero without EAC's knowledge → flag |
| **PT (Processor Trace) / LBR abuse** | Rare, but current-build queries MSR `0x1D9` (`DEBUGCTL`) via the driver to detect LBR active unexpectedly |

### Why "hook briefly" has a shelf life

The hook detector runs on a ~2-5 second rotation. So a cheat that hooks for 500ms, does its thing, unhooks for 2 seconds, hooks again has **maybe a 25% chance per cycle of being caught**. Over a match's worth of ticks that's effectively guaranteed.

---

## C.4 — Game-state manipulation indicators

Reading game memory from outside the process (which the handle strip prevents directly, so this moves kernel-side) can be caught via telemetry **if the cheat's visible effects correlate:**

- Impossibly consistent trajectory tracking (ML on movement data)
- Sub-tick reactions (ML on input vs event timing)
- Occluded-target reactions (ML on fire-events against line-of-sight info the server knows)

**Inside the process, memory reads are invisible** — nothing gets flagged just for reading. The flag path is behavioral: once the cheat uses what it read, server-side correlation catches it.

### Trigger-bot pixel sampling

(Reading screen pixels to detect a colored target region, then auto-firing.)

Caught via:

- Input-anomaly detector's time-to-shot-after-input pattern
- Current-build frame-capture ML looking for aim-circle overlays even if the circle isn't rendered to the client (sampling via DXGI capture of a region)
- Pixel-reader code paths read from the swapchain — the agent doesn't instrument this directly, but the input ML correlates

### Macro input

(Hardware or software that generates recoil-compensation mouse patterns.)

Caught by the input anomaly detector's distributional analysis. Real human recoil compensation has characteristic noise; macros don't.

---

## C.5 — External / hardware-assisted cheats

These don't inject into the game process at all. They:

- Read memory via DMA (a PCIe card with an FPGA doing arbitrary PCIe reads without CPU involvement), or
- Operate purely visually via a capture card + second PC setup, or
- Inject input via a hardware device (kmbox, Arduino-based mouse injectors)

EAC's reach here is limited.

### DMA attacks via PCIe cards

Current-build EAC enumerates PCI devices via its driver, reads each device's PCI config space, hashes vendor/device/subsystem IDs and the capabilities list.

- Known FPGA DMA cards (common chipsets are Xilinx 7-series FPGAs) have characteristic config spaces.
- Rules.bin can block specific vendor IDs.
- Counter from the DMA side: spoof the PCI config space. High-end DMA setups do this. Detection becomes probabilistic.

### Kernel DMA Protection / VT-d / IOMMU state

EAC reports whether DMA Protection is enabled.

- Systems with it enabled → DMA attacks neutralized at hardware level.
- Systems without it → vulnerable.
- Cheater running a DMA setup typically has to disable DMA protection. EAC reports this. Combining "no DMA protection" + "generic performance anomaly" + "win rate outlier" correlates.

### kmbox / external mouse injectors

- Input anomaly detector sees the input stream.
- kmbox-style devices try to pass themselves as legit mice at the USB level.
- Mouse driver chain inspection (current-build) tries to identify the chain of driver objects behind the user-input path.
- kmbox typically connects as a standard HID mouse → driver chain looks normal.
- Detection falls back to input stats.

### Capture card + second-PC setups

Frame-grab from first PC into second PC's inference pipeline, aim commands sent back via kmbox.

- Game PC's EAC agent sees only input events coming in.
- Actual cheat logic is on another machine.
- **Input anomaly is the only detection surface here.**
- This is the hardest cheat category to catch.
- Current-build input ML is specifically trained on these patterns, with mixed success.

---

## C.6 — Signature-based patterns in rules.bin

Rules.bin ships thousands of specific byte patterns targeting known cheat frameworks:

- Manual-mapper boilerplate (reflective-loader stubs, IAT fixup loops)
- Common cheat UI framework strings (ImGui class names and strings, if the overlay class got through the window scanner)
- Popular trainer frameworks' signature constants
- Cheat loader PE header anomalies (TLS callback positions, section-name patterns)
- DMA-card driver fingerprints (for DMA setups that install a driver on the game PC side)
- SMBIOS spoofer fingerprints (known-fake vendor strings)

Pattern matching runs per module load and periodically against each VAD. This is one reason the agent's VAD-scan workload is nontrivial — it's running a few hundred patterns against each executable region.

---

## Detection category summary

| Cheat architecture | Primary detection | Secondary |
|-|-|-|
| In-process DLL (classic) | VAD scan, module scan, handle-open log | Thread scanner, hook detector |
| In-process manual-mapped | VAD walker (ground truth) | Transient-exec detector, entropy check |
| In-process hollowing | Integrity timer | Pattern scan on hollowed region |
| Kernel driver (BYOVD) | `DRIVER_LIST` + WDAC state | `CALLBACK_DUMP` |
| Kernel driver (manual-mapped) | `CALLBACK_DUMP` (if registers callbacks) | VAD scan in game can't see it; relies on behavioral correlation |
| Hypervisor cheat | Anti-VM (CPUID timing, APIC, HV brand) | Behavioral correlation |
| External PCIe DMA | PCI config space enum + config hash | Behavioral correlation |
| kmbox / external input | Mouse driver chain inspection | Input anomaly ML |
| Capture-card second-PC | Input anomaly ML only | — |
| Trigger-bot pixel sampler | Input time-to-shot + frame-capture ML | — |
| Macro / recoil script | Input distributional ML | — |

**The pattern:** EAC catches client-process anomalies. Fortnite's server-authoritative checks catch game-state anomalies. Behavioral ML correlates across both. External cheats evade EAC but can't evade game-state checks because the cheat has to affect game state to be useful.

---

**Next:** [D — EAC emulation](13-eac-emulation.md)

```

`docs/13-eac-emulation.md`:

```md
# D — EAC emulation

What emulating EAC means, why you'd do it (offline / private-server / mod-platform use cases), and a working PoC of a stub EAC client that satisfies a game's integration contract without running anti-cheat checks.

**Code lives in [`poc/eac-emu-stub/`](../poc/eac-emu-stub/).** This doc is the explainer.

---

## What "emulating EAC" means

Two possible halves:

| Half | What it is |
|-|-|
| **(1) Client-side stub** | A replacement `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` with the same export table, same ordinals, same function signatures as real EAC. All exported functions return "success" or no-op values. |
| **(2) Backend emulator** | A stand-in for Epic's EAC backend. Talks the EAC backend protocol over EOS SDK, issues valid-looking session signatures using keys the emulator controls, which the game's local EAC client accepts. |

The PoC in this repo is **(1) only**. (2) is private-server territory — the server side signs tokens the client verifies, so you need corresponding pubkeys on the client side. The mechanics mirror (1), inverted.

---

## What (1) alone gets you

**Can:**

- Let a game that integrates EAC start up and run its own code path without EAC blocking it.
- Enable:
  - Offline single-player / sandbox / creative modes that don't require matchmaking.
  - Private-server implementations (OG emulations like the older seasons of Fortnite on private servers).
  - Research / modding work.
  - UE5 integration debugging without touching EAC's live telemetry.

**Cannot:**

- Play against Epic's live Fortnite servers. The backend verifies session signatures from a private key EAC holds, not in the client. A stubbed client gets kicked at matchmaking time.

---

## Stub DLL architecture

- **Match the real `EAC_x64`'s export table ordinal-by-ordinal.** The game imports by ordinal, so ordinal 5 in your stub must be whatever ordinal 5 means in the real EAC (`Client_Init`, whatever). If the game calls your stub expecting `Client_RegisterPeer` but your stub's ordinal N is `Client_PollStatus`, the game crashes or malfunctions.
- **Each stubbed function takes the same parameters as the real one** and returns a value the game will interpret as "fine, move on." For status-returning functions, that's typically `EOS_EResult::EOS_Success` (value 0) or equivalent.
- **Callback-registering functions** (`Client_SetCallback` and family) store the callback pointer and periodically call it with "nothing bad detected" results.

### Ordinal recovery

The actual EAC exports from the EOS SDK's anti-cheat client interface are publicly documented at the EOS developer docs. Classic EAC exports are a superset of those on top of the EOS base — the additional exports cover features like protected network messages, trust-level queries, multi-peer session handling.

To match a target Fortnite build:

1. Open the real `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` in CFF Explorer.
2. Dump the export table — it's by ordinal only.
3. Open the Fortnite shipping exe's IAT for its `EAC_x64.dll` imports.
4. Positional mapping — game's Nth import is EAC's Nth export.
5. Name candidates come from UE5's public anti-cheat module source + EOS SDK public samples.
6. Update your stub's `.def` to match.

---

## The PoC — what's in [`poc/eac-emu-stub/`](../poc/eac-emu-stub/)

- [`eac_emu_stub.c`](../poc/eac-emu-stub/eac_emu_stub.c) — single C file implementing the stub. ~200 lines.
- [`eac_emu_stub.def`](../poc/eac-emu-stub/eac_emu_stub.def) — module definition file assigning ordinals.
- [`build.bat`](../poc/eac-emu-stub/build.bat) — MSVC build script.
- [`README.md`](../poc/eac-emu-stub/README.md) — PoC-specific notes.

### Build

Open a Developer Command Prompt for VS, run:

```bat
cd poc\eac-emu-stub
build.bat
```

Or manually:

```bat
cl /LD /O2 eac_emu_stub.c /Fe:EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll ^
   /link /DEF:eac_emu_stub.def
```

Output: `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` + a `.lib` and `.exp`.

### Use

1. Rename the real `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` in your Fortnite install to `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll.bak`.
2. Drop the stub in its place.
3. Launch via `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping.exe` directly — **not** the `_EAC` wrapper, which runs integrity checks on EAC itself.
4. The game's IAT resolves against your stub, the stub returns success, the game proceeds as if EAC said OK.

---

## Limits of this approach

### Server-side rejection

Epic's backend doesn't accept sessions without a valid EAC backend session signature. A stubbed client gets kicked at matchmaking time. The stub is **ONLY useful for:**

- Offline single-player / sandbox / creative modes that don't require matchmaking
- Private-server implementations where you control both ends
- Research / modding where EAC integration otherwise prevents loading

### Launcher wrapper refuses the stub

`FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping_EAC.exe` loads `EasyAntiCheat.exe` first, which verifies the on-disk hash of `EAC_x64.dll` **before** injection. Your stub has a different hash, the launcher refuses.

**Bypass:** Launch the base `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping.exe` directly, which doesn't do this check. The game's own argv-parsing recognizes it's been launched outside the EAC wrapper and either exits (newer builds outright exit) or silently runs in a degraded mode (older builds).

### Current-build signature check breaks the stub on live Fortnite

Current builds (2026) have added an in-game check: the shipping exe verifies that EAC's init callback has returned not just success but also a matching **Ed25519 signature over session state** that only the real EAC can produce.

A stub's callback return won't carry that signature → current Fortnite will **exit at startup** even when launched directly.

### Workarounds for current-build shipping exe check

The private-server community has handled this a few ways:

- **Patch the shipping exe to skip the EAC init signature check.** Find the verification function via string xrefs like `"anti-cheat init failed"` or similar, replace the compare/branch with a `jmp`. Ships with the exit-at-startup check fully neutralized.
- **Implement real backend emulation with your own signing key.** Replace the pinned Epic pubkey in the shipping exe (or in `EAC_x64`) with yours. Your emulated backend signs sessions with the matching private key. The stub propagates these signatures. Full private-server path.

---

## Full private-server stack

For running a private Fortnite server (e.g. Fortnite OG emulation), the complete picture:

| Component | What it does |
|-|-|
| **(a) Stub `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll`** (or patched shipping exe skipping EAC init signature check) | Makes the client not block on EAC |
| **(b) Private server** | Implements Fortnite's game protocol |
| **(c) Backend emulation** | Stands in for Epic's matchmaking, leaderboards, and critically the EAC backend session-signing |
| **(d) Patched shipping exe** | Accepts your emulated backend's signature (replace pinned pubkey in shipping exe or `EAC_x64` with yours) |

Community-maintained projects doing this openly (Project Reboot and similar efforts) have implemented all four. Detailed implementation is out of scope for this doc — the shape above is the roadmap.

---

## Historical analogies

Nothing novel in the architecture. The specific implementation against current Fortnite is just more work than against older games because current-build EAC verifies signatures the stub can't produce.

- **EmuWarface** — Warface private servers.
- **Various EAC-less private servers** for games that have gone offline.
- **UDK-era Unreal games with EAC stubs** for modding.

Each follows the same pattern: stub client + emulated backend + patched game binary where needed.

---

## What this PoC does NOT do

Being explicit about scope:

- **Does not bypass live-server session signing.** Can't be used to play on Epic's servers.
- **Does not disable EAC's driver.** The kernel driver still loads if the service is running. The stub operates at the DLL layer.
- **Does not provide any cheat capability.** Just makes the game not depend on EAC's integration contract. Cheating still requires separate work and is not in scope here.
- **Does not include the private-server backend.** Just the client-side half.

Purely research / modding / private-server scaffolding.

`docs/14-workflow-and-checklist.md`:

```md
# 14 — Workflow & verification checklist

No magic. Plan on 2-3 weeks of focused work for a specific EAC version.

---

## Reversal plan

### Week 1

**Days 1-2 — File acquisition.**

- Pull everything per [01 on-disk inventory](01-on-disk-inventory.md).
- Hash it all.
- Snapshot copies that you never touch, work on the copies.

**Days 3-4 — Kernel driver.**

- Static pass in IDA.
- Identify all callback registrations.
- Document the IOCTL dispatcher.
- Enumerate every IOCTL's semantics.

This is the foundation — everything else depends on understanding what services the driver provides.

### Week 1-2

**Days 5-9 — User-mode agent.**

- Dump from memory, fix IAT with scylla.
- Devirt VMProtect with NoVmp.
- Hand-fix residual handlers.
- Label the subsystems (pipe client, driver client, rules engine, each scanner, telemetry worker, integrity worker).

This is the longest single chunk.

### Week 2

**Day 10 — rules.bin.**

- Find the root key.
- Implement decrypt.
- Dump the plaintext.

Biggest-payoff-for-smallest-effort item.

**Day 11 — Pipe protocol.**

Short, since you already understand most of it from reversing the agent.

**Days 12-13 — Network layer.**

- Find the pin.
- Patch it.
- MITM a session.
- Decode telemetry packets.

### Week 2-3

**Days 14-16 — Fortnite game-side integrity.**

- Big binary (200+ MB).
- UE5 reflection dumps (unreal-dumper and similar) for type info first.
- Then focused reversing on `IOnlineAntiCheatCommon` and the movement/weapon validation code.

### Week 3

**Day 17 — EOS variant delta pass.** Quick if you did classic EAC first.

**Day 18 — Writeup.** Consolidate IDBs, notes, extracted artifacts into a single document. (This repo is the shape that writeup should take.)

### Time scaling

- Double all time estimates if you've never done kernel RE or VMProtect devirt.
- Triple them, honestly.

---

## Environment setup

### Do not reverse on your gaming machine

EAC telemetry is continuous even when Fortnite isn't running — the service stays up, the driver keeps its callbacks installed. Running IDA alongside Fortnite will not immediately ban you, **but** having the EAC backend observe IDA's process tree on the same hardware ID that plays Fortnite is a data point they can use.

### Dedicated VM, snapshots before every run, second NIC for isolation

- Hyper-V or VMware.
- Fresh Windows install.
- Snapshots before every EAC launch.

### Do not run Fortnite inside the VM

Unless you have a hidden-hypervisor build of your hypervisor — EAC's anti-VM flags will surface and your account's fingerprint will tie to that VM's "hardware."

### Kernel driver work needs test-signing-mode or a DSE bypass

Do that on a machine you don't care about. HVCI will fight you — disable it on your research VM. Secure Boot same.

### Keep two copies of everything

EAC updates overwrite the on-disk files silently if the service runs. One copy you work on, one copy you never touch so you always have a known-good version.

### PatchGuard is accidentally your friend

Modern Windows kernel policy means EAC cannot inline-hook ntoskrnl or do SSDT games — they have to use legal callback APIs. That bounds what the driver can do and what you need to look for.

---

## Tooling

### Disassembler / decompiler

- **IDA Pro 8.x+** with Hex-Rays x64 decompiler — gold standard.
- **Ghidra 11+** — free alternative, weaker on VMProtect.
- **Binary Ninja 4.x** — strong HLIL.

### Unpackers / deobfuscators

- [`can1357/NoVmp`](https://github.com/can1357/NoVmp) + [`vtil-project/VTIL-Core`](https://github.com/vtil-project/VTIL-Core) — VMProtect devirtualizer + IL.
- Scylla (via x64dbg) — IAT reconstruction.
- UnpacMe (online) — convenience.
- Unicorn Engine — emulation helper.

### Debuggers

- **x64dbg** — user-mode.
- **WinDbg (preview)** — kernel.
- **KD** with VirtualKD or network kernel debugging — pair host + guest VM.
- [`wbenny/HyperDbg`](https://github.com/wbenny/HyperDbg) — hypervisor-based, bypasses EAC's anti-debug cleanly.

### Memory & process tools

- Process Hacker 2 / System Informer
- Process Monitor (procmon)
- ETW (Event Tracing for Windows) — WPA
- PE-bear / CFF Explorer — PE inspection
- [`hasherezade/pe-sieve`](https://github.com/hasherezade/pe-sieve)

### Network

- Wireshark + SSLKEYLOGFILE patch (own browser only; EAC pins its own, patch per [08](08-protection-stack.md))
- mitmproxy
- Fiddler

### Kernel / driver

- DriverView (Nirsoft)
- DSEFix / KDMapper — **only on test systems.** Used to load an unsigned inspector driver for scanning EAC's driver. **Do not point these at production game sessions.**

### Note-taking

- Obsidian or plain markdown
- Hex-Rays Lumen for collaborative IDA DBs

---

## Verification checklist

Everything in the docs is a map. The specific values you MUST pull from your own pulled binaries before treating any of this as "my reversed constants":

- [ ] Device name (random suffix)
- [ ] Symbolic link name
- [ ] `ObCallbacks` altitude string
- [ ] IOCTL XOR key
- [ ] Numeric IOCTL codes (after XOR)
- [ ] `ObCallback` strip mask for process AND thread
- [ ] rules.bin — magic, version, salt behavior, AES-GCM IV length, root key, signing public key (Ed25519)
- [ ] TLS pin set — how many pins, each SPKI SHA256
- [ ] Pipe magic (`'EACP'`)
- [ ] Pipe opcode numeric values
- [ ] Telemetry endpoint hostnames for your version
- [ ] Flag ID numeric values
- [ ] `EAC_x64.dll` export ordinals
- [ ] List of modules the integrity timer hashes
- [ ] List of modules the hook detector compares against bundled baselines
- [ ] SMBIOS tables the env_worker captures and compares on drift
- [ ] Current rules.bin content (plaintext)

When every item above has a specific value from YOUR pulled binaries, you have a complete reversed internals document for YOUR version of EAC. This repo plus your constants table is the artifact.

Anything less is approximation — useful approximation, tells you what to look for, where to look, how to interpret what you find — **but not reversing.** Reversing means your constants match the binary's constants byte-for-byte. The architecture above is scaffold. Filling in specific values is the work.

---

## Closing

Treat anything in this repo older than six months as a starting hypothesis, not gospel. EAC is a moving target. Windows is a moving target. The intersection is a constantly-shifting surface.

The three focused sections ([A cleaner](10-cleaner-ban-traces.md), [B spoofer](11-hwid-spoofing.md), [C cheat detection](12-cheat-detection.md)) and the [D EAC emulation PoC](13-eac-emulation.md) are the deliverables most people want. The rest is context for those.
