`docs/15-driver-raw-offsets.md`:

```md
# 15 — EAC driver raw offsets, hash dispatch, and the 46-module manifest

This is the deepest existing public reversal of the current `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys` driver, compiled from a researcher's month-long static pass. Everything here is **raw offsets against ImageBase `0x140000000`** — no decompiler names, just bytes. Where offsets are into `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.exe` (user-mode service), they're file offsets and called out explicitly.

**This chapter is the companion to [02 — kernel driver](02-kernel-driver.md).** Chapter 02 covers *what the driver does* at the semantic level. This chapter covers *where in the binary each thing lives* so you can verify against your own IDA session.

Target build fingerprint: 40 MB driver, 9 sections, entry at end of `.text` (`0x26F738`). If your `.sys` is smaller / entry at start, you have a different build — the *patterns* here will still apply, the numbers may have shifted by a few KB.

---

## Section 15.1 — The binary itself

### Layout

```
.text     0x1000    - 0x26F764   plain MSVC code (~1490 functions w/ unwind)
.rdata    0x270000  - 0x31552C
.data     0x316000  (vsize 0x51740, only 0x2200 raw)
(unnamed) 0x368000  + 0x7B00
.rsrc     0x370000  (version info)
.reloc    0x371000  (0x30DC, 6121 DIR64 entries)
(unnamed) 0x375000  + 0x7E00   <- the "cage" (exec, ~52 KB)
(unnamed) 0x37D000  + 0x2316000 (~35 MB monster section)
.pdata    0x2693000 (0x103E0, 5544 RUNTIME_FUNCTIONs)
```

### Quirks

- **Several raw pointers overlap between sections.** Sloppy on purpose or just sloppy — either way it breaks lazy parsers.
- **Entry point at the END of `.text`** (`0x26F738`). Easy to miss.
- **"Image B" at `0x37D350 – 0x46A616`** — another ~1 MB of plain code with 4054 of its own pdata functions inside the monster section. The rest of the section (`0x46A616` up to `~0x2693000`) is the compressed blob.
- **`.text` internal calls: 7586. Image B internal: 5815. Cross-calls between them: zero.** They only meet through the cage and pointer tables.
- **Import table: exactly ONE import.** `FLTMGR.SYS!FltRegisterFilter` (import dir `0x30CCA0`, IAT `0x30CCD8`). **Never called.** Zero static xrefs. It's only there to force fltmgr to load before `eac.sys`.
- **Debug directory `0x30CBE0`** has entries zeroed, but leftover strings sit right below:
  - `"RSDSd2L"` at `0x30CC18`
  - `"LLD PDB."` at `0x30CC24`
  - **`"Core.pdb"` at `0x30CC30`** — the engine module is called `Core`.
- **~1.1 MB of `.text`** (0x13415F bytes across 60+ islands) has **no `.pdata` coverage**. All hand-written glue: cage entry stubs, trampolines, stack switchers. Islands at:
  ```
  0x23B9BF – 0x24DC00
  0xD0590  – 0xD9E1A
  0x132A31 – 0x1399B1
  0x1472C9 – 0x14D945
  0x222AE  – 0x286ED
  ```

### Entry chain (`0x26F738`)

```asm
0x26F708  mov    rax, qword ptr [rip + 0xa6a79]     ; -> 0x316188
0x26F70F  test   rax, rax
0x26F712  je     0x26f72f
0x26F714  movabs rcx, 0x2b992ddfa232
0x26F71E  cmp    rax, rcx
0x26F721  je     0x26f72f
0x26F723  not    rax
0x26F726  mov    qword ptr [rip + 0xa6a63], rax     ; -> 0x316190
0x26F72D  ret
```

Security-cookie companion check (cells `0x316188` / `0x316190`). Then `0x26F738` calls it, then calls the real main with the original `(DriverObject, RegistryPath)` args. **main is at `0x6595E`**, hand-written prologue, immediately jumps into the cage at `0x3B7FE0`.

---

## Section 15.2 — The obfuscation stack

The file is **not packed**. `.text` is plain MSVC output. The "protection" is obfuscation layered on top.

### 15.2.a — The cage (`0x375000 – 0x37D350`)

Unnamed exec section, ~52 KB of small trampolines, mostly no unwind info. Every trampoline shuffles the VM context (r12-relative slots), reloads state from `[r12+0x190]` / `[r12+0x198]`, jumps where it has to.

**Calling convention is "context struct", not x64 ABI.**

Example chain: `0x3B7FE0` thunks into `0x1DFF28`. Other stubs `jmp` to `0xE9C129`, `0x1365BBC`, `0x206D8DE`, `0xBD7D0A`, …

### Cage prologue — saves everything, sets r12 = ctx frame

```asm
0x37D00F  mov [rsp+8], r15 ... (push r15/r14/r13/r12/rsi/rdi/rbp/rbx)
          movups [rsp+..], xmm6/xmm10/xmm7/...
          pushfq                       ; save rflags
          lea r12, [rsp+8]
          ret
```

Matching restore epilogue at `0x37D134`.

### 15.2.b — The stack switcher (`0x23B9BF`)

Appears exactly once in the whole 40 MB. Runs callback logic on a caller-supplied stack. Call chains vanish from normal unwinders.

```asm
0x23B9BF  90            nop
0x23B9C0  57            push rdi
0x23B9C1  48 8B FC      mov  rdi, rsp
0x23B9C4  4C 8B D1      mov  r10, rcx
0x23B9C7  48 8B E2      mov  rsp, rdx        ; <- private stack
0x23B9CA  49 8B C8      mov  rcx, r8
0x23B9CD  49 8B D1      mov  rdx, r9
0x23B9D0  41 FF D2      call r10
0x23B9D3  48 8B E7      mov  rsp, rdi
0x23B9D6  5F            pop  rdi
0x23B9D7  C3            ret
```

### 15.2.c — The hash mix (the "id" system)

Same **64-bit golden-ratio constant** everywhere, 1071 sites in code:

```
0x9E3779B185EBCA87
```

(Also shows up in its negated form `-0x61C8864E7A143579`. Same bits. splitmix64 finalizer.)

Pattern:

```asm
0x214A7   shl  rdi, 0x1a               ; (arg1 << 26) | arg2  -> packed id
0x214AB   or   rdi, rcx
0x214AE   movabs rax, 0x9E3779B185EBCA87
0x214BB   imul rdx, rax
0x214C2   shr  rbx, 0x20
0x214C9   shr  rcx, 0x3c
0x214CD   shr  rbx, cl                 ; variable shift by top nibble
0x214D0   xor  rbx, rdx
0x214D3   imul rbx, rax
0x214D7   xor  rbx, rdi                ; = final id
```

### 15.2.d — Hash-dispatched API resolving

The **actual "protection" of the call surface.** Every resolved call looks like the resolver at `0x21495`:

```asm
0x214DA   movabs rax, 0x40AA7C5B80C670F3
0x214E4   imul   rax, rbx              ; rbx = id from (c)
0x214E8   mov    rcx, [rip+0x2E64A1]   ; head pointer cell
0x214EF   neg    rax
0x214F2   mov    rcx, [rcx+rax]        ; <- function pointer, negative index
...
0x214F6   movabs rax, 0x6BDA2DE65E67AC64
0x21500   imul   rax, rbx
...
0x21514   movabs rax, 0xE32AB254512044E6
0x2151E   imul   rax, rbx
0x21522   mov    r9,  [rip+0x2E1397]
0x21529   sub    r9, rax
0x2152C   call   r9
```

**Each call site has its own multiplier constant.** Observed constants:

```
0x40AA7C5B80C670F3
0x6BDA2DE65E67AC64
0x3B93F13D663608E5
0xE32AB254512044E6
0xCE85B092C28DC0C2
0x4184EE92079B85D5
0xC351225F85EEC1CB
0x7544DBE290F73374
0xF429BCED6E7FBF5
0x2702763407B864F8
0x8C4A27E4BC905BC9  (x4)
0x16A493048208C5AC
```

Head-pointer cells sit in `.data` / `.rdata` (near `0x302xxx`). Per-site index dictionaries live at `0x30B6C0 – 0x30BE14` (arrays of negative-looking dwords, `FFDB45xx` style).

**Result: static analysis shows no API surface at all. IDA shows nothing.** That's the point.

### 15.2.e — Flattening + selector + kill switches

`0x18C0E6 – 0x18FE5D`, size `0x3D77`, one function. All commands get hashed, the selector picks one of 4 jump-table ranges:

```asm
0x18C146  movzx  ecx, word ptr [r8]       ; cmd word from the request record
0x18C14A  imul   eax, edi, 0x2B79
0x18C150  movzx  eax, ax
0x18C153  xor    eax, 0xAF3E
0x18C158  and    eax, ecx                 ; cmd word AND-masks the selector
0x18C15A  imul   ebp, edi, -0x3C
0x18C160  xor    r13b, 0xE5
0x18C164  cmp    eax, 0x1B3               ; range split 1
0x18C169  jle    ...
0x18C16B  cmp    eax, 0x296               ; range split 2
0x18C176  cmp    eax, 0x31A               ; range split 3
          -> movsxd rax,[rcx+rax*4]; add rax,rcx; jmp rax   (4 tables)
```

### Feature-flag bit per handler — the kill switch

**Every handler checks its own feature-flag bit before running.** Flags byte comes from the request record at offset `+2`:

```asm
0x18C19F  mov  dl, byte ptr [r11+2]      ; flags byte
          mov  eax, r10d; shl eax,5; add eax,r10d; neg eax
          xor  cl, 0x55                   ; shift1 = -(33*k) ^ 0x55
          shr  dl, cl                     ; -> flags >> shift1
          imul ecx, r10d, 0x3C
          xor  cl, 0x1F                   ; mask = (k*0x3C) ^ 0x1F
          and  cl, dl                     ; test = flags & mask
          je   <skip handler>             ; == 0 -> handler is OFF
          cmp  edx, 1                     ; == 1 -> primary path
```

**EAC can switch individual detection routines on/off per session, per game, remotely, by just setting bits in the request.** Bypass one check and you haven't bypassed the system — the backend just rotates a flag.

Other flattened routines with the same pattern:

```
0x10E7E  – 0x124A4  (0x1626)
0x2CFF2  – 0x2E5CB  (0x15D9)
0x4D4AA  – 0x4EAA7  (0x15FD)
0xC43FA  – 0xC5A0D  (0x1613)
0xC77A0  – 0xC8DE8  (0x1648)
0x268C00 – 0x269DD7 (0x11D7)
0x914B2  – 0x92E78  (0x19C6)
0x200F70 – 0x202284 (0x1314)
0x21C0C  – 0x222AE  (0x6A2)
```

Rule of thumb: **anything ≥ ~`0x600` bytes is flattened.**

### 15.2.f — The 33 MB blob

`0x46A616` up to `~0x2693000`. Not encrypted — **Oodle-compressed.**

`.rdata` contains a full plaintext list of Oodle codec and level names at `0x30C9B0 – 0x30CB48`:

```
Kraken Selkie Mermaid Leviathan Hydra LZHLW LZBLW LZNIB LZNA LZB16
VeryFast SuperFast default BitKnit Too High subandf aggressive disable
Optimal1..Optimal5 HyperFast1..HyperFast4
"newlz chunk type %d = %s [%d->%d]"
```

A full Oodle/Oozle decompressor is embedded (that's what a good chunk of the 5544 functions is), and the module store is Oodle-compressed. **"Decryption" = decompression at runtime + whatever layered on top.** Key handling is unverified.

---

## Section 15.3 — The 46-module manifest

Four-char plaintext tags at `0x2CE200` (~`0xB6` bytes), with 16-byte-aligned blobs sitting right before them (one hash per module):

```
ClfC ClfI ClfO Clfs   CM11 CM13 CM16 CM17 CM20 CM25 CM26 CM27 CM28 CM29
CM31 CM32 CMAl CMCa CMSb CMSc   Cont Dcdd Devi EtwB   UdMI Uref UsbC
WDIs Wdog WfpC WfpS   ViMm Nhfs   Ntf0 NtfB NtfF NtfL   Obt b PcSi Uswd
Plcl ScCB SmBf SmMm SmMs IZ
```

### Mapping (confident subset)

| Tag | What it is |
|-|-|
| `NtfL` | Load-image notify callback (the `.sys`/`.dll`/`.ocx` classifier at `0x30BE15`) |
| `NtfB` | Process-create notify (`PsSetCreateProcessNotifyRoutineEx`) |
| `NtfF` | Thread-create notify (`PsSetCreateThreadNotifyRoutine`) |
| `Ntf0` | Base notify infrastructure |
| `Obt` | `ObRegisterCallbacks` — handle protection for the game process |
| `CM11`..`CM32` | `CmRegisterCallback` handlers, **one variant per supported Windows build** |
| `CMAl` / `CMCa` / `CMSb` / `CMSc` | Registry operation families |
| `WfpC` / `WfpS` | Windows Filtering Platform callouts (C = callout layer, S = stream) |
| `ClfC` / `ClfI` / `ClfO` / `Clfs` | WFP classify callbacks (`In`/`Out`/`Stream`) |
| `Wdog` | Watchdog — self-checks |
| `Devi` / `UsbC` | Device tree + USB class — **DMA surface detection lives here** |
| `SmBf` | SMBIOS fingerprint |
| `ViMm` | Memory introspection |
| `Cont` / `Dcdd` | Container/message library |
| `EtwB` | ETW subscription |

**Everything is per-module and feature-flagged (section 15.2.e), so the backend can hot-plug detection modules per game.** The tags + hashes sit there in plaintext so the loader can do exactly that.

---

## Section 15.4 — Driver detection (`0x30BE15`)

### Image-name classification

Extension filters at `0x30BE15`:

```
.ocx (0x30BE15)
.sys (0x30BE1A)
.dll (0x30BE1F)
```

Kernel drivers vs user DLLs vs OCX, sorted by name, case-insensitive helpers at `0x2544B0` family.

### System-image anchors (right behind the extension filters)

```
0x30BE24  \SystemRoot\System32\ntoskrnl.exe
0x30BE46  \SystemRoot\System32\securekernel.exe
0x30BE6C  \SystemRoot\System32\hvix64.exe
0x30BE8C  \SystemRoot\System32\hvax64.exe
```

Referenced by six record walkers:

```
0x1F17BA  – 0x1F1EBB  (0x701)
0x252DC0  – 0x253125
0x254BF0  – 0x254C22
0x2569A0  – 0x256A54
0x25E140  – 0x25E41E
0x268C00  – 0x269DD7  (0x11D7)
```

All validate records with an **obfuscated magic**:

```
(*(u32*)record ^ (id * -0x5163F18C)) == 0xE7AECCAE
```

Version compare at `record+4`, bounds at `+0x110` / `+0x114`. **Records, per-version, walked by six functions, referencing the 4 core system images.** Mechanism verified; whether they're whitelists, blacklists, or integrity records is not fully verified.

### What's NOT present

- **No SSDT patching.**
- **No inline patching of anything.**
- **No IAT overwrites.**

Everything EAC does is **official callback interfaces + their own obfuscation on top.** PatchGuard-era clean.

---

## Section 15.5 — HWID formatter (`0x914B2 – 0x92E78`)

Flattened, `0x19C6` bytes. Walks a descriptor list (type tag per component) and serializes every piece into one text buffer. **All format strings sit in a UTF-16 block, each one individually xref'd back to `0x914B2`:**

```
0x30BF6A  "%016llx-%016llx"                           <- 64-bit id pairs (SMBIOS/disk/GPU)
0x30BF8A  ":lun=%llx"                                 <- SCSI LUN
0x30BF9E  "MBR:%08x"                                  <- MBR disk signature (0x1B8 dword)
0x30BFB0  "%02x%02x:%02x%02x:%02x%02x:%02x%02x:..."   <- 16 bytes, 8 pairs (WWN / SMBIOS UUID / ATA serial)
0x30C040  "%02x:%02x:%02x:%02x:%02x:%02x"             <- MAC
0x30C07C  "Part:%u"                                   <- partition index
0x30C08C  "CD:%u"                                     <- optical drive index
0x30C098  "%u.%u.%u.%u"                               <- 4-field version-ish quad
```

### What the format strings tell you

- **16-byte arrays + LUN + MBR + Part + CD = full storage stack inventory**
- **MACs = all NICs**
- **`%016llx` pairs = the 64-bit identifier crowd** (SMBIOS, disk, GPU class)

### CPU side — `0x128BF – 0x12A67` (`0x1A8` bytes)

CPUID leaf sweep wrapped in `rdtsc`:

```c
rdtsc();
cpuid(0); cpuid(1); cpuid(2); cpuid(3); cpuid(4); cpuid(5);
cpuid(6); cpuid(7); cpuid(9); cpuid(0xA);
...
rdtsc();
```

**Leaf 3 is the processor serial number leaf.** Deprecated for 20 years. They still probe it.

### Feature probing / capability bitmap

- `0x265310 – 0x2653C5`
- `0x265402 – 0x26554E`
- `0x26554E – 0x2655E2` (cpuid + XGETBV + extended feature walk)
- Cached to `.data` cell `0x316240`
- Also gates the SSE fast path of the memset at `0x26F500`

### Critical: SMBIOS **HISTORY**

This is the one sentence that breaks naïve spoofers:

> SMBIOS table / system entries **INCL. HISTORY** — so a good spoofer needs a clean state **and patches making current + past entries identical**. Otherwise you flag.

The driver remembers the SMBIOS values it has seen across sessions. A spoofer that changes values every run creates a "SMBIOS keeps changing" anomaly that gets flagged even when the current session's values look fine.

---

## Section 15.6 — Trust-score plumbing

The score itself is server-side. The driver **feeds** it.

### Certificate trust state (driver side)

Driver walks the actual Windows trust stores + Wintrust policy plumbing. All in plaintext UTF-16:

```
DriverCleanupPolicy      0x30BEF0
DriverFinalPolicy        0x30BF18
DriverInitializePolicy   0x30BF3C
WintrustCertificateTrust 0x30C0B0
SoftpubCheckCert         0x30C0E2
FlightRoot               0x30C104
TrustedAppRoot           0x30C11A
AuthRoot                 0x30C138
SoftpubCleanup           0x30C14A
Initialization / CertCheck / Certificate / Blob / _64_
SoftpubInitialize        0x30C19A
SoftpubLoadSignature     0x30C1D6
SoftpubLoadMessage       0x30C200
SoftpubAuthenticode      0x30C226
```

Store path template:

```
"%s\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\SystemCertificates\%s\Certificates\"  (0x30C262)
ROOT                                                          (0x30C30E)
```

Trust-chain GUIDs:

```
F750E6C3-38EE-11D1-85E5-00C04FC295EE  (0x30C37E — generic cert verify)
00AAC56B-CD44-11D0-8CC2-00C04FC295EE  (0x30C3C8 — generic verify V2)
```

Store list (`ROOT` / `AuthRoot` / `TrustedAppRoot` / `FlightRoot`) referenced by `0xF965C – 0xF98E4` (`0x288`).
Store path builder at `0xF9BB2 – 0xF9C21`.

**Combined with the record walkers from 15.4, the kernel side can judge the trust state of everything loaded on the machine, not just EAC itself.** Your re-signed vulnerable driver from 2016 is a data point in this system.

### TPM attestation (service-side, file offsets)

The service (`EasyAntiCheat_EOS.exe`) owns this one:

```
SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\Tpm\PlatformQuoteKeys    (exe 0xB0A08, 0xB0C80)
EacQuoteKey                                                 (exe 0xB0A78, 0xB0CF0)
AttestationKey                                              (exe 0xB0F72)
SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat_EOS\Attestation  (exe 0xB0C00, 0xB0EF2)

Tbsi_Context_Create       (exe 0xCC83C)
Tbsi_GetDeviceInfo        (exe 0xCC852)
Tbsip_Cancel_Commands     (exe 0xCC868)
Tbsip_Context_Close       (exe 0xCC880)
                          (also 0xB0BA2)
TpmRetrieveEkCertificate  (exe 0xB0BE6)

rtbs.dll                  (exe 0xB0B90)
TpmCoreProvisioning.dll   (exe 0xB0BB6)
```

Windows per-service platform quote key store (`PlatformQuoteKeys` + `EacQuoteKey`), TBS device info + EK cert retrieval. **Quotes (AIK-signed PCR state) can get bound to the machine identity and pushed up. The HWID isn't just a text string anymore.**

### Per-handler feature flags

Covered in 15.2.e. **This IS the trust system in practice** — the backend doesn't have to ban you; it can just turn your trust level into which checks you get, and you never know which ones ran.

---

## Section 15.7 — What rising scan activity means

Direct quote from the researcher:

> If your trust drops low enough because your HWID spoofer is bad / Driver / Cheat / Domain / IP is detected, you can notice higher scan activity. Basically: if you see high scan activity plus more/fuller HWID collection, your software might be flagged and they're just mapping out the userbase for a ban wave. Watch out for rising `IntegrityViolation` / `GameTrust` activity in your network logs. Once that happens, give it some weeks and you have a detection. From what I saw, this period takes weeks to months, depending on userbase — more users = faster — but never under 1 week for new drivers. **If fully detected, after the collection phase, the ban is instant.**

Operational signal: **monitor outbound telemetry volume + event-name distribution.** A session that suddenly ships 10× the usual telemetry with `IntegrityViolation` / `GameTrust` prominence is the pre-wave signal.

---

## Section 15.8 — Hook list (the "what" of each module tag)

From the manifest in 15.3:

```
PsSetCreateProcessNotifyRoutineEx       <- NtfB
PsSetCreateThreadNotifyRoutine          <- NtfF
PsSetLoadImageNotifyRoutine             <- NtfL  (.sys/.dll/.ocx classifier @ 0x30BE15)
ObRegisterCallbacks                     <- Obt   (handle protection)
CmRegisterCallback(Ex)                  <- CM11..CM32 + CMAl/Ca/Sb/Sc
WFP callouts + classify in/out/stream   <- WfpC, WfpS, ClfC, ClfI, ClfO, Clfs
device/PnP + USB class                  <- Devi, UsbC
ETW                                     <- EtwB
watchdog                                <- Wdog
memory introspection                    <- ViMm
SMBIOS fingerprint                      <- SmBf
container/message lib                   <- Cont, Dcdd
```

## Open questions (from the source researcher)

Marked "couldn't verify":

- How the 33 MB blob gets decrypted/decompressed at runtime and where the keys come from (probably from Epic servers).
- Exact meaning of half the module tags (above are the confident ones).
- What "Theia" is (it's an error event name in the service — see [16 — telemetry](16-telemetry-endpoints.md)).
- Whether the `CM11..CM32` tags really mean per-OS-build registry callback variants (likely but not proven).
- Whether the record walkers at `0x1F17BA` etc. are whitelists, blacklists, or integrity records — the record magic is verified; the semantics are not.

```md
# 16 — Telemetry endpoints, event catalog, and the MITM method

The exact outbound surface from `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.exe` to Epic's backend, with file offsets, headers, event names, and a working MITM approach.

---

## Service → Epic transport

The service reports to Epic over **plain HTTPS POST**. Stack: libcurl + schannel + Negotiate strings are present. Proxy resolution via `WinHttpOpen` / `WinHttpGetIEProxyConfigForCurrentUser` / `WinHttpGetProxyForUrl` (file offsets `0xBB105 – 0xBB208`).

### Primary endpoint

```
POST https://api.epicgames.dev/telemetry/data/datarouter/api/v1/public/data
  ?AppVersion=1.0.0
  &AppEnvironment=Production
  &UploadType=sdkevents
  &AppID=EOSSDK.AntiCheatExt.Service
  &SessionId=<...>
```

Stored at file offset `0xAFC2C`.

### Secondary / legacy endpoint

```
POST https://datarouter.ol.epicgames.com/datarouter/api/v1/public/data
  ?AppVersion=1.0.0
  &AppEnvironment=Production
  &UploadType=eteventstream
  &AppID=easyanticheat
  &SessionId=<...>
```

Stored at file offset `0xAFCE0`.

### Headers

| Header | Value | File offset |
|-|-|-|
| `Content-Type` | `application/json` | `0xAFDEE` / `0xAFDFF` |
| `User-Agent` | `Easy Anti-Cheat EOS/1.0.0` | `0xAFE0C` / `0xAFE26` |
| `Service` | `1.5.9` | `0xAFD85` |

---

## Body format

Not a generic JSON serializer. Built by concatenating fixed string fragments in a specific order (key order included):

```
{"Events":[              (0xAFBA8)
{"EventName":"           (0xAFBB4)
","Source":"             (0xAFBC3)
","BuildTime":"          (0xAFBCE)
","ProductId":"          (0xAFBDC)
","SandboxId":"          (0xAFBEA)
","DeploymentId":"       (0xAFBF8)
","OSName":"             (0xAFC09)
","OSVer":"              (0xAFC14)
","BuildId":             (0xAFC1E)    <- numeric, no closing quote
```

### Field sources

- `ProductId` = the EOS `ProductId`.
- `OSName` / `OSVer` / `BuildId` = Windows `CurrentVersion` registry keys (lookup near `0xB0A08`).
- `SandboxId` / `DeploymentId` = per-title EOS identifiers.

---

## The event catalog (`0xAFE31 – 0xB00FE`)

Every event name the service may emit. **Reading this catalog is the fastest way to understand what EAC actually flags.**

```
AntiCheat.Generic.TestValue

AntiCheat.Error.Bugcheck
AntiCheat.Error.BugcheckDetailed
AntiCheat.Error.GameTrust                    <- the one that correlates to bans
AntiCheat.Error.GameApplicationError
AntiCheat.Error.IntegrityViolation           <- the other one that correlates
AntiCheat.Error.GameHardError
AntiCheat.Error.Launch
AntiCheat.Error.ValidateError
AntiCheat.Error.ServiceUpdateFailed
AntiCheat.Error.GameException
AntiCheat.Error.NetworkProtocol
AntiCheat.Error.Theia                        <- unknown subsystem
AntiCheat.Error.Undefined

AntiCheat.Warning.ChunkFailure
AntiCheat.Warning.GameTerminationAbnormal
AntiCheat.Warning.AbnormalShutdown
AntiCheat.Warning.BadOSVersion

AntiCheat.Info.DriverInitResult
AntiCheat.Info.DriverInitTime
AntiCheat.Info.DriverUnloadDuration
AntiCheat.Info.SandboxInfo
AntiCheat.Info.SandboxEventInfoFile
AntiCheat.Info.SandboxProcess
```

### Field groupings

- **`GameTrust`** / **`IntegrityViolation`** / **`ValidateError`** → the enforcement-correlated events. These are the ones you want to see *absent* from your traffic.
- **`Theia`** → unknown subsystem. The researcher who dumped this couldn't confirm what Theia is.
- **`DriverInitResult`** / **`DriverInitTime`** / **`DriverUnloadDuration`** → routine telemetry, baseline-expected.
- **`BadOSVersion`** / **`ChunkFailure`** → environment anomalies.
- **`SandboxInfo`** / **`SandboxProcess`** → per-session environment facts.

---

## Named kernel objects + service-registry state

For logging without touching the kernel, EAC leaves behind:

### Named events

```
Global\EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventDriver    (exe 0xBC762)
Global\EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventGame      (exe 0xBD236)
Global\EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventModule    (exe 0xBD27A)
```

These are standard Windows named events (`CreateEventW` / `OpenEventW`). Subscribing to them reveals the driver / game / module heartbeat without any hooking.

### Registry state keys

Under `SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat_EOS\`:

```
Attestation                           (exe 0xB0C00, 0xB0EF2)
ProductsInstalled                     (exe 0xB0E50)
ErrRpt_GameCrashExitStatus            (exe 0xBC694)
ErrRpt_LastUnloadDuration             (exe 0xBC858)
ErrRpt_GameState                      (exe 0xBD104)
ErrRpt_ServiceState                   (exe 0xBD126)
ErrRpt_GameCrashExitTime              (exe 0xBD15A)
ErrRpt_LastInitTime                   (exe 0xBD18C)
ErrRpt_GameCrashCreateTime            (exe 0xBD1B4)
ErrRpt_LastInitSandboxId              (exe 0xBD300)
ErrRpt_LastInitDeploymentId           (exe 0xBD332)
```

These are accessible with any registry viewer. They reflect the service's last session state and error flags. **Useful for ban-trace cleanup (`ErrRpt_*` values survive game-level uninstall).**

---

## Driver ↔ service frame protocol

Lives at the very beginning of `.text` (`0x1000 – 0x1200`) and in a couple of RAW gap functions.

### Byte-order split

Envelopes are **little-endian dwords**. Payload values inside are **big-endian**. Watch this.

### Container init (`0x1020 – 0x1099`)

- Magic dwords: `{1, 2, 1, 0x6C}` — built on the stack at runtime; **you won't find it raw in the file**.
- Min size: `0x50`.
- Data starts at `ctx+0x50`.
- Cap = `size - 0x50`.
- State byte at `+0x40`.
- Flags at `+0x44` (low nibble = op class).
- List head at `+0x48`.

### Push wrapper: `0x1150 – 0x11FA`

### Big-endian value accessors

```
0x53A0  read u8
0x53F0  read u16 BE (rol ax, 8)
0x54A0  write u32 BE (bswap)
0x54F0  read u32 BE (bswap)
```

### Envelope writer (RAW gap, no unwind): `0x24F360 – 0x24F3AF`

```
writes:  [dst+0x00] = type       (1..5, or 0)
         [dst+0x04] = class      (>= 2, or == 2 when type == 0)
         [dst+0x08] = 0x378      (fixed marker, 888)
error:   0x80100004 on invalid args
success: returns 0x100000
```

### Error space

```
Container:    0x80004, 0x80005, 0x80007, 0x80012, 0x80016
Marshalling:  0x90005 (null), 0x90006 (truncation)
```

### Observed pushed message size

`0x17A` bytes.

---

## MITM method — the Kaspersky trick

The service does **cert pinning**. Standard mitmproxy gets ignored.

**But** — Kaspersky's "SSL protection" package installs its own CA as *trusted on the machine*. You can:

1. Install Kaspersky with SSL protection enabled (or extract the CA from an existing install).
2. Export Kaspersky's CA cert + private key.
3. Use that CA as the signing CA for mitmproxy.
4. The service accepts certs signed by that CA (because it's in the trust store Kaspersky put it in).
5. mitmproxy sees cleartext.

Combined with:

- The **named events** (`Global\EasyAntiCheat_EOSEvent*`) for driver/game/module heartbeat signals.
- The **`ErrRpt_*` registry state** for post-session state inspection.
- **Named-pipe tapping** inside the game process for the agent ↔ launcher pipe (see [05 — pipe protocol](05-pipe-protocol.md)).

you get the **full outbound picture without touching the kernel at all.**

---

## What you capture per session

Running the above setup against one Fortnite session gives you:

| Source | Captures |
|-|-|
| mitmproxy on datarouter endpoint | Every event name + event body + session id |
| Pipe tap in-process | Agent ↔ launcher heartbeats, flag reports, module list, VAD list, driver list |
| Named-event subscriber | Driver init / game init / module load timing |
| Registry snapshot | ErrRpt state, Attestation state |

Running the same across N sessions on the same machine gives you the **per-handler feature-flag rotation** (see [15.2.e](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#152e--flattening--selector--kill-switches)) — because the backend is deciding which handlers fire each session, and you can watch the flag bitmap change.

---

## Signal: pre-ban activity

Direct observation: **rising `IntegrityViolation` / `GameTrust` event frequency is the pre-wave signal.**

Normal baseline is `DriverInitResult` + `DriverInitTime` + a few `SandboxInfo` per session. If you start seeing `IntegrityViolation` or multiple `GameTrust` per session, your HWID / driver / network has likely been flagged and the backend is collecting correlation data for an imminent ban.

Weeks-to-months window typically; **minimum ~1 week for new drivers.** Once fully detected and after collection phase, **ban is instant.**

---

**Next:** [17 — Trust-score model & feature flags](17-trust-score-model.md)

```

`docs/17-trust-score-model.md`:

```md
# 17 — Trust-score model & per-handler feature flags

How EAC's backend decides what detection runs on **your** machine, and how that interacts with your spoofer/cheat/driver.

The score is server-side. The client never sees it. The driver and service **feed** it. The feedback loop is which handlers fire on your next session.

---

## The three inputs

### 1. Certificate trust state of the machine

Driver walks Windows trust stores + Wintrust policy plumbing. See [15.6](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#156--trust-score-plumbing) for the specific string offsets.

**What this means operationally:**

- Every signed driver on your machine is a data point.
- Signed cheat drivers from 2016 with leaked / revoked certs show up as trust-positive but fingerprint-negative.
- A fresh clean Windows install with only Microsoft + major vendor signers has the highest baseline trust.
- A machine with many third-party / unknown signers has lower baseline trust.

### 2. TPM attestation

EAC captures the TPM endorsement key, requests PCR quotes signed by the AIK (Attestation Identity Key), binds the attestation to the EAC session id. Controlled by registry keys:

```
SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\Tpm\PlatformQuoteKeys
EacQuoteKey
AttestationKey
SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\EasyAntiCheat_EOS\Attestation
```

**What this means operationally:**

- Quotes are **cryptographically bound to physical TPM state.** A running hypervisor / tampered boot changes the PCR values → different quote → anomaly.
- The HWID isn't "just a text string" anymore when TPM attestation is in play. It's a cryptographic proof of boot state.
- Spoofers that don't address TPM (most don't) report consistent TPM-quote values across sessions. If the rest of the HWID changes but the TPM quote doesn't, the backend knows it's the same machine with a spoofer running.

### 3. Per-handler feature flags (the live-rotation dial)

This is the one most people don't know about.

The flattened command dispatcher at `0x18C0E6 – 0x18FE5D` ([15.2.e](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#152e--flattening--selector--kill-switches)) implements a per-handler feature-flag bit. Every detection routine checks its own flag before running:

```asm
mov  dl, byte ptr [r11+2]      ; flags byte from request record
mov  eax, r10d; shl eax,5; add eax,r10d; neg eax
xor  cl, 0x55                   ; shift1 = -(33*k) ^ 0x55
shr  dl, cl                     ; -> flags >> shift1
imul ecx, r10d, 0x3C
xor  cl, 0x1F                   ; mask = (k*0x3C) ^ 0x1F
and  cl, dl                     ; test = flags & mask
je   <skip handler>             ; == 0 -> handler is OFF
cmp  edx, 1                     ; == 1 -> primary path
```

**The feature-flag byte comes from the request record (`record+2`).** The request record is pushed from server-side to the driver per session. **The backend decides per-session which handlers fire.**

---

## What this means for "bypassing" EAC

If you bypass one check, you haven't bypassed the system. **The backend just rotates a flag.**

Consider a specific case: you build a spoofer that defeats `NtfL` (load-image notify). The first session, `NtfL` fires, finds nothing suspicious. The backend's correlation model notices "this machine had many driver loads in previous sessions, but none now — anomaly." Next session, the backend sets a flag turning on `SmBf` (SMBIOS fingerprint) with more granular checks + `WfpC` (WFP callouts) for network-level anomaly correlation. You might not even notice because you successfully defeated `NtfL`.

This is why "my cheat works, I tested it" doesn't mean "I'm safe." **What ran on your test session is not what will run on your next session.**

---

## Operational signals

### Normal session baseline

- Low event volume (maybe 5-20 events over a match).
- Mostly `DriverInitResult`, `DriverInitTime`, `SandboxInfo`, `DriverUnloadDuration`.
- No `IntegrityViolation` / `GameTrust` / `ValidateError`.

### Elevated-scan session (you're being correlation-mapped)

- Higher event volume (50-200+ per match).
- Multiple `IntegrityViolation` or `GameTrust` events.
- More granular `SandboxInfo` / `SandboxProcess` events (per-process reporting rather than per-session summaries).
- Higher-detail HWID collection (more `%016llx` pairs in the formatter output — meaning more storage/GPU/firmware IDs serialized).
- Possibly: **domain / IP rotation observed** in your outbound DNS logs (EAC backend trying to see if you'll follow a shifting endpoint).

### Pre-ban-wave session

- `IntegrityViolation` or `GameTrust` becomes near-constant.
- Session cuts short or gets disconnected with a soft error.
- Game crashes under what-looks-like-innocent conditions.
- Matchmaking into shadow-ban queue (matches with anomalously low-skill opponents, or lobbies that feel "off").

### Signal priority

Direct quote from the researcher:

> If you see high scan activity plus more / fuller HWID collection, your software might be flagged and they're just mapping out the userbase for a ban wave.

### Timeline

| Phase | Duration | Observable |
|-|-|-|
| Clean baseline | Normal | Few events, no error-family |
| Suspicion phase | Days | `IntegrityViolation` starts appearing |
| Collection phase | Weeks–months (never under 1 week for new drivers) | Scan activity rises, HWID collection broadens |
| Correlation complete | — | Backend has enough data |
| Ban wave | Instant once triggered | Hard ban or HWID ban |

---

## Why "rotating IP + IPv6 AAAA" is useful

Also directly from the researcher:

> I noticed signs pointing towards network endpoint observation, so if your low-user cheat gets detected but the driver itself is clean, rotating domain and server IP might help with that. I recommend IPv6 on AAAA record.

The idea: **when the backend starts observing that your cheat talks to a known-bad endpoint, that endpoint becomes a correlation data point separate from HWID.** Rotating domain + IPv6 AAAA records moves the observable endpoint faster than EAC's rule-update cycle can track. Doesn't help if the HWID itself is already flagged — only helps if the driver/cheat is clean but the network ties back to known-bad telemetry.

---

## Spoofer implications

### Naïve spoofer (static, single-session)

Rotates HWID values per session. **Caught by SMBIOS history** (section 15.5): "SMBIOS keeps changing" is itself an anomaly.

### Durable spoofer (single consistent fake HWID per machine)

Holds a fake HWID consistently. Harder to catch on HWID alone. **But** — trust score depends on more than HWID:

- Cert trust state (hard to spoof without reinstalling Windows).
- TPM attestation (very hard to spoof).
- Driver list fingerprint (your driver load pattern is itself a fingerprint).

### Smart spoofer (HWID + TPM + cert trust state)

Would need:

- Consistent fake HWID across sessions.
- A spoofed TPM (hypervisor-emulated, runs into anti-hypervisor flags).
- A clean cert-trust fingerprint (avoid loading uncommon signed drivers).

Which is why most commercial spoofers come as a **bundle: HWID spoof + specific-driver loader + "use with only these signed utilities installed"**. Not just a spoofer in isolation.

---

## Why bans take weeks (and then are instant)

The feature-flag rotation + correlation model means **the backend wants to be confident before issuing a hard ban.** False positives cost Epic more than missing a cheater for a week. So:

1. Suspect → low-rotating feature flags to probe which handlers your machine "behaves weirdly" under.
2. Correlate across N sessions.
3. Threshold hit → add to ban queue.
4. Ban wave → everyone in the queue gets banned at once.

Advantage from Epic's view: **cheat developers can't tell which specific session triggered the ban**, so they can't A/B-test their way out. The feedback signal is delayed by weeks.

---

**Next:** [18 — ETW-TI and kernel hazards for spoofers](18-etw-ti-and-kernel-hazards.md)

```

`docs/18-etw-ti-and-kernel-hazards.md`:

```md
# 18 — ETW-TI and kernel hazards for spoofers

The specific kernel-side observability mechanisms that current-build EAC uses, and what each one constrains about spoofer / cheat kernel code.

This chapter is the "stuff that catches you besides the obvious callbacks."

---

## ETW-TI subscription

**The Microsoft-Windows-Threat-Intelligence ETW provider.** GUID: `{F4E1897C-BB5D-5668-F1D8-040F4D8DD344}`.

A kernel ETW provider that emits events **from inside ntoskrnl** for specific suspicious operations. Normally subscribable only by Protected Process Light (PPL) subscribers — which EAC's driver qualifies as via its WHQL cross-signature.

### What it reports

Observable events when a process calls these from any thread:

| Operation | Event |
|-|-|
| `NtAllocateVirtualMemory` with exec protection | Virtual-allocation event |
| `NtProtectVirtualMemory` to change protection | **ProtectVirtualMemory event** |
| `NtMapViewOfSection` with exec | Mapped-view event |
| `NtReadVirtualMemory` cross-process | Cross-process-read event |
| `NtWriteVirtualMemory` cross-process | Cross-process-write event |
| `NtSetContextThread` cross-process | Context-set event |
| `NtQueueApcThread` cross-process | APC-queue event |
| `NtCreateThreadEx` cross-process | Remote-thread event |

Each event includes: **source process, target process, operation parameters, call stack.**

**EAC subscribes to this provider** (via the `EtwB` module from [15.3](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#section-153--the-46-module-manifest)) and relays suspicious events into its own telemetry.

---

## The spoofer problem — confirmed in `spoof.sys`

Direct evidence from a shipping HWID spoofer driver (`desire_market` build):

```
shm PATCH_PTE refused (EAC TI ProtectVm)
shm ALLOC refused (EAC TI AllocVm)
```

The spoofer uses a **shared-memory IPC** (`\BaseNamedObjects\EacEmuShm`) for user-mode cheat code to request kernel operations from the spoof driver. When the driver detects that EAC's TI subscription is active, it **refuses to perform PATCH_PTE or ALLOC on behalf of user-mode**, because those operations would generate TI events the EAC agent relays.

**The spoofer loses capability when EAC is watching.** This is the reason real commercial spoofers either:

1. Load **before** EAC's ETW subscription registers (race at boot).
2. Use operations that don't trigger TI events (physical-memory PTE writes, direct MMIO).
3. Accept the restriction and operate only in constrained ways.

---

## PatchGuard — what it prevents

**Kernel Patch Protection** periodically verifies a set of kernel structures and `.text` regions. Modifications → bugcheck `CRITICAL_STRUCTURE_CORRUPTION` (0x109).

Protected structures (non-exhaustive):

| Structure | What's protected |
|-|-|
| `ntoskrnl.exe` `.text` | Inline-hooking ntoskrnl functions is a bugcheck |
| `ntdll.dll` critical functions (user-mode but validated) | Some critical ntdll functions |
| `SSDT` (service descriptor table) | SSDT patching = bugcheck |
| `IDT` (interrupt descriptor table) | IDT hooking = bugcheck |
| `GDT` | GDT modifications = bugcheck |
| `KdpDebugRoutineSelect` | Kernel debugger table |
| Processor `MSRs` (specific set: `LSTAR`, etc.) | Syscall-handler MSR = bugcheck |
| `PsInvertedFunctionTable` | Exception-handling table |
| `KiServiceTable` | System call table |

**Implications for cheat / spoof drivers:**

- **No inline patching of ntoskrnl functions.** Use legal callbacks only.
- **No SSDT hooks.** Use kernel callbacks that forward to your handler.
- **No MSR rewriting of `LSTAR` to intercept syscalls.** The whole syscall-hooking approach is dead.

Legal alternatives:

- `PsSet*NotifyRoutine` for process/thread/image creation observation.
- `ObRegisterCallbacks` for handle-open interception.
- `CmRegisterCallback` for registry monitoring.
- `PsSetLegoNotifyRoutine` — stack-swap-time callback (rare use).
- `FltRegisterFilter` for filesystem filtering.
- `NdisFRegisterFilterDriver` for NDIS filtering.
- `MmGetSystemRoutineAddress` + device filter stacks for IOCTL interception.

**Everything EAC does is legal callback territory.** So anything you build has to coexist with EAC's callbacks without breaking the semantics EAC expects.

---

## HVCI / Code Integrity

**Hypervisor-Protected Code Integrity.** When enabled:

- Kernel pages marked executable must have a valid Microsoft-rooted signature.
- No kernel-mode `rwx` pages.
- No arbitrary executable allocation via `MmAllocateContiguousMemory` with `PAGE_EXECUTE_READWRITE`.
- Driver loads outside the Microsoft signing chain are rejected by the hypervisor, not just by user-mode code integrity.

**Implications:**

- **BYOVD on an HVCI-enabled system is harder.** The vulnerable driver loads, but any arbitrary kernel code you map via its primitive has to pass the hypervisor's integrity check too.
- `kdmapper` and similar tools fail on HVCI-enabled systems unless specifically designed for HVCI bypass (which involves finding a way to run attacker-chosen code in an existing legitimate executable region).
- EAC reports HVCI state as part of environmental fingerprint. Systems with HVCI off are a data point. **Turning HVCI off to run a cheat is itself a soft flag.**

---

## DMA Protection / Kernel DMA Protection

Controlled by:
- UEFI firmware's `DMA Protection` setting.
- Windows' `Kernel DMA Protection` policy.
- VT-d / AMD-Vi (IOMMU) state.

When enabled, **PCIe devices can only DMA into memory the OS has explicitly mapped for them.** Breaks external DMA cheats cleanly — the FPGA card can't just read anywhere; it has to go through IOMMU-mediated mappings.

**EAC reports DMA Protection state.** Systems with it off while a DMA cheat is active are a correlation data point. "This user has DMA Protection off" + "sometimes their aim is suspiciously perfect" = soft flag for behavioral correlation.

---

## Driver load auditing via WDAC

**Windows Defender Application Control** block list. Updated by Windows Update. EAC's `DRIVER_LIST` IOCTL ([02 — kernel driver](02-kernel-driver.md)) returns each loaded driver's WDAC-match state.

Known block-listed vulnerable drivers (publicly documented, representative subset):

```
RTCore64.sys       (MSI Afterburner — arbitrary MSR read/write)
GPC-U64.sys        (GPC — physical memory read/write)
ATSZIO64.sys       (ATSZ — arbitrary MSR + IO)
GDRV.sys           (Gigabyte — arbitrary I/O port)
DBUTIL_2_3.sys     (Dell — arbitrary MSR R/W)
WDLoadNTDriver.sys (WinRing0-related — arbitrary MSR)
AsIO.sys           (ASUS — arbitrary I/O port + MSR)
EneIo.sys          (ENE — physical memory read)
NalDrv.sys         (Intel Nahimic — arbitrary MSR)
LhaReb.sys         (various rebranded Realtek variants)
```

**EAC flags on WDAC-match regardless of whether the signature is still valid.** Microsoft revokes faster than Epic's rules.bin updates, so WDAC-match catches freshly-revoked drivers before rules.bin would.

---

## Boot chain observability

ACPI tables, UEFI variables, Secure Boot state, WBCL (Windows Boot Configuration Log) — all accessible to EAC's driver.

From `spoof.sys` strings:

```
AcpiRegistryPatched
AcpiTablesPatched
BootCurrent
BootNext
BootOptionSupport
BootOrder
SecureBoot
WBCLColdboot
WBCLDrtmColdboot
WindowsBootSession
```

These are the fields the spoofer has to maintain consistent spoofed values across. Specifically:

- **ACPI tables**: DSDT, SSDT, FACP, APIC, DMAR, IVRS, HPET, WAET, MCFG, BGRT, TPM2, TCPA, SLIC, MSDM, SRAT, SLIT, BERT, HEST, ERST, FPDT, NHLT, XSDT, RSDT, OEM1-4, DBG2, DBGP, WSMT, IORT, LPIT.
- **UEFI boot vars**: Order + current + next.
- **Secure Boot state**: enabled/disabled — EAC reports this.
- **WBCL** (Windows Boot Configuration Log): the event log of boot-phase decisions. Useful forensic artifact.

**A durable spoofer has to patch all of these before EAC's `env_worker` reads them.** Current-build EAC re-reads ACPI OEM ID + table IDs on the ~30s env_worker tick.

---

## Why cheats pre-load before EAC

Timing matters. If your driver loads **before** EAC's:

- Your filter drivers register above the storage/NDIS/display stack.
- Your ObCallbacks register before EAC's (altitude competition — you can pick an altitude above EAC's).
- You read baseline HWID / ETW state before EAC does.
- EAC's self-integrity check sees a kernel with "extra" callbacks that weren't there when previous session's baseline was captured.

Current-build EAC catches late-loaded drivers via the "load time" classifier in the `DRIVER_LIST` IOCTL ([02](02-kernel-driver.md)) — drivers that load outside the boot-time window are flagged. **Which is a double-edge** — pre-boot is better for driver-load hygiene, worse because EAC's initial baseline captures your spoofer's effects as "the norm" and you can't change them later without drift detection firing.

---

## Signal: the ETW providers EAC subscribes to

Reconstructed from static analysis + behavior:

- `Microsoft-Windows-Threat-Intelligence` (TI).
- `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-Process`.
- `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-Registry` (complementing CmRegisterCallback).
- `Microsoft-Windows-DNS-Client`.
- `Microsoft-Windows-Winsock-AFD`.
- `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-Audit-API-Calls`.

These are in addition to the custom callbacks (process/thread/image/handle). ETW gives EAC a second data channel into the same events, resistant to the "unregister the callback" cheat approach.

---

## Spoofer build requirements summary

For a spoofer to work reliably against current EAC:

1. **Load pre-boot** (UEFI bootkit or very-early boot driver) OR **load before EAC's service starts** AND register before EAC's TI subscription fires.
2. **Patch SMBIOS at the physical table + ntoskrnl cache.**
3. **Hook storage stack** (disk.sys, storahci, stornvme, storport) for serial-number IOCTLs.
4. **Hook NDIS** for permanent-MAC IOCTL.
5. **Hook display stack** or inject EDID into registry early.
6. **Patch ACPI OEM IDs** consistently.
7. **Don't trigger TI events** when operating — so no `NtProtectVirtualMemory` to change page protections after boot, no `NtAllocateVirtualMemory` for executable memory. Use boot-time allocations, no runtime patching.
8. **Maintain SMBIOS history consistency** — same values across sessions.
9. **Keep driver image hash out of WDAC block list** — rotate BYOVD or self-sign with clean cert.
10. **Match load time to "boot bulk" window.**

This is a lot. Which is why "spoofer development" is a full-time job for the few teams that actually ship reliable ones, and why commercial spoofers cost what they do.

---

**Next:** [Analysis — three emulator architectures compared](analysis/00-index.md)

```md
# 19 — Exhaustive fingerprint / trace / check surface (the catch-all)

Everything EAC can observe about your machine, every trace it leaves, every identifier that goes into HWID binding, every CPU/firmware/kernel/network check — in one catalog. If something isn't in the other docs, it's in here. Cross-referenced where appropriate.

**Read as reference, not linear.** Use Ctrl+F.

---

## Part 1 — Hardware identifiers (every field, every subsystem)

### 1.1 — CPU identifiers

**Via CPUID (user-mode accessible, no kernel required):**

| Leaf | Field |
|-|-|
| `0x00` | Vendor string (GenuineIntel / AuthenticAMD / HygonGenuine / CentaurHauls / etc.) + max leaf |
| `0x01` | Family / Model / Stepping (EAX), brand / APIC ID / core count (EBX), feature flags (ECX + EDX) |
| `0x02` | Cache + TLB descriptors (Intel legacy) |
| `0x03` | **Processor Serial Number** — deprecated on P4+, still probed by EAC per [15.5](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#section-155--hwid-formatter-0x914b2--0x92e78) |
| `0x04` | Cache topology (deterministic cache parameters) |
| `0x05` | MONITOR/MWAIT info |
| `0x06` | Thermal and power management features |
| `0x07` | Structured extended feature flags (sub-leaves 0, 1, 2) |
| `0x09` | Direct Cache Access (DCA) |
| `0x0A` | Architectural Performance Monitoring |
| `0x0B` | Extended topology (SMT, core, package) |
| `0x0D` | XSAVE features + state components |
| `0x0F` | Resource Director Technology (RDT) monitoring |
| `0x10` | RDT allocation |
| `0x11` | — |
| `0x12` | SGX capabilities (sub-leaves 0, 1, 2+) |
| `0x14` | Processor Trace enumeration |
| `0x15` | **TSC and core crystal clock info** — `CoreCrystalFreq = TSCFreq * EBX / EAX` |
| `0x16` | Processor frequency info (Base, Max, Bus) — must be nonzero on real hardware |
| `0x17` | SoC Vendor attributes |
| `0x18` | Virtual Address Translation parameters |
| `0x19` | Key Locker info (Intel KL) |
| `0x1A` | Hybrid information (P-core vs E-core on 12th gen Intel+) |
| `0x1B` | PCONFIG target identification |
| `0x1C` | Last Branch Records (LBR) |
| `0x1D` / `0x1E` | AMX (Advanced Matrix Extensions) tile / TMUL info |
| `0x1F` | V2 extended topology |
| `0x20` | HRESET features |
| `0x21` | Reserved / AMX-reserved |
| `0x40000000` | **Hypervisor-present leaf** — brand string if HV present |
| `0x40000001..0x4000000A` | Hyper-V TLFS leaves if Hyper-V is HV |
| `0x80000000` | Max extended leaf |
| `0x80000001` | Extended features (LAHF, SVM, LM, etc.) |
| `0x80000002..0x80000004` | Brand string ("Intel(R) Core(TM)...") — 48 chars |
| `0x80000005` | L1 cache + TLB |
| `0x80000006` | L2 + L3 cache |
| `0x80000007` | APM features |
| `0x80000008` | Virtual + physical address sizes |
| `0x8000000A` | SVM (AMD virtualization) features |
| `0x80000019` | TLB characteristics |
| `0x8000001D` | Cache properties |
| `0x8000001E` | Extended APIC + topology |
| `0x8000001F` | SEV + SME features (AMD confidential compute) |
| `0x80000020` | PQoS |
| `0x80000021` | Extended feature identification (AMD) |
| `0x8FFFFFFF` | Centaur-private / reserved |

**Via MSR reads (kernel-mode only):**

| MSR | Address | What |
|-|-|-|
| `IA32_PLATFORM_ID` | `0x17` | Platform ID for microcode binding |
| `IA32_APIC_BASE` | `0x1B` | APIC base + enable + BSP flag |
| `IA32_FEATURE_CONTROL` | `0x3A` | VMX enable, SGX enable, etc. |
| `IA32_TSC_ADJUST` | `0x3B` | TSC adjustment value (per-core) |
| `IA32_SPEC_CTRL` | `0x48` | Speculation control (IBRS/STIBP/SSBD) |
| `IA32_PRED_CMD` | `0x49` | Branch prediction barrier |
| `IA32_BIOS_UPDT_TRIG` | `0x79` | Microcode update |
| `IA32_BIOS_SIGN_ID` / `IA32_UCODE_REV` | `0x8B` | **Microcode revision** |
| `IA32_PLATFORM_INFO` | `0xCE` | Platform-specific info (max non-turbo ratio etc.) |
| `IA32_PERFEVTSEL0..7` | `0x186+` | Performance counter config |
| `IA32_PERFCTR0..7` | `0xC1+` | Performance counter values |
| `IA32_MTRRCAP` | `0xFE` | MTRR capabilities |
| `IA32_SYSENTER_CS/ESP/EIP` | `0x174..0x176` | SYSENTER state |
| `IA32_DEBUGCTL` | `0x1D9` | Debug control (LBR, BTS) |
| `IA32_MTRR_PHYSBASE0..N` / `PHYSMASK0..N` | `0x200+` | Memory Type Range Registers |
| `IA32_PAT` | `0x277` | Page Attribute Table |
| `IA32_EFER` | `0xC0000080` | Extended Feature Enable Register (LMA, SCE, NXE, SVME) |
| `IA32_STAR/LSTAR/CSTAR/FMASK` | `0xC0000081..84` | Syscall state (PatchGuard protects these) |
| `IA32_FS_BASE` / `GS_BASE` / `KERNEL_GS_BASE` | `0xC0000100..102` | Thread-local base registers |

### 1.2 — SMBIOS (all tables)

Per [15.5](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#section-155--hwid-formatter-0x914b2--0x92e78), accessed via `NtQuerySystemInformation(SystemFirmwareTableInformation, 'RSMB')`, WMI `Win32_*`, or `GetSystemFirmwareTable`. **Includes history** — driver remembers values across sessions.

| Type | Table | Key fields |
|-|-|-|
| 0 | BIOS | Vendor, Version, Release Date, BIOS Characteristics, Embedded Controller firmware version |
| 1 | System | Manufacturer, Product, Version, **Serial Number**, **UUID**, WakeUp Type, SKU, Family |
| 2 | Baseboard | Manufacturer, Product, Version, **Serial Number**, Asset Tag, Features, Chassis Handle |
| 3 | Chassis | Manufacturer, Type, Version, **Serial Number**, Asset Tag, Boot-up State, Power Supply State, Thermal State, Security Status, OEM Info |
| 4 | Processor (per CPU) | Socket, Manufacturer, Version, Voltage, External Clock, Max Speed, Current Speed, Status, **Processor ID**, Serial Number, Asset Tag, Part Number |
| 7 | Cache (per cache) | Socket Designation, Configuration, Max Size, Installed Size, Speed, Error Correction Type, System Cache Type |
| 9 | System Slots | Slot Designation, Type, Data Bus Width, Current Usage, Length, Slot ID, Characteristics |
| 11 | OEM Strings | Free-form OEM strings (often used for Windows OEM activation keys, vendor info) |
| 12 | System Configuration Options | Configuration strings |
| 13 | BIOS Language Info | Installable languages, current language |
| 16 | Physical Memory Array | Location, Use, Error Correction, Max Capacity, Number of Devices |
| 17 | Memory Device (per DIMM) | Total Width, Data Width, Size, Form Factor, Locator, Bank Locator, Type, Type Detail, Speed, Manufacturer, **Serial Number**, Asset Tag, Part Number, Rank, Configured Memory Speed, Minimum/Maximum Voltage |
| 19 | Memory Array Mapped Address | Starting Address, Ending Address, Memory Array Handle |
| 20 | Memory Device Mapped Address | Starting Address, Ending Address, Memory Device Handle |
| 32 | System Boot Info | Boot Status |
| 38 | IPMI Device Info | IPMI version, I2C target address, etc. |
| 39 | Power Supply (per supply) | Manufacturer, Serial Number, Asset Tag, Model, Revision |
| 41 | Onboard Devices Extended Info | Reference Designation, Device Type, Device Status, Type Instance, Segment Group Number, Bus Number, Device/Function Number |

### 1.3 — Storage devices

Beyond physical disk serial:

| Field | Access path |
|-|-|
| Physical disk serial, model, firmware | `IOCTL_STORAGE_QUERY_PROPERTY` → `StorageDeviceProperty` |
| Disk adapter info | `IOCTL_STORAGE_QUERY_PROPERTY` → `StorageAdapterProperty` |
| NVMe EUI64 + NGUID | NVMe Identify Controller via `IOCTL_STORAGE_PROTOCOL_COMMAND` |
| NVMe namespace ID | per-namespace identifier |
| NVMe controller ID (CNTLID) | Identify Controller response |
| NVMe subsystem NQN | NVMe Subsystem Qualified Name |
| SATA controller device ID | PCI config space of the storage controller |
| SATA signature | PM registers of each port |
| **SCSI LUN** | per [15.5](15-driver-raw-offsets.md) — formatter string `":lun=%llx"` |
| **MBR disk signature** | Dword at offset `0x1B8` of MBR — formatter `"MBR:%08x"` |
| **Partition index** | formatter `"Part:%u"` |
| **Optical drive index** | formatter `"CD:%u"` |
| Volume serial (NTFS) | 32-bit, boot sector |
| Volume serial (FAT) | 32-bit, VBR |
| $MFT entry 0 creation time | Volume creation timestamp, forensically stable |
| EFI System Partition GUID | GPT, stable per install |
| GPT Disk GUID | GPT header, per-disk unique |
| GPT Partition GUIDs | per-partition, stable |
| S.M.A.R.T attribute values | Power-on hours, reallocated sector count, etc. — not identifying alone but contributes |
| BitLocker volume ID (if encrypted) | per-volume FVE ID |
| Shadow Copy history | Per-volume VSS snapshot list |
| SRL (Storage Replica Log) presence | Server SKUs |

### 1.4 — Network (deep)

Beyond MAC addresses:

| Field | Source |
|-|-|
| Permanent MAC per adapter | `OID_802_3_PERMANENT_ADDRESS` via NDIS |
| Current MAC | `OID_802_3_CURRENT_ADDRESS` |
| Interface GUID | `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Network\{4d36e972-...}\<guid>` |
| Driver DeviceInstanceID | `PCI\VEN_XXXX&DEV_XXXX&SUBSYS_XXXX&REV_XX\<instance>` |
| IPv6 interface identifier (SLAAC) | EUI-64 derived from MAC |
| IPv6 temporary addresses | RFC 4941, randomized |
| IPv6 link-local | `fe80::...` |
| Configured DNS servers | per-interface |
| DHCP client identifier | `DhcpIdentifier` registry value |
| WAN public IP | `nltest`, external HTTPS probe, or routing table |
| ARP cache entries | gateway MAC + neighbor list |
| **Wi-Fi SSID history** | `HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\NetworkList\Profiles\` + `Signatures\Unmanaged\` |
| Wi-Fi BSSID (AP MAC) during session | wlanapi |
| **Bluetooth adapter address** | `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\BTHPORT\Parameters\LocalServices` |
| Bluetooth paired device list | Same registry hive |
| **TLS JA3/JA4 fingerprint** of your client hello | EAC backend observes passively |
| TCP MSS, window size, timestamp option | OS fingerprint passive |
| IPv6 temporary-address rotation cadence | Correlation signal |
| Default route metrics + DNS cache patterns | Route table + dnsapi |
| Teredo / 6to4 state | If enabled, identifier in prefix |
| NDI (Networking Diagnostic Information) telemetry provider | ETW |
| WinHTTP proxy config | `HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Internet Settings` |

| Field | Source |
|-|-|
| GPU adapter LUID | `IDXGIAdapter::GetDesc1` |

| EDID extensions (CEA) | Audio formats, HDMI vendor-specific data block |

### 1.6 — USB / HID

Each connected device:

| Field | Source |
|-|-|

| Field | Source |

### 1.8 — Firmware / boot (deep)

| Field | Source |
|-|-|
| UEFI firmware vendor + version | `GetFirmwareEnvironmentVariable` |
| **Secure Boot state** | `BOOT_OPTIONS` + UEFI var `SecureBoot` |
| **Secure Boot DB / DBX / KEK / PK hashes** | UEFI vars, TPM-measured |
| Boot Configuration Data (BCD) GUIDs | `BCDEdit` output / `HKLM\BCD00000000` |
| BootCurrent / BootNext / BootOrder | UEFI vars |
| HVCI state | `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\DeviceGuard\Scenarios\HypervisorEnforcedCodeIntegrity` |
| VBS state | `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\DeviceGuard\EnableVirtualizationBasedSecurity` |
| DMA Protection (Kernel DMA) | Policy registry + ACPI DMAR table |
| IOMMU (VT-d / AMD-Vi) state | ACPI DMAR/IVRS table parsing |
| **WBCL** (Windows Boot Configuration Log) | `C:\Windows\Logs\MeasuredBoot\` — per-boot `.log` files with TPM-measured values |
| **WBCL Coldboot / DRTM Coldboot** events | As called out in [spoof.sys](analysis/01-desire-market-emulator.md) |
| **ACPI tables** (all) | `NtQuerySystemInformation(SystemFirmwareTableInformation, 'ACPI')` — DSDT, SSDT, FACP, APIC, DMAR, IVRS, HPET, WAET, MCFG, BGRT, TPM2, TCPA, SLIC, MSDM, SRAT, SLIT, BERT, HEST, ERST, FPDT, NHLT, XSDT, RSDT, OEM1-4, DBG2, DBGP, WSMT, IORT, LPIT |
| ACPI OEM ID + OEM Table ID | All tables have these — fingerprint of firmware |
| MSDM table contents | OEM Windows product key — not read by EAC typically but fingerprint source |
| Firmware mode (UEFI vs Legacy) | `GetFirmwareType` API |
| CSM (Compatibility Support Module) state | UEFI setting |
| TPM version (1.2 / 2.0) | TBS `Tbsi_GetDeviceInfo` |
| TPM manufacturer | TBS |
| TPM firmware version | TBS |
| **TPM Endorsement Key public** | TPM2_ReadPublic for EK handle |
| EK certificate | `TpmRetrieveEkCertificate` |
| TPM PCR values (per bank: SHA1, SHA256) | TPM2_PCR_Read |
| AIK (Attestation Identity Key) | Per-platform, OS-provisioned |
| TPM quote on PCRs | **Bound to EAC session via `EacQuoteKey`** |
| Windows DRTM (Secure Launch) state | Boot measurement events |

### 1.9 — Memory (RAM) fingerprint

| Field | Source |
|-|-|
| RAM module serials (per-DIMM) | SMBIOS type 17 |
| RAM manufacturer per-DIMM | SMBIOS type 17 |
| RAM part number per-DIMM | SMBIOS type 17 |
| Total installed RAM | `GlobalMemoryStatusEx` |
| **SPD (Serial Presence Detect) raw data** | SMBus (via `\Driver\smbctrl` / `\Driver\i801` — called out in [spoof.sys](analysis/01-desire-market-emulator.md)) — **`RamSpdSerials`** field |
| XMP profile ID (if enabled) | SPD extension blocks |
| Memory channel configuration | SMBIOS |
| ECC vs non-ECC | SMBIOS + chipset |

### 1.10 — Power / battery

| Field | Source |
|-|-|
| Battery serial number | `GetSystemPowerStatus` + ACPI `_BIX` |
| Battery manufacturer, chemistry, design capacity | ACPI `_BIX` |
| AC adapter type | `SYSTEM_POWER_STATUS` |
| UPS presence + model | If present |
| CPU thermal design power (TDP) | SMBIOS |

### 1.11 — Camera / webcam

| Field | Source |
|-|-|
| Attached camera device paths | DirectShow / `IMFActivate` |
| Camera serial numbers (per-device) | USB descriptor if applicable |

### 1.12 — PCI device enumeration

For every PCI device, EAC's `Devi` module ([15.3](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#section-153--the-46-module-manifest)) can extract:

| Field |
|-|
| Vendor ID / Device ID |
| Subsystem Vendor ID / Subsystem Device ID |
| Revision ID |
| Class / Subclass / Programming Interface |
| Capabilities list (walk linked list at config offset 0x34) |
| PCIe capability — Device/Link/Slot caps + control + status |
| Message Signaled Interrupts (MSI/MSI-X) state |
| Bus/Device/Function (BDF) — topology |
| Secondary bus number (bridges) |
| BAR values (Base Address Registers) |
| Interrupt line + pin |
| **PnP instance path** — e.g. `PCI\VEN_10DE&DEV_2504&SUBSYS_41501458&REV_A1\4&3C24B4C&0&0008` — covered under `PciInstancesSpoofed` in [spoof.sys](analysis/01-desire-market-emulator.md) |
| ACS (Access Control Services) capability |
| ATS (Address Translation Services) state |
| PRI (Page Request Interface) state |

### 1.13 — SMBus / sensors

Called out by name in [spoof.sys](analysis/01-desire-market-emulator.md) (`\Driver\SMBUS`, `\Driver\amd_smbus`, `\Driver\i801`, `\Driver\smbctrl`):

| What |
|-|
| SPD data for RAM (via SMBus addresses 0x50-0x57) |
| Super I/O controller IDs |
| Thermal sensor readings |
| Fan controller state |

| `DigitalProductId` | Same key — 4 bytes of install ID, per-install unique |
| `DigitalProductId4` | Newer schema |
| **Edition ID** | Same key, `EditionID` |
| **Product Name** | Same key, `ProductName` |
| **CurrentBuild** / `CurrentBuildNumber` / `UBR` | Same key — exact build + Update Build Revision |
| `DisplayVersion` / `ReleaseId` | Same key — "22H2" / "24H2" etc. |
| `RegisteredOwner` / `RegisteredOrganization` | Same key |
| Upgrade history | `HKLM\SYSTEM\Setup\Source OS *` subkeys if upgraded |
| Windows Update HistoryID | `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\WaaSMedicSvc\PendingActionsCache` + `WindowsUpdate` client ID |
| Windows **Device ID** (via Microsoft Account) | `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\DsSvc` |

## Part 3 — Kernel-side observable surfaces

### 3.1 — ETW providers EAC may subscribe to

Beyond `Microsoft-Windows-Threat-Intelligence` ([18](18-etw-ti-and-kernel-hazards.md)):

| Provider | What it reveals |
|-|-|
| `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-Process` | Process creation, thread creation, image load (cross-check against callbacks) |
| `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-Registry` | Every registry read/write (cross-check against CmRegisterCallback) |
| `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-File` | File operations |
| `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-Memory` | Memory allocation events |
| `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-Audit-API-Calls` | Audited sensitive API calls |
| `Microsoft-Windows-DNS-Client` | DNS queries made by any process |
| `Microsoft-Windows-Winsock-AFD` | Socket operations |
| `Microsoft-Windows-TCPIP` | TCP connection events |
| `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-PnP` | Device plug/unplug |
| `Microsoft-Windows-USB-USBHUB3` | USB hub events |
| `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-Image` | Image load events |
| `Microsoft-Windows-Services` | Service state changes |

### 3.2 — Kernel structures walked

| Structure | Access |
|-|-|
| `PsLoadedModuleList` | Walk list head to enumerate loaded drivers |
| Driver object trees | `IoDriverObjectType` namespace walk |
| Device object trees | per `IoDeviceObjectType` |
| `KeServiceDescriptorTable` | SSDT (PatchGuard protected, read-only observation) |
| `KiServiceTable` entries | For syscall-function fingerprinting |
| `MmPfnDatabase` | Page Frame Number database — can detect swapped physical pages |
| `PsActiveProcessHead` | Active process list head |
| EPROCESS `VadRoot` offset | Per-process VAD tree root ([02](02-kernel-driver.md)) |
| EPROCESS `Token` | Current token |
| EPROCESS `ImageFileName` | Short process name |
| EPROCESS `Peb` | Process Environment Block pointer |
| ETHREAD `Tcb.ApcState` | APC queue state — detects APC injection |
| ETHREAD `StartAddress` | Thread entry point |
| ETHREAD `Win32StartAddress` | User-mode thread start |
| `KUSER_SHARED_DATA` | Shared kernel/user page — version info, time, feature flags |
| `KPRCB` (Processor Control Block) | Per-CPU state including context switches |

### 3.3 — Handle table walks

| What |
|-|
| System-wide handle table enumeration (`NtQuerySystemInformation(SystemHandleInformation)`) |
| Handles to protected process (filter by target PID) |
| Handles to protected threads |
| Handles to protected sections |
| Granted access masks per handle |
| Opener PID per handle |

### 3.4 — Object namespace

| Objects created / observed by EAC |
|-|
| `\Device\EasyAntiCheat_<random>` |
| `\DosDevices\EasyAntiCheat` |
| `\BaseNamedObjects\Global\EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventDriver` |
| `\BaseNamedObjects\Global\EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventGame` |
| `\BaseNamedObjects\Global\EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventModule` |
| Named pipes (`\Device\NamedPipe\EasyAntiCheat_<guid>`) |
| Shared memory sections (`\BaseNamedObjects\...`) |

### 3.5 — Memory introspection (`ViMm` module)

| What |
|-|
| VAD tree enumeration per process |
| EPROCESS field consistency (e.g., `UniqueProcessId` matches handle table) |
| Page protection state per VAD |
| Executable memory not backed by a module (shellcode detection) |
| Transient-executable VADs (R/W → R/X flip detection) |
| Entropy analysis of executable regions |
| PE header integrity for loaded modules |
| Section hash drift from baseline |

### 3.6 — Register / context inspection

| What | Access |
|-|-|
| Debug registers Dr0..Dr7 (per thread) | `GetThreadContext` |
| Control registers CR0..CR4 | kernel-side, MSR-adjacent |
| XCR0 (extended control) | `XGETBV` |
| FS/GS base | `rdfsbase` / `rdgsbase` or MSR |
| MXCSR state | FPU state save |
| Flags register | per-syscall analysis |

## Part 5 — Timing fingerprints

Per [07 Fortnite integrity](07-fortnite-integrity.md) + [15.5 HWID](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#section-155--hwid-formatter-0x914b2--0x92e78), EAC uses rdtsc timing extensively:

| Timing probe | What it detects |
|-|-|
| rdtsc delta across CPUID | CPUID vmexit cost under hypervisor |
| rdtsc delta across MSR read | MSR vmexit cost under hypervisor |
| rdtsc delta across port I/O | I/O vmexit cost |
| rdtsc delta across interrupt | Interrupt latency fingerprint |
| QPC (QueryPerformanceCounter) vs rdtsc | TSC consistency across cores |
| HPET vs TSC | Timer source consistency |
| APIC timer vs rdtsc | Timer cross-check |
| TSC desync between cores | CPU-level fingerprint |
| Context switch latency | Scheduler fingerprint |
| IPI (Inter-Processor Interrupt) latency | SMP fingerprint |

## Part 7 — EOS / Epic identity stack

Beyond machine identity, Epic specifically tracks:

| Field | Source |
|-|-|
| Epic Account ID (EpicAccountId) | Online auth session |
| Epic Product User ID (EOS_ProductUserId) | Per-product-sandbox identity |
| Epic Device ID | `%LOCALAPPDATA%\EpicGamesLauncher\Saved\DeviceIdCache` |
| Epic launcher install GUID | `HKLM\SOFTWARE\Epic Games\EpicGamesLauncher\AppDataPath` + install-time stamp |
| OAuth refresh tokens | Encrypted in launcher data |
| Session access tokens (JWT) | Memory + launcher data |
| EOS SandboxId (per game) | Per product |
| EOS DeploymentId | Per product |
| Payment method fingerprints (if purchases made) | Server-side only, not readable client-side |
| Linked platform accounts | Steam, Xbox, PSN, Nintendo — linked at account level |
| Friend list | Server-side, cached locally per session |
| Fortnite session continuation tokens | Per-match |
| Battle Pass state | Per-account |

**Account-level HWID binding:** Epic ties specific machine identifiers to the account at signup time + first-time-on-new-hardware moments. Playing from a new HWID on a banned account requires either (a) HWID spoof to match a non-banned HWID, (b) a new account and new hardware altogether.

---

## Part 8 — Cheat-indicator enumeration (expanded from [12])

Additional signals beyond [12 cheat detection](12-cheat-detection.md):

### 8.1 — Hypervisor-specific indicators

| Signal |
|-|
| CPUID hypervisor bit (`CPUID 1 ECX[31]`) |
| CPUID 0x40000000 brand string |
| Hyper-V-specific leaves (0x40000001-0x4000000A) |
| MSR `MSR_IA32_FEATURE_CONTROL` lock state |
| VMX state via `vmread` (requires ring 0, but observable indirectly) |
| APIC ID topology inconsistency |
| Intel PT (Processor Trace) availability vs expected |
| LBR (Last Branch Record) MSR state (`IA32_DEBUGCTL.LBR`) |
| TSC-adjust MSR nonzero |
| Shadow MMIO regions in physical memory |
| Timing: CPUID cost ratio vs rdtsc baseline (detailed in [18](18-etw-ti-and-kernel-hazards.md)) |
| VM-exit cost of specific instructions (cpuid, rdmsr, invlpg, specific port I/O) |
| `rdpmc` behavior (fixed counter availability under HV) |

### 8.2 — DMA cheat indicators

| Signal |
|-|
| PCI config space of every device (vendor/device/subsys IDs) |
| **PCIe capability list walk** — ACS, ATS, PRI, device/link/slot caps |
| Unknown vendor IDs or recent-FPGA vendor IDs (Xilinx, Lattice, Altera) |
| Devices with implausible subsystem IDs (SubVendor = SubDevice, or trivial patterns) |
| IOMMU state — if disabled, DMA attack possible |
| Devices with Bus Master enable bit set but no legitimate reason |
| MMIO regions mapped outside expected ranges |
| USB-C alternate mode / Thunderbolt DMA capability |

### 8.3 — Kmbox / external input indicators

| Signal |
|-|
| USB-connected HID device whose VID/PID matches known kmbox fingerprints |
| Mouse driver chain inspection (as per [04](04-user-mode-agent.md)) |
| HID report descriptor hash matches known kmbox / Arduino-mouse |
| USB device enumeration pattern during session (hot-plug of a "mouse" mid-session is suspicious) |
| Input event timing — kmbox-injected events have characteristic latency to USB polling intervals |

### 8.4 — Screen-reader / capture-card cheat indicators

| Signal |
|-|
| Capture card devices in device tree |
| HDMI/DP output cloning state |
| DWM composition flags |
| Second GPU output in use |
| Frame-capture-rate anomaly (game frames being pulled at non-standard rate) |

### 8.5 — Kernel-callback-based cheat indicators

| Signal |
|-|
| Competing `PsSetCreateProcessNotifyRoutine` entries — CALLBACK_DUMP IOCTL returns these |
| Competing `PsSetLoadImageNotifyRoutine` entries |
| ObCallbacks table — unknown altitudes nearby EAC's altitude |
| CmRegisterCallback entries — unknown callback routines registered |
| Driver images with hashes not in WDAC block list AND not in Microsoft's signed-driver set |
| Manual-mapped drivers (no backing file at recorded path) |

### 8.6 — User-mode cheat indicators

Beyond what [12] covers:

| Signal |
|-|
| LoadLibraryExW calls with non-standard flags |
| LoadLibrary from non-system paths into protected process |
| Manual IAT rebuild patterns (byte signatures of reflective loader IAT fixup) |
| Hash-resolved import patterns (`imul rax, 0x<hash>` + lookup) — the same technique EAC uses, so ironic |
| Suspicious thread creation flags (`CREATE_SUSPENDED` from external caller) |
| Window message hooks (`SetWindowsHookEx` with `WH_*` from non-whitelisted module) |
| Vectored exception handler count increase |
| AddDllDirectory additions from non-game paths |

---

## Part 9 — Kernel objects a spoofer must be aware of

From [spoof.sys](analysis/01-desire-market-emulator.md) + general knowledge:

| Object | What it is |
|-|-|
| `\BaseNamedObjects\EacEmuShm` | Shared memory for user ↔ kernel IPC in the spoof driver |
| `\Instances\XHWIDSpoofer Instance` | Minifilter instance name |
| `\Driver\Disk` | Base disk driver — filtered for serial/model spoof |
| `\Driver\storahci` | AHCI storage driver |
| `\Driver\stornvme` | NVMe storage driver |
| `\Driver\storport` | Storage port driver |
| `\Driver\USBHUB` + `\Driver\USBHUB3` | USB hub drivers |
| `\Driver\BTHPORT` / `\Driver\BTHUSB` | Bluetooth |
| `\Driver\hidusb` | HID over USB |
| `\Driver\SMBUS` / `\Driver\AMDSMBUS` / `\Driver\amd_smbus` | SMBus (for SPD / RAM serial) |
| `\Driver\i801` | Intel SMBus controller |
| `\Driver\smbctrl` | SMBus controller abstraction |
| `\Driver\IntelTA` | Intel Trusted Agent |
| `\FileSystem\Ntfs` / `\FileSystem\refs` | Filesystem drivers (for volume serial manipulation) |
| `\Registry\Machine\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\Class\{4d36e967-...}` | **Disk drives** PnP class |
| `\Registry\...\Class\{4d36e968-...}` | **Display adapters** PnP class |
| `\Registry\...\Class\{4d36e96e-...}` | **USB controllers** PnP class |
| `\Registry\...\Class\{4d36e97b-...}` | **SCSI and RAID** PnP class |
| `\Registry\Machine\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Enum\DISPLAY` | Display enumeration |
| `\Registry\Machine\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Enum\MONITOR` | Monitor enumeration |

## Part 10 — The TPM attack surface (deep)

Per [15.6](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#156--trust-score-plumbing):

### What EAC requests from TPM

| Operation | Purpose |
|-|-|
| `TBS_Create_Context` | Open a TBS session |
| `TBS_GetDeviceInfo` | TPM version / manufacturer |
| `TPM2_ReadPublic` on EK handle (`0x81010001` typically) | Read EK public — strong identifier |
| `TpmRetrieveEkCertificate` | Retrieve EK cert from TPM NV storage |
| PCR reads (`TPM2_PCR_Read`) on PCRs 0-23 | Measured boot state |
| AIK-signed quote generation | Bind to EAC session |

### PCR meanings (standard)

| PCR | What it measures |
|-|-|
| 0 | Core Root of Trust for Measurement (CRTM), BIOS code |
| 1 | Platform configuration / firmware options |
| 2 | Option ROM code |
| 3 | Option ROM configuration |
| 4 | IPL (Initial Program Loader) code (Windows Boot Manager) |
| 5 | IPL configuration |
| 6 | State transitions and wake events |
| 7 | **Secure Boot policy, DB, DBX, KEK, PK** |
| 8 | Windows OS Loader (winload.efi) |
| 9 | Windows Resume Loader (winresume.efi) |
| 10 | Windows OS Kernel |
| 11 | BitLocker access control (sealing key) |
| 12-15 | Reserved / debugging / deeper kernel |
| 16 | Debug |
| 17-22 | Dynamic Root of Trust (TXT/SVM) |
| 23 | Application support |

### Why PCR 7 matters for EAC

If PCR 7 changes between sessions on the same machine, it means Secure Boot policy / DB / KEK was touched → anomaly. **Loading unsigned drivers changes measured boot → PCR drift → EAC sees inconsistent attestation.**

### Why TPM is "nearly impossible to spoof"

- The EK private key never leaves the TPM. You can't extract it.
- Quotes are AIK-signed, and AIKs are themselves rooted in the EK.
- Even if you virtualize the TPM, the EK of the virtual TPM is different from the physical EK, and Epic's backend binds the EAC session to the EK it saw on signup.

---

## Part 11 — Specific checks summarized by what they catch

| What EAC wants to catch | Checks used |
|-|-|
| User-mode cheat injected into game process | VAD walker, module scan, thread callback, image load callback, pattern scan |
| External memory reader | Handle strip (ObCallbacks), ENUM_HANDLES |
| Cheat kernel driver loaded | DRIVER_LIST + WDAC state, CALLBACK_DUMP, PsLoadedModuleList walk |
| Cheat kernel driver manual-mapped | VAD + no backing file, missing LDR entry |
| Hypervisor-based cheat | Anti-VM (CPUID, APIC, timing), MSR state |
| DMA attack via FPGA PCIe card | PCI config enum, IOMMU state, DMA protection state |
| Kmbox / external input hardware | Mouse driver chain, HID descriptor hash, input anomaly ML |
| HWID spoofer (various quality) | SMBIOS history, TPM attestation drift, ETW-TI violations, cert trust state |
| Debugger attached | Multiple anti-debug (PEB, flags, HWBP, timing, kernel) |
| VM environment | Anti-VM suite |
| Overlay drawing over game | Window scanner + DXGI capture + ML |
| Game memory tampering | Integrity timer (self + game modules) |
| Modified EAC itself | Driver self-hash + agent self-hash |
| Server-sent message tampering | Ed25519 signatures on EAC packets |
| Macro / recoil script | Input distributional ML |
| Trigger-bot pixel sampling | Input time-to-shot, frame-capture ML |
| Impossibly good aim / tracking | Server-side behavioral ML |
| New hardware from banned account | HWID fingerprint hash comparison |

---

## Part 12 — The things not easily spoofed

Hierarchy of "how hard to fake" for common HWID sources, worst-to-best spoofer target:

1. **TPM EK public hash** — near-impossible without clearing physical TPM or hypervisor virt-TPM.
2. **TPM PCR values / attestation quotes** — hypervisor virt only.
3. **SMBIOS values with history matching** — hard but doable with persistent driver.
4. **CPU identifiers (CPUID)** — hypervisor-level only; bare-metal nearly impossible.
5. **ACPI OEM IDs (DSDT, SSDT, OEM strings)** — doable with ACPI table patching at boot.
6. **Permanent MAC via NDIS** — doable with NDIS filter.
7. **Physical disk serial** — doable with storage filter driver.
8. **Volume serial (NTFS boot sector)** — doable but conflicts with Secure Boot + BitLocker.
9. **Monitor EDID** — doable via registry write + display driver filter.
10. **Machine GUID / registry identity fields** — trivial registry writes.

**Operational implication:** a spoofer that only handles items 6-10 looks like a spoofer. A spoofer handling 3-10 is "commercial quality." Item 1-2 requires either TPM clear (destroys BitLocker) or hypervisor (triggers anti-VM).

---

## Part 13 — Summary catalog

Fields to verify in your own live-reverse session against YOUR machine, cross-referenced to the docs:

- Hardware inventory: see Parts 1.1-1.12
- OS inventory: see Parts 2.1-2.5
- On-disk trace inventory: see Part 6
- Kernel surfaces EAC uses: see Part 3
- TPM: see Part 10
- Network: see Part 4
- Timing: see Part 5
- EOS/Epic identity: see Part 7

**Nothing significant is omitted from this catalog as of the current build of the researcher's knowledge.** If you encounter something not listed, (a) it's a very recent EAC addition, or (b) it's a non-EAC side-channel Epic uses outside the anti-cheat pipeline. Either way worth documenting and adding here.
