`docs/20-live-server-bypass-reality.md`:

```md
# 20 — Live-server bypass: what's actually required, what's been tried, what works

The gap the PoC in [13 EAC emulation](13-eac-emulation.md) doesn't close. This chapter is "why" — what makes Fortnite's live-server authentication cryptographically hard to forge, which attacks theoretically close it, and what real projects have actually achieved.

---

## Part 1 — What "live-server bypass" actually requires

Fortnite's server-side acceptance of your session depends on EAC producing signed proofs that only the real EAC can produce. Walking the chain:

### The chain of signatures

1. **Launcher → EAC** — `FortniteLauncher.exe` signs a session token (per [03 launcher](03-user-mode-launcher.md)) with a private key baked into the launcher's signed binary + rotated per Epic build.
2. **EAC Driver → Service** — Driver SELF_CHECK IOCTL signs (hash || nonce || bootCookie) with **ed25519 private key embedded in the driver** ([02 kernel driver](02-kernel-driver.md)).
3. **EAC Agent → Fortnite Shipping** — Agent's init callback produces an ed25519 signature over session state ([00 current-build notes](00-current-build-notes.md)). Private key for this is session-ephemeral and derived via ECDH with Epic's backend.
4. **EAC Service → Epic Datarouter** — Service posts telemetry signed by an authenticated TLS session, cert pinned ([16 telemetry](16-telemetry-endpoints.md)).
5. **EAC Backend → Game Server** — Epic's backend returns session-attestation tokens the game server verifies.

**Each link must be unforgeable from the client side.** Break any one and the chain's intent is compromised.

### What you'd need to forge a live Fortnite session

| Link | Required to break | Feasibility |
|-|-|-|
| 1 — Launcher session token | Steal launcher signing key OR patch Launcher to use your key | **Infeasible.** Launcher binary is signed with Epic's cert; patching it breaks its own signature check. |
| 2 — Driver SELF_CHECK ed25519 | Extract private key from signed `.sys` OR find reversal path to leak during runtime | **Infeasible.** Key is in VMProtect'd section. Even if extracted, backend verifies (hash matches public key record) so you'd need to rotate your driver's hash and get it on Epic's whitelist. |
| 3 — Agent init ed25519 | Compromise the ECDH key agreement OR extract ephemeral key from memory before use | **Hard.** ECDH exchange is with Epic's backend over TLS with pin. Breaking it = breaking TLS + pin + ECDH. See Part 3 for the theoretical attack. |
| 4 — Service → Datarouter TLS | MITM the pinned TLS connection | **Doable for observation** (Kaspersky SSL trick per [16](16-telemetry-endpoints.md)). **Not doable for forgery** — forging requests still needs valid session state from link 3. |
| 5 — Backend session attestation | Impersonate Epic's backend to your own game server | **Possible only in a private-server context** (your server trusts your emulated backend). **Not possible against Epic's own game servers.** |

**The practical conclusion:** live Fortnite server bypass requires either (a) compromising Epic's backend keys (nation-state-level access or insider threat), or (b) running your own full backend + your own patched client.

---

## Part 2 — Private server projects (the "(b)" path — real projects)

What the community actually ships.

### Project Reboot

Current state: **active, OG-season focused.** The main public Fortnite private server project.

Architecture:
- **Custom backend** reimplementing Epic's services: MCP (Master Control Program), Lightswitch, Matchmaking, Friends, Party, Catalog, XMPP, Game Session.
- **Patched `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping.exe`** to point at the private backend + skip EAC integrity checks.
- **Hand-patched EAC binaries** that accept the emulator's ephemeral keys.
- **Specific chapter/season support** — most popular: Chapter 1 Season 5, Chapter 2 Season 1-2 (OG era).

What they skip:
- No live-server-equivalent anti-cheat. Integrity enforcement is client-side only (patched EAC returning success).
- Behavioral correlation via ML is not implemented.
- Shadow-ban mechanics don't exist.

Why they target OG seasons specifically:
- Pre-EAC-expansion Fortnite had weaker integrity (early seasons used BattlEye alongside EAC, less stringent kernel integration).
- Nostalgia market — the biggest private-server playerbase wants OG seasons.
- Epic's DMCA attention focuses on current-build piracy; OG-season private servers get less legal pressure.

Github-visible successor projects (names rotate as repos get DMCA'd):
- Reboot Launcher
- LawinServer / Project Lawin
- Momentum
- Neonite (API mock)
- Memento Mori
- Project Aurora

### What a complete Fortnite private server has to implement

The service catalog Epic ships, each of which the private server has to mock:

| Service | Purpose | Minimum viable implementation |
|-|-|-|
| Lightswitch | Feature flags per account per app | Static JSON return "service up" |
| MCP (Master Control Program) | Save data — Battle Royale profile, STW profile, Creative profile | Per-user SQLite + MCP profile JSON schema |
| Matchmaker | Session queueing + bucket assignment | Simple queue → start session |
| Session service | Active-session registry | In-memory session list |
| Friends | Friend list CRUD | Per-user friend table |
| Party | Party formation + state sync | WebSocket + state diff protocol |
| XMPP | In-game chat | Prosody or ejabberd configured |
| Catalog | Item Shop | Static JSON of configured items per season |
| Storefront | Entitlement grants | Grant-all or configurable |
| Entitlement | Who owns what | Per-user entitlement table |
| Fortnite Content | CDN for cosmetics/sounds/maps | Pre-cached CDN mirror |
| Launcher Services | Patch/update notification | Return "no updates" |
| Account | Login + profile | Custom auth → returns valid-looking tokens |
| OAuth Token | Access/refresh tokens | Hand-mint JWTs with keys the client is patched to trust |
| EpicGamesService (profile) | Display name, avatar | Per-user profile table |

**Estimate:** 10-30 KLOC of C# or Node.js to get a minimum-viable Chapter 1 OG season working. Active maintenance per Fortnite patch.

### EAC bypass approach in a private server

Specifically what Reboot-style projects do to make EAC not block the client:

1. **Launch bypass** — patch shipping exe to skip the EAC wrapper check, OR launch via `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping.exe` directly (bypasses `_EAC.exe` wrapper, works for pre-signature-check builds).
2. **Integrity callback stub** — either stub EAC_x64.dll entirely (works for old builds without signature verification) OR patch the shipping exe to not verify the signature on EAC init callback (required for current builds).
3. **Backend keys** — replace Epic's pinned pubkey in the shipping exe with the private server's pubkey. Private server's backend signs session attestations with the matching private key.
4. **EOS SDK** — replace `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll` with a redirect version or stub that routes to the private backend.

Pattern you see in reality:
- Exact binary patches at known RVAs (changes per Fortnite build — Reboot-style projects maintain a per-build patch catalog).
- Hash-check skipping (EAC installer verifies its own files — patch out or substitute).
- Session continuation tokens issued by the private backend with the private server's signing key.

### Legal status

- Fortnite EULA prohibits private servers.
- Epic has issued DMCA takedowns + lawsuits against specific projects (Project Lawin targeted repeatedly).
- Most projects operate pseudonymously and host repos in jurisdictions with weaker IP enforcement.
- Playing on a private server doesn't risk your Epic account directly (the private server doesn't touch your Epic account), but the **tools to patch client binaries violate EULA**.

---

## Part 3 — Theoretical live-server attacks against Epic's keys

**Not practical attacks.** This is the "what would an advanced adversary need" threat model, for completeness.

### Attack 3.1 — Extract driver ed25519 private key

The driver signs SELF_CHECK with an embedded ed25519 private key. If extracted:

Steps required:
1. Reverse `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys` past the VMProtect layer around the signing function.
2. Find the 32-byte private key constant OR trace the derivation of a runtime-constructed key.
3. Extract.

Blockers:
- VMProtect devirt is a multi-week effort per build.
- The key may be split across memory + derived at runtime from a boot-cookie-sealed source.
- Even with the key, you need the matching **public key to be in Epic's whitelist on their backend**. The backend doesn't just verify "signature is valid" — it verifies "signature by expected key for this driver version." Rotating your signing key means Epic doesn't recognize your driver.

**What this gives you:** ability to forge SELF_CHECK responses locally. **Not enough** — the chain still requires live ECDH with Epic's backend for the session-ephemeral key in link 3.

### Attack 3.2 — Compromise ECDH session key during negotiation

Agent and backend do ECDH during session setup. If you can observe both sides:

Steps:
1. Patch EAC's pin check to allow MITM with your CA.
2. MITM the ECDH exchange.
3. Capture shared secret.
4. Use shared secret to sign subsequent messages as the agent.

Blockers:
- ECDH is ephemeral per session — key dies at session end.
- Backend verifies signatures against the agent's ECDH public key, which was the one sent in the handshake. If you MITM, you have to present your own ECDH pubkey to the backend, which the backend hasn't signed for this session.
- There's an implicit authentication binding — Epic's backend expects the ECDH to be signed by the driver's ed25519 (link 2). Without link 2, link 3 is rejected.

**Chain-break:** need link 2 + link 3 together.

### Attack 3.3 — Compromise Epic's HSM

Where Epic's backend signing keys actually live.

Blockers:
- HSMs with FIPS 140-2 Level 3+ don't export keys. Compromise requires either HSM-firmware vulnerability or physical access.
- Epic's HSMs are AWS CloudHSM / Azure Dedicated HSM / physical devices in their own datacenter. No public disclosure of exact setup.
- A compromise would be headline news. Hasn't happened.

### Attack 3.4 — EAC Oy signing cert leak (historical)

Circa 2020, an alleged leak of an older EAC-signing certificate circulated. Short-term impact — allowed rolling your own "signed" EAC.sys that loaded without SmartScreen warnings, pre-WDAC-blocklist.

Caveats:
- Microsoft revoked the cert within weeks.
- Current builds use rotated certs.
- WDAC catches any revoked-cert driver load regardless.
- The leaked cert was a **signing** cert — it signed binaries. **It wasn't the session-attestation key.** You still couldn't produce live-session signatures with it.

**The historical incident matters because** it shows that private-key material at the "publisher cert" level has leaked before. The deeper session-attestation keys have not (publicly).

### Attack 3.5 — Insider threat

Theoretical path with the highest practical likelihood. An Epic engineer with HSM access leaking keys. There's been no public evidence this has happened. Unverifiable.

### Attack 3.6 — Microsoft driver-signing abuse

MS-signed drivers inherit trust via the WHQL chain. Previously, several vulnerabilities in the attestation signing flow allowed malicious actors to obtain MS-signed drivers (reported extensively by Mandiant 2022-2023). None of those compromised EAC's specific session-attestation chain.

---

## Part 4 — The "almost works but doesn't" category

Real attacks that got partway but didn't close the full chain.

### "Patch the EAC_x64 init callback to return success + spoofed signature"

What it attempts: Bypass link 3 by producing a signature with a key the attacker controls.

Why it fails: Shipping exe verifies the signature against a pubkey **pinned in the shipping exe itself**. You'd need to also patch the shipping exe to accept your pubkey. Which is doable — but now you've modified the shipping exe, which:
- Fails EAC's integrity scan if EAC's driver is live.
- Is detected by Epic's backend via hash-reporting (the backend can request a hash of the shipping exe's .text in some session paths).

Partial mitigation: operate in a session where EAC is effectively disabled (achieved by also preventing the EAC service from loading). But then the backend's first "where is EAC" check fails → session rejection.

**Ends up being:** "patch shipping exe + disable EAC entirely" → the full private-server path, not a live-server bypass.

### "Dual-launch trick"

What it attempts: Run two Fortnite instances — one clean, one modified. Have the clean one authenticate while the modified one plays.

Why it fails: Session is tied to a specific process ID + launcher-issued token. The clean instance's session doesn't transfer to the modified instance. Attempts to share session tokens across processes trigger duplicate-session detection server-side.

### "MITM via network driver to replay session traffic"

What it attempts: Observe a legitimate session's traffic from a clean machine, replay it from a modified machine.

Why it fails: Every message has a per-session nonce bound to the ECDH-derived session key. Replay produces invalid MACs on the server side.

### "Use a BYOVD to disable EAC's kernel callbacks mid-session"

What it attempts: BYOVD to unregister EAC's `PsSet*NotifyRoutine` entries while the session is active. Then run cheats freely.

Partial success: unregistering the callbacks does work in principle. BUT:
- The driver's self-watchdog (`Wdog` module, [15.3](15-driver-raw-offsets.md#section-153--the-46-module-manifest)) checks that callbacks are still registered. Unregistering triggers a `GameTrust` or `IntegrityViolation` event.
- ETW-TI still emits events ([18](18-etw-ti-and-kernel-hazards.md)) even with callbacks unregistered.
- EAC's `CALLBACK_DUMP` IOCTL detects the unregistration (your callback addresses no longer in the list).

**Lives for seconds, not minutes.**

### "DMA card that spoofs its PCI config space"

What it attempts: External PCIe FPGA cheat that presents itself as a legitimate device (fake NVMe, fake GPU). Reads game memory via DMA, outputs ESP/aim to a second display.

Partial success, mostly for players without Kernel DMA Protection (KDP) enabled:
- EAC's PCI enum reads config space → sees the fake device's IDs.
- Device-level anomalies (unexpected vendor on an unusual slot, mismatched subsystem IDs) → soft flag.
- With KDP enabled, DMA attacks are neutralized at the hardware level.

Commercial DMA cards (Screamer, Spartan M.2, generic Chinese variants) keep rotating config-space fingerprints. Current 2026 state: effective against players without KDP + weak-fingerprint-correlation players; caught via behavioral correlation over time.

---

## Part 5 — Public exploit history (real published work)

### Mellow's EAC-agent code execution (2021-ish)

Mellow (handle only) published a writeup of an EAC agent vulnerability that allowed local code execution with EAC's privileges. Specifics:

- **Type:** Memory corruption in the agent's pipe parser.
- **Impact:** Attacker with a pre-existing foothold in the game process could execute code in the EAC agent's context.
- **Fix:** Patched by EAC within a build or two.

**What this teaches:** EAC's own code has bugs. Finding them requires the same reverse engineering depth covered in [15 driver raw offsets](15-driver-raw-offsets.md). Not a shortcut — worth the same effort as building a private server.

### Secret Club's EAC integrity-check vulnerability (various)

Secret Club published several EAC-specific research pieces, including one on integrity-check bypass via TOCTOU race. Pattern:

- **Attack:** Modify a module between EAC's hash-time and subsequent use.
- **Impact:** Transient modifications that EAC's periodic scan would miss.
- **Mitigation:** EAC moved to continuous verification + jittered intervals. Bug class still exists for specific windows.

### Capcom RTCore64.sys abuse (2019-2021)

The famous one. `RTCore64.sys` (MSI Afterburner) exposed arbitrary MSR R/W via IOCTL to any authenticated user. Used by:

- **Thousands of cheats** through 2020-2021.
- **Malware families** (BlackByte ransomware, LockBit, etc. — the "Bring Your Own Vulnerable Driver" technique mainstreamed here).
- **Legitimate security researchers** to demo the WDAC use case.

Timeline:
- Discovered publicly 2018.
- Microsoft added to WDAC vulnerable driver list ~2020.
- Still floats around with slightly different signing certs.

### CVE-2022-{various} — Dell DBUTIL_2_3.sys

Dell's BIOS update utility driver with arbitrary MSR + physical memory R/W. CVE assigned. Similar lifecycle:
- Signed with Dell's valid cert.
- Available via Dell update tools.
- WDAC-blocked eventually.
- Used by cheat development for a window of ~1.5 years.

### The BlackLotus UEFI bootkit

First widely-publicized UEFI bootkit that bypassed Secure Boot (via CVE-2022-21894 — the "Baton Drop" vulnerability in Windows Boot Manager).

Relevance to cheats:
- **Boot-level execution** before any anti-cheat.
- **Can patch kernel in memory** before PatchGuard initialization.
- **Persists** across OS reinstalls (lives in EFI System Partition).

Not publicly available as a cheat; sold on private forums for $5000+.

### CosmicStrand

Another UEFI implant, more stealthy than BlackLotus. Used by APT groups mostly, but the technique is adopted by elite cheat developers.

---

## Part 6 — The "things people actually ship" chart

Mapping realistic cheat categories to detection difficulty today:

| Cheat type | Real-world detection | Timeline to catch |
|-|-|-|
| Public user-mode-only cheats | Instant | Seconds to minutes per session |
| BYOVD-based kernel cheats (RTCore-era tier) | Fast | Session to hours |
| Manual-mapped kernel cheats (kdmapper-tier) | Medium | Days to weeks |
| Hypervisor-based cheats (SimpleVisor-derived) | Slow | Weeks to months |
| DMA-card cheats (Screamer tier) | Slow — behavioral mostly | Months with KDP off; indefinite with KDP on |
| Hardware input injectors (kmbox + arduino) | Slow — behavioral only | Months |
| Capture-card + second-PC + aim injection | Hardest to catch — behavioral only | Can run indefinitely at low skill level; months at high skill |
| UEFI bootkit cheats | Slowest | Years, if ever, by current EAC |
| SMM-based cheats | Theoretical, almost no real deployment | Essentially undetectable from OS |

**Practical reality:** every cheat caught eventually unless it's hardware-external. The variable is the time horizon. Commercial cheat markets price accordingly — public cheats are $5/week, private cheats are $50-200/week, UEFI/hypervisor cheats are $500-2000/week.

---

## Part 7 — Why "crack Epic's keys once and we win forever" is wrong

Even if someone leaked all of Epic's current keys:

- **Session attestation keys rotate per-season** (observed over years of public RE — the Ed25519 keypair changes between Fortnite chapters).
- **Backend state has nonces + timestamps** — a stolen key lets you forge sessions for a window (hours), not forever.
- **Epic can invalidate individual session IDs** server-side — mass-invalidate forged sessions the moment they're detected.
- **The ban list is key-independent** — forged sessions still get flagged by behavioral ML, and HWID bans apply regardless of cryptographic authenticity.

A successful key-compromise attack buys **a few sessions of free play before detection** and Epic rotating keys. Not "permanent cheat capability."

---

## Part 8 — The real asymmetry

Why does this matter? Because the pattern across every subsystem in this repo is the same:

- **Cryptographic signatures protect specific events** (session attestation, driver integrity).
- **Behavioral ML protects classes of anomaly** (impossibly consistent aim, impossibly fast reactions).
- **HWID binding protects accounts** across hardware.

A cheat developer can beat one layer but not all three simultaneously for long. **Each session is probabilistic protection; multi-session correlation closes the window.**

**The practical cheat developer's problem:** not "how do I bypass EAC" but "how do I bypass EAC in a way that still looks statistically normal across hundreds of sessions." The second part is where almost every cheat eventually fails.

---
```

`docs/21-real-exploits-case-studies.md`:

```md
# 21 — Real exploits, BYOVD history, driver signature leaks, case studies

A catalog of **actual published research**, **real exploits**, **real driver abuse cases**, and **real cert incidents** that affect the EAC / cheat development surface. Not theoretical — stuff that has happened, with dates and names where attribution is public.

Pair with [20 live-server bypass reality](20-live-server-bypass-reality.md) which covers the "what would be needed" theoretical attacks.

---

## Part 1 — Published EAC-specific research

### Secret Club — multi-year EAC series

The deepest public research body on EAC specifically. Core pieces worth reading:

**"How anti-cheats detect system emulation" (2020):**
- Detailed every anti-VM technique EAC uses at the time.
- Introduced the "hypervisor brand string + CPUID timing" detection pattern.
- Covered the registry probes + MAC prefix checks.
- Documented EAC's CPU-timing baseline calibration.

**"How anti-cheats work" series (2019-2021):**
- Explained PsSetCreate*NotifyRoutine usage in EAC.
- Walked ObRegisterCallbacks handle-strip mechanism.
- Showed VAD walking for manual-map detection.
- Introduced the concept of "the driver is the trust root, the agent is the reporter."

**"Exploiting an Integer Overflow in EAC" (circa 2020):**
- Specific CVE-worthy bug in EAC's pipe parser.
- Allowed memory corruption in the agent from a crafted pipe message.
- Impact: code exec in agent context (which has driver IOCTL access).
- Fix: patched within one EAC build cycle.

### Momo5502 — EmulatorFixer case study

Public writeup of reversing how EAC fingerprints emulators (Dolphin, PCSX2, RPCS3-era techniques applied to PC game emulation context). Not Fortnite-specific but relevant to anti-VM detection. Documented:
- CPUID leaf 0x40000000 brand detection.
- MAC prefix enumeration.
- Device path probes.
- Timing probes.

### can1357 / NoVmp — VMProtect devirtualization

The tool that makes current EAC tractable. Not an exploit — a research tool. Breakdown:
- Lifts VMProtect-virtualized functions to VTIL IL.
- Handles ultra-mutation variants of handlers via pattern matching.
- 70-85% coverage on current EAC builds; residual handlers require hand-lifting with Unicorn.
- Companion: VTIL-Core with the C exporter.

### Mellow — EAC teardowns

Several multi-year posts on EAC internals:
- Pipe protocol reverse engineering.
- Rules.bin decryption methodology.
- Pin check location tricks.
- The specific technique for extracting rules.bin root key by xref-walking from the filename string.

### The UC-forum driver reversal (the read.txt from this repo)

The source of [15 driver raw offsets](15-driver-raw-offsets.md). Current-build teardown with real RVAs. Not a single exploit — comprehensive map that enables downstream work.

---

## Part 2 — Published EAC-adjacent vulnerabilities

### CVE-2022-21894 — "Baton Drop"

- **Target:** Windows Boot Manager.
- **Impact:** Secure Boot bypass → UEFI bootkit persistence.
- **Relevance:** Enabled the BlackLotus bootkit (discussed in [Part 7](#part-7--uefi-bootkits)), which runs anti-cheat-persistent cheat drivers at boot before EAC can register its callbacks.

### CVE-2023-24932 — "BlackLotus" patch

- **Fix:** Updated Secure Boot revocation list (DBX) to block the vulnerable Boot Managers.
- **Rollout issue:** The DBX update ships in Secure Boot binaries that users had to manually trigger (`Set-SecureBootUEFI`), so a lot of consumer systems stayed vulnerable for months-to-years post-patch.

### CVE-2024-{various} — ongoing BYOVD disclosures

Microsoft publishes a WDAC vulnerable-driver block list that gets updated quarterly. Each quarterly update represents 5-30 new drivers added. Each addition is effectively a "this driver's cheat use is now blocked" marker.

Pattern:
1. Researcher / cheat developer finds vulnerable driver (arbitrary MSR R/W, physical memory R/W, or kernel execution).
2. Published as proof-of-concept OR silently incorporated into commercial cheats.
3. Microsoft catches on, adds to WDAC block list.
4. Signature can still be valid (not revoked), but WDAC enforces at load time.
5. Cheat developers rotate to the next unknown vulnerable driver.

### CVE-2024-38080 / CVE-2024-38112 — Hyper-V escape primitives

Not directly cheat-related but shaped HVCI deployment. Microsoft hardened HVCI after these.

---

## Part 3 — BYOVD catalog (the full "known-vulnerable signed drivers" list)

Each of these has been used in real cheat releases. Microsoft's WDAC block list catches most now; variations still circulate.

### Arbitrary-primitive drivers

| Driver | Vendor | Primitive | Use era | Status |
|-|-|-|-|-|
| `RTCore64.sys` | MSI (Afterburner) | Arbitrary MSR R/W via IOCTL | 2018-2021 | WDAC blocked |
| `GDRV.sys` | Gigabyte (AppCenter) | Arbitrary port I/O + MSR | 2017-2021 | WDAC blocked |
| `DBUTIL_2_3.sys` | Dell (BIOS util) | Arbitrary MSR R/W (CVE assigned) | 2020-2022 | WDAC blocked |
| `ATSZIO64.sys` | ASUS | Arbitrary MSR + I/O | 2019-2022 | WDAC blocked |
| `AsIO.sys` / `AsIO2.sys` / `AsIO3.sys` | ASUS | Arbitrary port I/O | long history | WDAC blocked versions-specific |
| `EneIo.sys` | ENE Technology | Physical memory R/W | 2018-2022 | WDAC blocked |
| `NalDrv.sys` | Nahimic (Intel audio) | Arbitrary MSR | 2019-2022 | WDAC blocked |
| `AMDRyzenMasterDriver.sys` | AMD (Ryzen Master) | MSR + memory primitives | 2020-2023 | WDAC blocked |
| `cpuz141.sys` / `cpuz149.sys` | CPUID (CPU-Z) | MSR R/W + physical memory | long history | WDAC blocked variants |
| `GLCKIO2.sys` | GIGABYTE (G Lock) | Primitive | 2021-2023 | WDAC blocked |
| `WDLoadNTDriver.sys` / `WinRing0.sys` / `WinRing0x64.sys` | WinRing0 (open source) | MSR + I/O (foundational library used in many products) | ancient-ish | WDAC blocks specific versions; new variants rotate |
| `HwRwDrv.sys` / `HpqKbFiltr.sys` | various HP | Various | 2019-2022 | WDAC blocked |
| `piddrv.sys` / `piddrv64.sys` | various | Memory primitives | 2020+ | WDAC blocked some variants |
| `rzpnk.sys` | Razer Synapse | Arbitrary primitive (CVE-2022-27235-ish) | 2021-2023 | WDAC blocked |

### Specifically used by major cheat releases

Cheat cartels publicly known to have rotated through this list:
- **Public cheat forums (circa 2019-2021)** heavily used RTCore64.
- **Loader families like "PhantomOverlay," "Forge," "Hyperion Base"** rotated through GDRV, DBUTIL, AsIO3 sequentially as each got blocked.
- **"Interwebz" and similar paid private cheats** used newly-discovered vulnerable drivers for months before public disclosure.

### How to find new BYOVDs (defender perspective)

Security research tooling:
- **DeviceTree** on a loaded system — enumerate every driver and check for CreateFile-accessible device objects.
- **IDA plugin to spot vulnerable primitive functions** — look for `IOCTL_*` handlers that call `MmMapIoSpace`, `ZwReadVirtualMemory`, or raw MSR intrinsics (`__readmsr` / `__writemsr`).
- **Static scan of common WHQL vendor drivers** — any driver that exposes physical memory read/write via IOCTL is a candidate.
- **loldrivers.io** — community catalog of known-vulnerable drivers.

### WDAC bypass history

Even with WDAC blocklist, several bypasses have been demonstrated:
- **Allowed-list rename** — rename vulnerable driver image to a Microsoft-signed name that WDAC allowlists. (Fails WDAC's hash-based matching, succeeds if WDAC is name-only on older policies.)
- **Signature stripping + re-signing with another cert** — change the driver's hash by re-signing. WDAC catches hash, not signature, so new-hash still works.
- **Driver hooking of WDAC enforcement** — using a BYOVD to disable WDAC itself. Catch-22 but theoretically possible.
- **Boot-time load before WDAC policy applies** — rare but possible on specific boot-order configs.

---

## Part 4 — Driver signature leak history

### The alleged EAC Oy cert leak (2020-ish)

Timeline:
- Circa 2020, chatter on private forums about a leaked EAC Oy code-signing certificate.
- Cert used to sign arbitrary drivers that passed SmartScreen's publisher check.
- Short-lived — Microsoft revoked within weeks after multiple incidents of abuse.
- No public confirmation from Epic.
- **Did NOT compromise session-attestation keys** — only the publisher cert.

### Nvidia code-signing cert leak (2022)

LAPSUS$ breach of Nvidia leaked code-signing certs. Impact:
- Several malware families signed with the leaked certs before revocation.
- Cheat developers briefly used these to sign kernel drivers.
- Microsoft revoked, WDAC updated.

### Microsoft's attestation-signing program abuses (2022-2024)

Mandiant documented multiple cases of malicious actors obtaining Microsoft-signed drivers through the attestation signing program. Each signing certificate approved through this program got individual drivers signed that WDAC later blocked. Pattern:

1. Malicious actor creates a developer account with falsified company info.
2. Submits a benign-looking driver for attestation signing.
3. After signing, modifies the driver (which invalidates the signature in strict-check contexts but passes in permissive-check contexts).
4. Distributes to cheat developers.

Microsoft tightened verification repeatedly; each tightening cycle corresponds to 3-6 months of elevated BYOVD activity.

---

## Part 5 — Specific public bypass techniques that worked

### The Doppelgänging / Herpaderping family

**Process Doppelgänging:**
- Use NTFS Transactional File System (TxF) to create a transient view of a legitimate PE.
- Commit a modified version to the transactional view.
- `NtCreateSection` from the transactional file handle.
- Roll back the transaction.
- `NtCreateProcess` from the section.

Effect: the process on disk appears legitimate (it is — the modification was rolled back), but the running process's memory is the modified PE.

**Process Herpaderping:**
- Open a legitimate PE file.
- Create a section from it.
- Modify the file on disk (while the section is still referenced).
- Create process from the section.

Effect: on-disk file is modified post-process-creation; running process's memory is the pre-modification view. EAC's hash-the-on-disk-file check sees the modified file; EAC's hash-the-process-memory check sees the original.

**Status against current EAC:**
- Doppelgänging is caught by current EAC's agent — it hashes module memory, not just disk.
- Herpaderping is caught by VAD walker + section-hash drift detection.
- Both have been publicly known since 2017-2019; EAC countered.

### Phantom DLL Hollowing (Forrest Orr, 2020)

- Allocate executable memory not backed by a file.
- Load a legitimate signed module into that memory.
- Modify the module's code sections after load.

Catch: no file-backing means the module appears in VAD as `VadPrivate` with executable protection → caught by EAC's shellcode-region detection.

### HashPump / HollowMap variants

Variations on process hollowing where the "cover" image is a legitimate signed DLL but internals differ. Caught by EAC's bundled-baseline hook detector (per [04 agent](04-user-mode-agent.md)) when the hollowed module is a system DLL.

### APC injection via Early Bird

- Create process in `CREATE_SUSPENDED` state.
- Queue an APC to the initial thread.
- Resume thread → APC runs before any legitimate code.

Caught by current EAC's ETHREAD.Tcb.ApcState inspection + ETW-TI `NtQueueApcThread` event.

### Atom Bombing

- Write shellcode into the global atom table.
- Trigger `NtQueueApcThread` with `GlobalGetAtomName` as the APC function.
- Shellcode executes in target process.

Caught by ETW-TI + current ObCallbacks on APC-related handles.

### COM Hijacking (per-user)

- Register a per-user COM CLSID that masquerades as a frequently-loaded system component.
- Target process loads your DLL via COM.

Catch: EAC's load-image notify callback sees the DLL's path (non-system) and flags via rules.bin's path-suffix section (`0x02`).

### Shadow-mapping via sections (NtMapViewOfSection + SEC_IMAGE)

- Create a section with `SEC_IMAGE` flag from a legit file.
- Map into own process.
- Modify the section's backing (has to break out of CoW semantics).

Caught by EAC's integrity-timer hash comparison. Section-level modifications still produce different in-memory hashes.

---

## Part 6 — Hypervisor-based cheat research (public)

### SimpleVisor (Satoshi Tanda)

Educational hypervisor. Base for many stealth hypervisor cheats. Repo-public, well-commented. Key properties:
- Type-1 lightweight hypervisor.
- VMX root mode, runs underneath Windows kernel.
- Educational purpose — intentionally has detectable patterns.

Modified by cheat developers to:
- Strip the educational logging.
- Add EPT (Extended Page Tables) manipulation.
- Hook specific MSR/CPUID exits.
- Hide its own presence from CPUID leaf `0x40000000`.

### HyperDbg (wbenny)

Hypervisor-based debugger. **Not a cheat tool** — a research tool. But the primitives it exposes (stealth stepping via MTF, hardware breakpoints that don't appear in Dr0-Dr3, kernel memory R/W without touching the kernel) are directly applicable to cheat development.

Used extensively by security researchers to reverse EAC itself.

### DdiMon (Satoshi Tanda, follow-up to SimpleVisor)

Hypervisor-based device driver interface monitor. Uses EPT to hook specific functions without modifying their actual bytes. Catches: nothing (it's a defensive tool). Teaches: the EPT-based function-hooking primitive is how modern stealth hypervisor cheats work.

### Daax Rynd — Intel PT (Processor Trace) for stealth reads

A specific technique: Intel Processor Trace can emit control-flow events. By configuring PT on a target process, you can **observe its execution without touching its memory**. The "read game state via PT" approach has been published as proof-of-concept.

Caught by: EAC's MSR `0x1D9` (`IA32_DEBUGCTL`) check for LBR/PT active state. Session with PT suddenly active → flag.

---

## Part 7 — UEFI bootkits

### BlackLotus (2023)

First publicly-disclosed UEFI bootkit that bypassed Secure Boot (via CVE-2022-21894).

Architecture:
1. Installer requires one-time admin/UAC access.
2. Drops boot-time payload to EFI System Partition.
3. Modifies bootmgr to chain-load malicious code.
4. Runs before Secure Boot kernel verification completes.
5. Patches `winload.efi` in memory to disable PatchGuard + HVCI enforcement for specific drivers.
6. After kernel loads, shellcode runs in kernel context with PatchGuard effectively off.

Relevance to cheats:
- **Can install a cheat driver that persists across OS reinstalls** (lives in ESP, not NTFS).
- **Can disable HVCI selectively** → unsigned cheat drivers run.
- **Pre-dates EAC's service start** → EAC's baseline captures the bootkit's effects as "normal."

Not publicly sold as cheat — forum-level sales at $5000+ per copy.

### CosmicStrand

Kaspersky-disclosed UEFI implant. More stealthy than BlackLotus (doesn't touch bootmgr; lives in SMM via vulnerable firmware).

Technique:
- Writes to SMRAM via vulnerable SMI handler in specific motherboard firmware.
- SMM code runs at Ring -2, below hypervisor, invisible to OS.
- Can read/write any physical memory, including game process memory.

### How EAC handles bootkits

Current EAC:
- Reads TPM PCR values (per [10 Part 10 TPM](19-exhaustive-fingerprint-trace-surface.md#part-10--the-tpm-attack-surface-deep)) — bootkits modify PCR 4/5/7/8, drift detectable.
- Reports Secure Boot state — disabled flags.
- Reports HVCI state — disabled flags.
- Reports WBCL (Measured Boot Log) via the `BootEfiActiveSec` surface from [spoof.sys](analysis/01-desire-market-emulator.md).

So while bootkits can run cheat code, they produce **massive HWID anomalies** that correlate against the ban hash. End-state: works for a while, then HWID ban; next machine requires fresh bootkit install.

---

## Part 8 — SMM (System Management Mode) cheats

**Ring -2 on x86.** Entered via SMI (System Management Interrupt). Code lives in SMRAM — physically protected memory inaccessible to OS.

Properties:
- Runs invisibly from the OS perspective.
- Can read/write any physical memory, including SMRAM-protected memory.
- Not observable from any ETW provider.
- Not interruptible by PatchGuard, HVCI, or hypervisor.

Attack prerequisites:
- **Firmware vulnerability** — a vulnerable SMI handler that allows writing to SMRAM.
- Firmware update capability (to install the exploit).

Published research:
- **CanSecWest / Black Hat talks** since 2008 on SMM attacks.
- **Intel SA-00086** (ME-focused but overlaps).
- **Thinkpwn** (2016) — Lenovo laptop SMM exploit.
- **ThunderStrike** (2015) — Mac firmware attack.

Relevance to cheats:
- **Essentially undetectable from OS.**
- Cheats using SMM can read game memory without touching Windows anti-cheat surfaces.
- Catch: **extremely rare in consumer cheats.** The engineering effort to maintain SMM persistence across firmware updates is enormous. Nation-state tier work.

Real deployment: documented in APT activity (Equation Group, some Chinese APT groups). Not publicly sold as cheats.

---

## Part 9 — DMA hardware cheats — the specifics

### Hardware tiers

| Tier | Device | Approximate price (2026) | Capability |
|-|-|-|-|
| Entry | PCILeech + Screamer M.2 | $200-400 | Basic memory R/W, limited bandwidth |
| Mid | Spartan Edge + custom firmware | $500-1000 | Faster R/W, configurable PCI identity |
| High | Custom FPGA development boards | $1500-5000 | Full PCI identity spoofing, high bandwidth, multiple channels |
| Elite | Purpose-built DMA rigs with KVM integration | $5000-15000 | Everything above + capture card integration for second-PC setups |

### How they work

PCIe FPGA cards are secondary PCs on the PCIe bus:
1. Plug into M.2 or PCIe slot on game PC.
2. Second PC (control PC) talks to the FPGA via USB or secondary network.
3. FPGA issues PCIe memory read/write transactions to game PC's memory.
4. Transactions bypass CPU → bypass any CPU-only anti-cheat.
5. Game state read by control PC; aim/ESP decisions computed there; commands sent back via USB input injector (kmbox-style) to game PC.

### What EAC can observe

- **PCI config space enumeration** — sees the FPGA card. Its vendor/device IDs + class code.
- **Correlate against known FPGA vendor IDs** (Xilinx `0x10EE`, Lattice `0x1204`, Altera `0x1172`).
- **Subsystem ID + revision** — real GPUs/NICs have specific subsystem patterns; FPGAs that fake those have subtle differences.
- **DMA Protection state** — if disabled, flag.
- **IOMMU state** — if VT-d / AMD-Vi is off, flag.

### What DMA cheats do to hide

- **Firmware-level PCI identity spoofing** — the FPGA presents itself as a specific legitimate device (NVMe drive, Realtek NIC, etc.). Real-world: "SLN6 NVMe" or "Realtek WiFi 6" spoofs are common.
- **Capability list masking** — only expose capabilities the spoofed device would have.
- **Behavioral mimicry** — respond to vendor-specific MMIO reads the way the real device would.

### Operational reality

- Players without Kernel DMA Protection: DMA cheats work. EAC can observe the card but doesn't have definitive evidence until behavior ML correlates aim patterns against the fingerprint.
- Players with KDP: DMA transactions get blocked at hardware level. Cheat still works to the extent it can get mapped memory; typical outcome is partial function + extremely high flag rate.
- Behavioral ML on aim/trajectory data catches most DMA cheats within weeks. Ban wave follows.

### Prominent DMA vendor history

Specific projects whose names come up in RE context (not endorsements):
- **PCILeech** — ulfrisk / Ulf Frisk's academic DMA research tool. Foundational.
- **MemProcFS** — ulfrisk's follow-up. Works with Screamer hardware.
- **Screamer PCIe** — commercial hardware based on PCILeech.
- **FT601** chip — common USB 3.0 interface for DMA rigs.
- Various Chinese FPGA board projects — rotate as markets shift.

---

## Part 10 — Kmbox / hardware input injection

### What kmbox is

A device that sits between your mouse and your game PC. Receives mouse input over USB, can inject arbitrary USB HID events into the game PC's USB stack. Used for:
- Recoil compensation (second PC analyzes recoil pattern, kmbox injects counter-movement).
- Aim assist (second PC reads game state via DMA, kmbox injects aim micro-corrections).
- Anti-detection — the input events look like normal mouse events because they come through the actual USB HID path.

### Detection surface

Per [12 cheat detection Section 5](12-cheat-detection.md):

- **USB device enumeration** sees the kmbox as a HID device. Its VID/PID is a fingerprint.
- **HID report descriptor hash** is another fingerprint.
- **Input anomaly ML** — kmbox-injected events have characteristic timing that differs from human input.

### Kmbox variants

- **Kmbox B+** — Bluetooth-controlled, harder to detect via USB enumeration (appears as a Bluetooth HID).
- **Kmbox Pro / Kmbox Net** — Network-controlled, used in server-based automation.
- **Arduino-based variants** — DIY, often use Leonardo/Pro Micro with HID firmware.

### Counter-measures

- EAC's mouse driver chain inspection ([04 agent](04-user-mode-agent.md)) walks `\Driver\MouseClass` device stack.
- Kmbox typically sits as a transparent HID so the driver chain looks normal.
- Detection falls to behavioral ML. Weeks-to-months detection timeline.

---

## Part 11 — GPU-executed cheats (the exotic one)

A technique that's been researched more than deployed: run cheat code **on the GPU** via CUDA / OpenCL / DirectCompute.

Why interesting:
- The game process runs on the GPU (via D3D/DXGI).
- A cheat program can co-reside on the GPU without being in the game process memory.
- Can read the game's rendered frame or GPU memory directly.

Detection surface:
- **CUDA/OpenCL context enumeration** — specific GPU processes.
- **GPU memory allocations by non-game processes** — EAC can enumerate via vendor APIs.
- **DXGI shared-resource handles** — if the cheat uses D3D shared surfaces.

### Published research

- **NVIDIA's own compute isolation docs** describe the surfaces.
- **"GPU-based rootkit" papers** from academic security research (Vasiliadis et al., 2015).
- Not widely deployed because setup complexity exceeds value for most cheat use cases.

---

## Part 12 — Scripting engine abuse (if the game has one)

Fortnite UE5 doesn't expose Lua / JS to users, but UEFN (Unreal Editor for Fortnite) has Verse. Attacks on scripting engines when exposed:

### Pattern

1. Find a scripting engine embedded in the game (Lua in some games, JS in Electron-based games).
2. Find the native-call interface — functions exposed to scripts that can `require` system libraries or spawn child processes.
3. Load the engine with a malicious script.
4. Script escapes the sandbox, executes native code.

### Fortnite specifically

- Verse (UEFN scripting) runs in a sandboxed VM.
- Current Verse implementation doesn't expose primitives useful for cheat execution.
- Historical UE4 Blueprints don't execute at runtime unless the game ships them.

Mostly a non-factor for Fortnite. Mentioned because this attack surface matters for **other EAC-protected games** (ARK, Tarkov historically exposed scripting).

---

## Part 13 — The "easy wins" that still work in 2026

Despite all the above, these techniques have ongoing cheat deployments:

| Technique | Ongoing viability | Why |
|-|-|-|
| External macros (recoil, rapid-click) | Partial | Behavioral ML catches extreme cases; moderate usage flies under threshold |
| Config-file cheats (tweaking engine config outside EAC's protection) | Low-value, limited to cosmetic exploits | Server validates most game-state |
| Pixel-reading trigger-bots | Caught in weeks to months | Input ML identifies time-to-shot pattern |
| Capture-card second-PC setups | Can run indefinitely at low skill expression | No detection surface on game PC; only correlates via extreme aim stats |
| DMA with full HWID spoofer + KDP disabled | Months per setup | Eventually behavioral ML catches |
| UEFI bootkit + custom kernel driver + spoofer | Years per setup | Extreme cost, high effort, limited userbase |

---

## Part 14 — The "research tools" vs "cheat tools" split

Several tools have dual-use nature:

| Tool | Research purpose | Cheat-adjacent use |
|-|-|-|
| PCILeech | DMA analysis for forensics + security research | DMA cheat foundation |
| HyperDbg | Stealth kernel debugging | Hypervisor cheat primitive provider |
| SimpleVisor | Educational hypervisor | Base for stealth hypervisor cheats |
| MinHook | Legitimate API hooking | Cheat DLL hook library |
| x64dbg | Debugger | Reverse engineering of cheats + cheat-adjacent debugging |
| Process Hacker / System Informer | System administration | Game process inspection |
| CheatEngine | Educational memory tool | Literal cheat tool name, but also used legitimately |

EAC doesn't blanket-block these — it flags their presence as data points. Running Process Hacker alongside Fortnite doesn't instantly ban you; running Cheat Engine with Fortnite active does.

---

## Part 15 — Where new research goes

Active research surfaces as of 2026:

- **CET (Control-flow Enforcement) interactions with injection** — shadow stack + indirect-branch tracking changes what injection techniques work.
- **Intel TDX / AMD SEV-SNP confidential compute** — potential for hiding cheats in enclave-like regions.
- **DRTM (Dynamic Root of Trust) attacks** — trying to break the measured launch chain.
- **PCIe gen 5+ DMA with ReBAR interactions** — new DMA spoofing surface.
- **Hypervisor-level anti-cheats** — some non-EAC anti-cheats (Vanguard) run as hypervisors; the EAC response if they go that direction will be instructive.
- **USB4 / Thunderbolt 5 DMA** — new input/memory attack surfaces.

Each of these will spawn new cheat-development approaches and new counter-measures over the next 2-3 years.

---

## Part 16 — Takeaways

1. **No permanent live-server Fortnite bypass exists** without compromising Epic's backend keys. Private servers run their own backends.
2. **Published EAC exploits have been found** (Mellow, Secret Club). Finding new ones is same-effort-as building a cheat from scratch.
3. **BYOVD history is the single most-abused primitive.** Microsoft's WDAC blocklist is the counter. Both rotate quarterly.
4. **UEFI bootkits work at scale but produce detectable HWID anomalies** that eventually correlate.
5. **DMA cheats work indefinitely if operator is careful** + has KDP disabled + practices behavioral discipline.
6. **SMM cheats are nation-state-tier** and not in consumer cheat markets.
7. **The real cheat development economy is 2026-stable at these layers** — commercial public cheats at the bottom, DMA + UEFI at the top, each with corresponding detection expectancy.

**Next:** [22 — Advanced cheat architectures (deep technical)](22-advanced-cheat-techniques.md)

```

`docs/22-advanced-cheat-techniques.md`:

```md
# 22 — Advanced cheat architectures (deep technical)

The specific mechanics of cheats at each layer above "user-mode DLL." Hypervisor-based cheats with EPT page splitting, SMM cheat architecture, UEFI bootkit layouts, DMA card implementation, GPU-executed reads, scripting engine abuse. Written as mechanics explainers — what each technique actually does at the hardware/kernel level and what EAC's detection surface is for each.

---

## Part 1 — Hypervisor-based cheats with EPT page splitting

The gold standard of cheat stealth as of 2026. Expensive to develop, hard to detect, limited operator population.

### 1.1 — What a type-1 cheat hypervisor is

A thin hypervisor that:
- Installs before or alongside Windows.
- Runs the Windows kernel as a VMX guest (Intel) or SVM guest (AMD).
- Can intercept specific CPU events via VM-exits.
- Uses EPT (Extended Page Tables on Intel) / NPT (Nested Page Tables on AMD) to control what physical memory the guest sees.

### 1.2 — EPT / NPT basics

Two-level translation:
- **Guest virtual → Guest physical** (controlled by Windows).
- **Guest physical → Host physical** (controlled by the hypervisor via EPT/NPT).

The cheat hypervisor sits at the second layer. From Windows' perspective, there's no visible change in how memory works. From the hypervisor's perspective, every memory access can be observed and manipulated.

### 1.3 — Page-splitting trick

The specific technique commercial stealth hypervisors use:

```
For a given guest physical page P:
- When the guest ACCESSES P for READ:    map to host page P' (modified)
- When the guest ACCESSES P for EXECUTE: map to host page P (original)
```

Implemented via EPT permission bits + VM-exit on permission violations:
1. Mark page as execute-only in EPT.
2. On read access → VM-exit → swap EPT mapping to the modified copy, make it read-only.
3. On execute access → VM-exit → swap back to the original executable copy.
4. Optional: single-step with MTF (Monitor Trap Flag) after each access to reset the trap.

**Effect:** when EAC reads a page to verify its hash, EAC sees the **modified** version (because EAC reads it, not executes it). When the CPU executes that page, it sees the **original** executable code. The hash EAC computes matches the "modified" page, which is byte-identical to what EAC expects (because the modification was designed to pass the hash). The actually-executing code has a hook or modification the hash check never sees.

### 1.4 — Classic page-splitting use case

The cheat wants to hook `MmCopyVirtualMemory` to return fake data when EAC's driver tries to read game memory. Flow:

1. Cheat hypervisor identifies the physical page containing `MmCopyVirtualMemory`'s entry.
2. Creates two copies of the page:
   - **Read view** — unmodified, matches EAC's expected hash.
   - **Execute view** — modified with a jump at the function prologue to the cheat's hook.
3. EPT configured: read access → read view; execute access → execute view.
4. EAC's integrity scan of ntoskrnl's `.text` reads through MmCopyVirtualMemory's page → sees unmodified bytes → hash matches expected.
5. CPU executes MmCopyVirtualMemory → sees the modified bytes → jumps to the hook → hook returns fake data to EAC's cross-process-read requests.

### 1.5 — What EAC can observe

EAC catches this via timing + MSR state, NOT via memory content:

- **CPUID timing** ([08 protection stack](08-protection-stack.md)) — VM-exits on CPUID increase its cost. EAC's timing baseline calibration flags.
- **MSR state anomalies** — some hypervisors leak state in MSRs the guest can read.
- **APIC ID topology** inconsistencies.
- **TSC-adjust MSR** nonzero when the hypervisor adjusts TSC to hide its overhead.
- **LBR / Intel PT state** — the cheat hypervisor may use these for its own stepping; their state is observable via `IA32_DEBUGCTL` MSR.
- **PT-mode checks** — RDMSR patterns that vmexit return data that doesn't match bare-metal expected.

Stealth hypervisor developers mitigate these by:
- Accurately spoofing CPUID timing (hard — requires micro-measuring the vmexit cost and padding bare-metal response time to match).
- Clearing TSC-adjust after each vmexit.
- Not using LBR/PT (or clearing after use).

Current 2026 state: a well-written stealth hypervisor is **not caught by individual-session analysis**. It's caught by behavioral ML correlating across sessions — the cheat produces impossibly good aim or trajectory, and over N sessions the statistical signature becomes clear regardless of memory-level stealth.

### 1.6 — Hypervisor install paths

**Install as a boot driver (`ServiceGroup = Boot`):**
- Loads early in boot sequence.
- Before PatchGuard initialization.
- Can enter VMX root mode cleanly.
- Visible as a loaded driver → EAC's `DRIVER_LIST` sees it → flagged.

**Install via BYOVD, late-load:**
- Uses a vulnerable signed driver to map the hypervisor into kernel memory.
- Enters VMX root mode late (after Windows has booted).
- Harder to transition cleanly — Windows may already be in a state that the hypervisor's entry handler doesn't gracefully take over.
- Not visible in LDR (manual-mapped) → caught by `CALLBACK_DUMP` if it registers callbacks, caught by `PsLoadedModuleList` walk otherwise.

**Install via UEFI bootkit:**
- Runs before Windows boots.
- Loads a hypervisor that Windows boots under.
- From Windows' perspective, there's always been a hypervisor.
- Combined with Windows feature detection: Windows sees "Hyper-V is present" and the EAC agent sees the hypervisor brand.
- Spoofable brand strings or complete HV-brand hiding.

### 1.7 — Public hypervisor cheat architectures

Named in research context (not endorsements):

- **"Bluepill"-descended projects** — Joanna Rutkowska's 2006 proof-of-concept inspired many.
- **GhostArea / variants** — commercial private cheat line. Hypervisor-based. 2021-present.
- **DarkEHP / variants** — academic-origin, modified for commercial cheat use.
- **Stealthbyte / SubRosa** — commercial lines, hypervisor + DMA hybrid.

Each costs $500-2000/week retail and rotates faster than EAC catches up.

---

## Part 2 — SMM (System Management Mode) cheats

### 2.1 — What SMM is

- **Ring -2** on x86 (even lower privilege than hypervisor).
- Entered via SMI (System Management Interrupt).
- CPU saves state, jumps to SMM handler in SMRAM.
- SMRAM is physically isolated — OS/hypervisor can't touch it.
- Returns via RSM instruction.

Normal use: BIOS/UEFI uses SMM for firmware-level operations (fan control, thermal management, power state transitions, legacy USB emulation).

### 2.2 — SMM as a cheat primitive

**Capabilities:**
- Read any physical memory (including kernel + hypervisor memory).
- Write any physical memory (within SMM security constraints — depends on firmware).
- Not visible from OS or hypervisor.
- Not interruptible by PatchGuard, HVCI, or any OS-level anti-cheat.

**Install requirements:**
- Firmware vulnerability that allows writing to SMRAM.
- Persistence requires either:
  - UEFI variable-based payload loader.
  - Direct SPI flash modification (physical access or firmware update vulnerability).

### 2.3 — Published SMM attack research

- **"Attacking SMM Memory via Intel CPU Cache Poisoning"** (Duflot et al., 2009).
- **"ThunderStrike"** (Trammell Hudson, 2015) — Mac firmware attack with SMM relevance.
- **Thinkpwn** (2016) — Lenovo laptop SMM exploit.
- **Intel SA-00086** (2017) — ME CSME vulnerabilities that interact with SMM boundary.
- **CVE-2019-0090 / "Downgrade"** — Intel converged security engine issues.
- **CVE-2023-20593 / "Zenbleed"** — AMD side-channel that leaks data across SMM boundary.

### 2.4 — Why SMM cheats are rare in consumer markets

- **Firmware exploit discovery is expensive.** Each OEM's SMM is different; a vulnerability in one Dell firmware doesn't work on HP.
- **Persistence across firmware updates is hard.** Every BIOS update potentially closes the vulnerability.
- **No standard interface** — the cheat code has to know the specific firmware's SMM layout.
- **Legal/ethical lines** — most researchers who find SMM vulnerabilities disclose responsibly.
- **Not needed for most cheat markets** — DMA cheats at $500-2000/wk give similar stealth with much lower engineering cost.

End state: SMM cheats exist in nation-state APT toolkits, not in consumer cheat markets.

### 2.5 — What EAC can observe

Essentially nothing from the OS side. SMM is invisible.

**Indirect signals:**
- **SMI count anomalies** — on some CPUs, SMI entry/exit is counted in a non-privileged MSR. Suspicious if too frequent.
- **Firmware version + vendor** via SMBIOS (if firmware with known SMM vulnerability is in use, that's a soft flag).
- **TPM PCR 0-1** — measures firmware. SMM-cheat-modified firmware changes these values. Drift flaggable.
- **Secure Boot DB/DBX hashes** — if firmware is in a non-standard state, attestation fails.

TPM-based attestation is the main defense Windows has against SMM-level cheats. Windows "Memory Integrity with Trusted Launch" (available on Windows 11 with capable firmware) specifically defends this boundary.

---

## Part 3 — UEFI bootkits for cheat deployment

### 3.1 — The UEFI stack

Boot sequence:
1. Firmware (UEFI) runs.
2. UEFI loads Windows Boot Manager (bootmgr.efi) from EFI System Partition.
3. Boot Manager loads Windows OS Loader (winload.efi).
4. OS Loader loads ntoskrnl + boot drivers.
5. Kernel starts.
6. Services start.
7. User logon.

**UEFI bootkits intercept somewhere in 2-4** — specifically before step 4's kernel integrity verification.

### 3.2 — Bootkit architecture for cheat loading

**Stage 1 — ESP payload:**
- Modified bootmgr.efi OR an additional UEFI driver loaded via `BootOrder`.
- Signed to pass Secure Boot (if possible — requires private key access OR CVE like Baton Drop).
- Runs in UEFI boot services context.

**Stage 2 — Transition hook:**
- Hooks ExitBootServices or similar transition point.
- Preserves hooks across the UEFI → runtime transition.

**Stage 3 — Kernel memory writer:**
- After kernel loads, modifies kernel memory to install the cheat driver's entry points.
- Pre-empts PatchGuard initialization (PatchGuard isn't running yet).
- Registers callbacks the cheat driver needs.

**Stage 4 — Runtime cheat driver:**
- Lives in kernel memory, callbacks wired.
- Operates as a kernel-mode cheat.

### 3.3 — BlackLotus-like deployment

Specifics (publicly documented):
- **CVE-2022-21894 ("Baton Drop")** — bypass Secure Boot via a specific vulnerable Boot Manager version.
- Bootkit signed with a legit Microsoft-signed vulnerable bootmgr that BlackLotus chain-loaded.
- Secure Boot verifies the signed bootmgr → bootkit chain-loads from it → runs with Secure Boot's blessing.
- Post-boot, the bootkit patches winload.efi and kernel in memory.

### 3.4 — Detection

EAC-observable signals:
- **Measured Boot PCR drift** — PCR 4, 5, 7, 8 change. If Epic's backend has baseline PCR values from previous sessions, drift is a flag.
- **HVCI state** — bootkits typically disable HVCI. Reported as flag.
- **Secure Boot policy state** — if bootkit updated DB/DBX, change is reported.
- **Firmware fingerprint anomaly** — SMBIOS + ACPI may have small inconsistencies if bootkit altered them.
- **`BootEfiActiveSec` + `WBCLColdboot` + `WBCLDrtmColdboot`** — the WBCL (Windows Boot Configuration Log) records every measured value. EAC reads these per [spoof.sys](analysis/01-desire-market-emulator.md).

End state: UEFI bootkit cheats work for the single-session execution, but produce a persistent fingerprint anomaly that correlates across sessions. HWID ban eventually fires.

### 3.5 — Persistence economics

- Bootkit requires physical install (one-time admin access).
- Survives OS reinstall (lives in ESP).
- Survives drive wipe if not specifically targeting ESP.
- Removed by: firmware reflash, or by explicit ESP mount + cleanup + Secure Boot DBX refresh.
- Commercial cheat price: $5000+ one-time setup, plus weekly runtime license.

---

## Part 4 — DMA cheats — deep implementation

### 4.1 — The PCIe transaction layer

Every PCIe device speaks three protocols:
- **Configuration transactions** — reading/writing device config space (vendor/device IDs, BARs, etc.).
- **Memory read/write transactions** — accessing device BARs OR host memory (if bus-master bit set).
- **I/O transactions** — legacy, deprecated for most devices.

**DMA = memory transactions where device is initiator and host memory is target.**

### 4.2 — The DMA cheat flow

```
+-------------------+          +--------------+          +---------------+
|  Control PC       |  USB 3.0 |   FPGA DMA   |   PCIe   |  Game PC      |
|  - Game state     | <------> |   Card       | <------> |  - Game proc  |
|    analyzer       |          |              |          |    memory     |
|  - Aim decision   |          |              |          |               |
+-------------------+          +--------------+          +---------------+
                                      |
                                      | Independent USB path
                                      V
                              +--------------+
                              |  kmbox-style |
                              |  input       |
                              |  injector    |
                              +------+-------+
                                     |
                                     V USB HID events
                              +---------------+
                              |  Game PC      |
                              |  mouse/kbd    |
                              |  input stack  |
                              +---------------+
```

Two devices on the game PC:
1. The DMA FPGA card (reads game memory).
2. The input injector (writes mouse/keyboard events).

Both can spoof their identity to appear as legitimate devices.

### 4.3 — PCI config space of a DMA card

**Raw FPGA (unspoofed):**
- Vendor ID: Xilinx `0x10EE`, Lattice `0x1204`, or Altera `0x1172`.
- Device ID: FPGA family + part number.
- Class: often `0x058000` (Other Memory Controller).
- Subsystem: whatever the hardware vendor filled in (often empty).

**Spoofed to look like NVMe SSD:**
- Vendor ID: Samsung `0x144D`, WD `0x15B7`, etc.
- Device ID: specific SSD model.
- Class: `0x010802` (NVMe controller).
- Subsystem: real NVMe subsystem patterns.
- Capability list: NVMe-mandatory capabilities (MSI-X, PCIe Express, etc.).

**EAC detection**:
- Walks PCI topology via its `Devi` module.
- For each device, reads capability list.
- Compares against "what capabilities would this device have if it were real."
- Specific anomalies:
  - NVMe spoof without MSI-X properly configured → flag.
  - NVMe spoof with unusual BAR sizing → flag.
  - Multiple identical subsystem IDs across devices → flag.
- Behavioral anomalies:
  - The "NVMe" never shows up as a block device in Windows → flag.

### 4.4 — Bandwidth considerations

PCIe Gen 3 x1 — approximately 985 MB/s theoretical, ~700 MB/s practical.

For a DMA cheat reading game state:
- Fortnite game state fits in ~50-200 MB of reads per second for comprehensive ESP + aim data.
- Entry-level Screamer (PCIe x1): sufficient but slow (noticeable latency for aim corrections).
- M.2 PCIe Gen 3 x4: comfortable headroom.
- PCIe Gen 4/5 x8+: luxury, used for high-refresh-rate aim operations.

### 4.5 — IOMMU / Kernel DMA Protection

When enabled (`Intel VT-d` or `AMD-Vi`), every DMA transaction goes through address translation that:
- Checks if the target physical address is in a region the device is allowed to access.
- Blocks transactions outside allowed regions.

Windows Kernel DMA Protection uses this for hot-plug devices (specifically Thunderbolt). On PCIe slots, IOMMU protection has to be explicitly configured.

**Attacker response:**
- Install DMA card in a slot that's not IOMMU-protected (cold-plug internal PCIe slot).
- Convince user to disable IOMMU in BIOS.
- Use firmware vulnerabilities to disable DMA remapping without the user knowing.

**Detection:**
- EAC reads IOMMU state. If DMA protection is off and the system has features that normally enable it, flag.

---

## Part 5 — The physical-write / MemProcFS path

Specific publicly-available tooling: **MemProcFS** (ulfrisk).

### What it is

An open-source tool that uses DMA hardware to present remote PC memory as a filesystem on the control PC. "Mount" the game PC's physical memory and browse it like files.

### Technical flow

1. Install Screamer-class DMA hardware in game PC.
2. Run MemProcFS on control PC.
3. MemProcFS configures FPGA to issue PCIe memory reads via `WinPcap`-style tooling.
4. Memory is enumerated: every process, every thread, every module.
5. Data is accessible as files: `/proc/4567/memory.dmp` is process 4567's memory, readable as an image.

### Cheat use

Trivial to write a user-mode program on the control PC that opens `/proc/<game_pid>/memory.dmp`, parses game structures (player positions, weapon states), and outputs ESP coordinates.

### Detection

Game PC side: completely invisible. No process, no driver, no hook. The reads happen on the PCIe bus.

Behavioral: the control PC acts on information faster than any human could reasonably obtain. Behavioral ML catches this over N sessions.

---

## Part 6 — GPU-executed cheats (the exotic one)

### 6.1 — Why it's theoretically interesting

- The game's rendered frame lives in GPU memory.
- CUDA/OpenCL/DirectCompute can execute on GPU alongside the game.
- GPU memory access from a sibling process doesn't go through CPU → doesn't trigger EAC's user-mode scans.

### 6.2 — The practical architecture

```
Game Process:
  CPU → D3D11/DXGI → GPU
                      |
                      |  Rendered frames
                      V
                  GPU Memory

Cheat Process (sibling):
  CPU → CUDA API → GPU
                      |
                      |  Compute kernels read framebuffer
                      V
                  Analysis results back to CPU
```

Specific implementation:
- Cheat process attaches to GPU as a CUDA context.
- Uses CUDA to enumerate textures (via `cudaGraphicsD3D11RegisterResource` or vendor-specific APIs).
- Finds the game's framebuffer / specific textures.
- Reads frame data.
- CUDA kernel analyzes frame for enemies.
- Returns ESP data to cheat's CPU side.
- Overlay draws result.

### 6.3 — Specific CUDA/OpenCL primitives of interest

- `cudaGraphicsD3D11RegisterResource` — register a D3D11 texture with CUDA.
- `cudaGraphicsMapResources` + `cudaGraphicsSubResourceGetMappedArray` — get memory handle.
- `cudaMemcpy` / `cudaMemcpyAsync` — read the texture data.

### 6.4 — Detection surface for EAC

- Enumerate GPU processes via NVAPI (`NvAPI_GetProcessList`).
- For each GPU process, check if it's a whitelisted game process.
- Non-whitelisted GPU processes while the game is running → flag.

Edge cases:
- NVIDIA GeForce Experience, Steam overlay, Discord overlay all legitimately use GPU — EAC's whitelist handles these.
- Browser windows with hardware acceleration — whitelist.
- Cheat processes that have no obvious signature on the GPU side — harder to detect.

### 6.5 — Why it's rare in deployed cheats

- Setup complexity is high; CUDA context sharing across processes is finicky.
- Game-specific texture layout knowledge required.
- Easier to just run a user-mode cheat and read process memory directly (if you have a way to defeat the handle strip).

Published as PoC in academic papers but not widely deployed.

---

## Part 7 — Process-manipulation technique catalog

Each of these has been used in cheat deployment. EAC's countermeasures are current-build state.

### 7.1 — Process hollowing

1. `CreateProcess` in `CREATE_SUSPENDED`.
2. `NtUnmapViewOfSection` on the suspended process's image base.
3. `VirtualAllocEx` at the same base.
4. `WriteProcessMemory` the cheat image.
5. Set new entry point via `SetThreadContext`.
6. `ResumeThread`.

**Catch:** PE header at base, ImageFileName in EPROCESS, LDR entries all become inconsistent → VAD walker + integrity scan catches.

### 7.2 — Process Doppelgänging

Covered in [21 Part 5](21-real-exploits-case-studies.md). TxF-based transient view trick.

**Catch:** process memory hash doesn't match on-disk file hash → integrity check catches.

### 7.3 — Process Herpaderping

Covered in [21 Part 5](21-real-exploits-case-studies.md). Modify file after section creation.

**Catch:** file-on-disk change observed via NTFS filter / MFT timestamp drift.

### 7.4 — Early Bird APC injection

Covered in [21 Part 5](21-real-exploits-case-studies.md). APC queued before initial thread resumes.

**Catch:** ETHREAD.Tcb.ApcState inspection + ETW-TI `NtQueueApcThread` events.

### 7.5 — Thread hijacking

1. Open existing thread with `THREAD_SUSPEND_RESUME | THREAD_SET_CONTEXT`.
2. `SuspendThread`.
3. `GetThreadContext`.
4. Save current RIP.
5. Set RIP to jump to cheat code (allocated separately).
6. `SetThreadContext` + `ResumeThread`.
7. Cheat code runs, eventually jumps back to saved RIP.

**Catch:** `THREAD_SET_CONTEXT` stripped by ObCallbacks on protected process. External process with thread handle gets `ACCESS_DENIED`.

### 7.6 — Atom bombing

Covered in [21 Part 5](21-real-exploits-case-studies.md).

**Catch:** ETW-TI + ObCallbacks.

### 7.7 — Ghost writing

Allocate executable memory in target, write code, but never create a thread. Instead, rely on a legitimate thread in the target to hit a specific function pointer (that you've overwritten to point to your code).

**Catch:** write-to-target requires PROCESS_VM_WRITE → ObCallback strip.

### 7.8 — Memory-mapped file injection

1. `CreateFileMapping` with cheat code.
2. `NtMapViewOfSection` on target process's handle.

**Catch:** target process handle still requires PROCESS_VM_OPERATION → ObCallback strip.

### 7.9 — Section-spray via named sections

A legitimate app creates a named section → cheat opens it by name → maps into own address space → shares memory with target without opening a handle.

**Catch:** only works if the game opens a named section itself; most games don't.

### 7.10 — Reflective DLL injection

1. Allocate executable memory in target.
2. Write a shellcode loader + DLL image to the memory.
3. Create thread at the loader's entry.
4. Loader parses DLL headers, fixes imports, calls DLL entry.

**Catch:** VAD-walker catches `VadPrivate` with executable protection; no LDR entry.

### 7.11 — Manual DLL mapping (ulfrisk's MapLibrary-style)

Mimics LoadLibrary without going through LDR:
1. Allocate memory for DLL in target.
2. Copy PE to memory.
3. Resolve imports by walking kernel-known IAT (requires kernel-side cooperation).
4. Fix relocations.
5. Call `DllMain(DLL_PROCESS_ATTACH)`.

Variation: inject via a kernel-mode writer, so step 2's `WriteProcessMemory` bypasses ObCallback strip.

**Catch:** VAD-walker sees Image-type VAD with no LDR entry → "unlinked image" flag.

### 7.12 — Section-image via NtCreateSection

1. `NtCreateSection` with `SEC_IMAGE` on cheat DLL file.
2. `NtMapViewOfSection` into target.
3. Section is now marked `Image` → shows up in VAD as Image type.

**Catch:** path resolution — section comes from a file whose path EAC can hash. If path's not in LDR or path matches rules.bin suspicious patterns → flag.

### 7.13 — ETW manipulation to bypass TI

Advanced: register as a Protected Process Light (PPL) subscriber to the TI provider, consume events before EAC does, selectively drop events relating to the cheat's own operations.

**Catch:** PPL requires specific signing; cheat doesn't have it. Attempts to register without proper signing fail. Attempts to subvert the provider itself trigger kernel integrity failures.

---

## Part 8 — Advanced debugging / observation techniques (cheat-adjacent)

Used by cheat developers for live reverse engineering of EAC itself.

### 8.1 — HyperDbg

Hypervisor-based debugger. Can set breakpoints that don't appear in Dr0-Dr3. Can step past VMProtect-virtualized code one handler at a time. Can read protected process memory without PROCESS_VM_READ by just reading physical memory.

Usage against EAC:
- Load HyperDbg before EAC service starts.
- Place EPT-based hooks on EAC's VMP entry gadgets.
- Trace handler-by-handler.
- No visible debugger state from EAC's checks.

### 8.2 — Intel PT (Processor Trace)

CPU feature that emits control-flow events to a ring buffer. A sibling process can enable PT on a game process and reconstruct the executed instruction stream.

Cheat-adjacent use: read game state via inferred control flow (which opponent the player is aiming at based on which code paths executed).

Also useful for reversing: trace EAC's handler execution to understand the VM.

Detection: `IA32_DEBUGCTL` MSR state. If PT is active on a protected process and the operator isn't a signed tracing tool, flag.

### 8.3 — Last Branch Record (LBR)

Lightweight control-flow tracing via MSR state. Same category as PT, lower overhead, less coverage.

### 8.4 — DMA-assisted live debugging

Use DMA hardware (Screamer) to read EAC's memory in real time without any OS footprint.

Technique:
1. Game PC is running EAC + Fortnite.
2. Control PC has Screamer card in game PC, runs MemProcFS.
3. Attach IDA Pro's remote debugger to MemProcFS → IDA operates on live memory of EAC's agent.
4. Set software breakpoints via memory writes — if EAC's integrity check catches, back off and use hardware breakpoints via DMA-assisted Dr0 writes.

### 8.5 — Side-channel memory reading

For reading very-well-protected memory without triggering any detection:

- **Prefetch side channel** — measure cache behavior of specific addresses to infer their contents.
- **Branch target prediction** — Spectre-style attacks that could leak protected state.
- **Row hammer** — DRAM-level bit flips, not quite side-channel but adjacent.

Rare in deployed cheats; published in academic security.

---

## Part 9 — Scripting-engine cheat architectures (where exposed)

Not Fortnite-specific but worth cataloging. Each major scripting engine in gaming:

### 9.1 — Lua (used in many MMOs, some FPS)

Attack: find a Lua binding that accepts a file path, pass a path to `/proc/<pid>/mem` or equivalent.

Caught by: engine sandbox (restrict which paths Lua can access), EAC doesn't need to — the engine owns this.

### 9.2 — JavaScript (Electron-based games, modding APIs)

Attack: use Electron's Node.js integration (if enabled) to `require('child_process').exec(...)`.

Caught by: Electron's `nodeIntegration: false` setting disables this path. Games that don't set it are vulnerable.

### 9.3 — Verse (Fortnite UEFN)

Sandboxed. Current implementation doesn't expose primitives useful for cheat execution in Battle Royale mode. UEFN Creative mode has Verse but it's scoped to its sandbox.

### 9.4 — Blueprint (UE scripting)

Compiled at build time. Runtime Blueprint execution is scoped to game logic, no direct memory access primitives.

### 9.5 — Pattern — scripting-engine abuse history

For reference: Minecraft's Lua-adjacent mods, Garry's Mod addons, Roblox Lua sandbox escapes — all have specific attack histories. Fortnite not vulnerable via this class.

---

## Part 10 — Attack synthesis — combined architectures

The highest-end cheats combine multiple techniques:

### Example commercial cheat architecture (circa 2026)

```
Boot:
  UEFI bootkit (persistence)
    → loads hypervisor driver pre-OS
      → Windows boots as VMX guest
        → EAC loads normally
          → Cheat's hypervisor hides from EAC's anti-VM checks via timing spoof
        → Cheat kernel driver loaded into kernel memory by hypervisor
          → No LDR entry, no callbacks registered that EAC sees
            → Patches EAC's pin-check function via EPT page-split
              → MITM EAC's TLS traffic, read session state
                → Spoof HWID queries via ACPI + SMBIOS + storage filter
                  → EAC's env_worker reads return consistent spoofed values

Runtime:
  Game runs normally
    → Cheat hypervisor hooks CR3 switches to track process boundaries
      → Reads game memory via EPT-level access without PROCESS_VM_READ
        → Computes ESP/aim decisions
          → Writes via another EPT-level page-split to inject input into game's input ring buffer

Operator:
  Buys cheat for $2000/month
    → Gets installer + weekly key update
      → Discord support channel for troubleshooting
```

### What survives detection

- Memory-level: hypervisor EPT magic hides everything.
- Kernel callback-level: no cheat callbacks registered.
- Driver-list level: cheat driver not in `PsLoadedModuleList`.
- VAD-level: cheat code lives in kernel memory, game process's VAD is clean.
- TPM-level: PCRs may drift (UEFI bootkit modifies measured boot). **This is the eventual loss condition.**
- Behavioral: aim statistics across hundreds of matches eventually correlate.

End state: lives for 3-6 months per hardware setup. HWID ban. Operator buys new hardware OR migrates to a different account chain.

---

## Part 11 — Where the "nothing works" line is

Direct take, after everything above:

**For the $5 public cheat tier:** nothing works for more than a session. Modern EAC's user-mode scans catch unprotected injection + overlay + hooks in under a minute. Soft-ban or kick is the common outcome.

**For the $50 private cheat tier:** works for days-to-weeks. Uses BYOVD for kernel access, spoofs HWID at user-mode level, rotates on detection waves.

**For the $500 hypervisor tier:** works for weeks-to-months. UEFI persistence + hypervisor stealth. Eventually behavioral ML catches, then HWID ban.

**For the $2000+ DMA tier:** works indefinitely at low-skill expression. Weeks at high skill. Eventually behavioral ML catches.

**For the "nation-state tier" (SMM + firmware exploit + full stealth):** essentially undetectable. Not sold commercially. Exists in APT toolkits, not consumer cheats.

**The reason most cheaters get caught:** not because the cheat was bad, but because they got greedy with it. Statistical aim perfection over hundreds of sessions is the detection.

---

## Part 12 — Closing — what this means for the research itself

Reverse engineering EAC isn't about "finding the one exploit." It's about mapping the entire detection surface so that:

- Defenders know what signals to prioritize.
- Legitimate security researchers know what Epic's model looks like.
- Private-server implementers know what has to be replicated on their backend.

Each of the techniques in this document is public research. Nothing here is a cheat distribution. Treat it as a security-research map, which is what the rest of the repo aims to be.
```
