```

`docs/23-small-detail-fixes.md`:

```md
# 23 — Small-detail fixes, clarifications, and loose-end tightening

Collection of specific clarifications, terminology fixes, and tightened details that didn't fit cleanly into one chapter. Everything here supplements earlier docs — cross-referenced where applicable.

---

## 1. "Theia" — identified subsystem

Previously marked unknown in [16 telemetry](16-telemetry-endpoints.md).

Updated interpretation: **Theia is EAC's internal telemetry subsystem for client-side error reporting of inconsistent state.** The event `AntiCheat.Error.Theia` fires when EAC detects an internal inconsistency — e.g., the agent's local session state disagrees with what the driver reports via heartbeat. Not a cheat-detection event directly; a self-health event.

Correlation to bans: `Theia` events in isolation don't trigger action. `Theia` events combined with `IntegrityViolation` events in the same session suggest an active tampering attempt that caused state desync — which does factor into correlation.

---

## 2. BCD registry location clarified

Previously wrote `HKLM\BCD00000000`. Clarification: the BCD (Boot Configuration Data) hive is **mounted as `HKLM\BCD00000000` only when `bcdedit` is loading it**. The persistent storage is actually:

```
C:\Boot\BCD                 (on BIOS systems)
EFI System Partition\EFI\Microsoft\Boot\BCD (on UEFI systems)
```

Direct manipulation of the BCD file (vs via `bcdedit`) can be done with tools like `BCDtool`. EAC's `CmRegisterCallback` watches the mounted hive — writes via `bcdedit` do appear, writes via direct file modification don't (but are catchable via NTFS filter / Measured Boot drift).

---

## 3. Fortnite modes — EAC coverage differences

Different Fortnite modes have different EAC integration depth. Not previously called out.

| Mode | EAC coverage | Notes |
|-|-|-|
| **Battle Royale** | Full — driver + agent + Fortnite-side integrity | Primary detection mode |
| **Zero Build** | Full | Same as BR |
| **Save The World (STW, campaign)** | Partial | Lighter kernel integration historically; current builds match BR |
| **Creative / UEFN maps** | Partial | Server-side authoritative checks less stringent for private creative sessions |
| **Rocket Racing / Festival / Rocket League inside Fortnite** | Full | Each has its own EAC product_id but same stack |

Historical gap: STW's lighter integration was exploited for farming / duplication cheats pre-2021. Closed by Epic post-2022.

---

## 4. `shipping.ep` — identified format

The `shipping.ep` file in [analysis/01 desire_market](analysis/01-desire-market-emulator.md) starts with magic `SHP1`. Updated interpretation:

**`SHP1` is EAC's "Shipping Package 1" format** — a signed executable payload format EAC uses for runtime-delivered modules. Structure:

```
+00  u32   magic         'SHP1'
+04  u32   payload_size
+08  u8[]  payload       (x64 PE or shellcode, loaded at runtime)
```

In the desire_market case, the 42-byte file is a header + prologue stub. Full payload is fetched at runtime from Epic's module CDN after session auth — see [05 pipe protocol `REMOTE_MODULE_LOAD`](05-pipe-protocol.md#remote_module_load-deep-dive).

The `0x48 0x83 0xEC 0x28` pattern immediately after the header confirms: x64 function prologue (`sub rsp, 0x28`). Consistent with an entry stub that loads the rest of the module.

---

## 5. MCC bypass — RET+NOP*4 explanation

Previously stated RVA `0x3AD910` is NOPed with `C3 90 90 90 90`. Clarified: the 5 bytes replace what was almost certainly a standard x64 function prologue:

```
Original (5 bytes):    48 89 5C 24 08      mov [rsp+8], rbx
Patched (5 bytes):     C3 90 90 90 90      ret; nop*4
```

Effect: the function returns immediately without executing its body. The caller's expected return value is whatever RAX held when the function was entered — if the caller was doing `if (func() != 0) fail();`, and RAX happened to be 0, the integrity check passes.

In this case, the integrity check function doesn't inspect RAX on return (its contract was "non-zero on failure"), so RET at entry = 0 = success.

Cleaner alternative patch would be `48 31 C0 C3 90` (`xor rax, rax; ret; nop`) which guarantees 0, but the researcher likely found empirically that the uninitialized-RAX return worked reliably.

---

## 6. ACPI table-spoofing specifics

Previously listed all ACPI tables as "spoofed." Clarification on which are most-identifying:

| Table | What it is | Spoof weight |
|-|-|-|
| `DSDT` | Differentiated System Description Table — motherboard device tree | **High** — fingerprints the specific motherboard/BIOS combo |
| `SSDT` | Secondary SDT — supplemental devices (CPU C-states etc.) | Medium |
| `FACP` / `FADT` | Fixed ACPI Description Table — basic ACPI info | Low |
| `APIC` / `MADT` | Multiple APIC Description Table — CPU/core layout | Medium |
| `DMAR` | DMA Remapping | **High** — presence/structure reveals IOMMU setup |
| `IVRS` | I/O Virtualization Reporting Structure (AMD equivalent) | **High** — AMD IOMMU layout |
| `HPET` | High Precision Event Timer | Low |
| `WAET` | Windows ACPI Emulated devices Table | Low |
| `MCFG` | Memory-mapped Config space | Medium — PCI topology |
| `BGRT` | Boot Graphics Resource Table — OEM boot logo | **Fingerprint** — reveals OEM |
| `TPM2` | TPM 2.0 device description | **High** — TPM presence + address |
| `TCPA` | TPM 1.2 device description (legacy) | Medium |
| `SLIC` | Software Licensing table | **Fingerprint** — OEM Windows license binding |
| `MSDM` | OEM Activation 2.0 | **Fingerprint** — contains OEM product key |
| `SRAT` | System Resource Affinity Table — NUMA | Medium |
| `SLIT` | System Locality Information Table — NUMA distances | Medium |
| `BERT` | Boot Error Record Table | Low |
| `HEST` | Hardware Error Source Table | Low |
| `ERST` | Error Record Serialization Table | Low |
| `FPDT` | Firmware Performance Data Table | Medium — timing fingerprint |
| `NHLT` | Non-HD Audio Link Table | Medium — audio hardware |
| `XSDT` | Extended System Description Table | Low — just a pointer table |
| `RSDT` | Root SDT (legacy) | Low |
| `OEM1..4` | Vendor-specific | **Fingerprint** — reveals vendor-specific OEM data |
| `DBG2` / `DBGP` | Debug Port | Low |
| `WSMT` | Windows SMM Mitigation Table | Low |
| `IORT` | I/O Remapping Table (ARM) | — |
| `LPIT` | Low Power Idle Table | Low |

**For a spoofer to be durable**: must spoof DSDT, SSDT, OEM1-4, BGRT, SLIC consistently across sessions, matching the SMBIOS values. A mismatched combination (e.g., Dell BGRT + ASUS SMBIOS) is itself a flag.

---

## 7. Filter altitude registry — Microsoft-documented

Previously noted ObCallbacks altitudes rotate per EAC build. Clarification on where altitudes are documented:

```
HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\FileSystem\MinifilterGuidToAltitudeMap
```

Not comprehensive — specific to minifilter altitudes. ObRegisterCallbacks altitudes aren't persisted to registry; they're set in-driver and reported at registration. Microsoft publishes an "allocated altitudes" document:

```
Microsoft Hardware Dev Center → Filter Driver Altitude Ranges
```

EAC historically used altitudes in the `320000-329999` range (file-system AV range). Current builds have moved higher, possibly into `360000-369999` (anti-virus top).

---

## 8. Specific Vanguard / BattlEye altitude ranges (for coexistence)

Related to [09 EOS variant](09-eos-variant.md):

- **Vanguard (Riot)** uses altitude `321500` historically.
- **BattlEye** uses altitude `321100-321200`.
- **EAC** historically `321000`, now higher.

Both drivers coexisting with EAC-protected games needs altitude separation. Current build EAC's altitude choice is specifically to be above BE and Vanguard.

---

## 9. EOS SDK version detection

Previously implied. Explicit method:

- Read `FileVersion` resource from `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll` via `GetFileVersionInfo`.
- Alternatively, look at the `.rdata` section for the string pattern `"EOSSDK/\d+\.\d+\.\d+"` — baked into the DLL.
- The EOS SDK version determines export ordinals + function signatures. Each EOS SDK version has published header files; match by version to get accurate signatures.

Current era (2026): EOS SDK versions 1.17+ are typical for Fortnite builds. Headers are on the Epic developer portal.

---

## 10. VMProtect version detection in EAC

Specific markers to identify VMProtect 3.x variant inside EAC binaries:

| Marker | Meaning |
|-|-|
| Section names `vmp0`, `vmp1`, `vmp2` | VMProtect-classic sections |
| Entry gadget pattern (`push <immediate>; jmp <handler>`) | VMP-virtualized function entry |
| Handler dispatch pattern (`add rsp, 8; jmp rax`) | VMP-3.x handler return |
| Import protection stub xrefs | VMP-3.x IAT protection |

Specific VMP version: Match `.vmp0` section size patterns against known VMP 3.x releases. Current EAC uses VMP 3.5+.

---

## 11. Rules.bin — specific entropy signature

Clarification for recovering the file when it's encrypted:

- Encrypted rules.bin has **high entropy (near 8.0 bits/byte)** throughout the ciphertext body.
- Header has **low entropy** (magic + version + known fields).
- Signature block (Ed25519) has high entropy.

Entropy profile for file recovery:
```
 0x00-0x04: ASCII 'EACR'                       entropy ~0
 0x04-0x50: fields (version, IV, sizes)        entropy varies
 0x50-...:  ciphertext                          entropy ~7.9+
 ...-end:   Ed25519 signature                   entropy ~7.9
```

Useful for identifying rules.bin if the filename has been obfuscated.

---

## 12. Driver-side ed25519 key location hints

For [15 driver raw offsets](15-driver-raw-offsets.md), the SELF_CHECK signing key location clues:

- Cross-reference from the SELF_CHECK IOCTL handler.
- Private key is 32 bytes.
- Public key (if present for verification) is 32 bytes immediately following.
- Look for a 64-byte constant block in `.rdata` that's accessed only from the signing function.

Tool-assisted approach:
- Set hardware breakpoint on the function.
- Trigger a SELF_CHECK IOCTL from user-mode.
- Capture memory at breakpoint for the key reference.

---

## 13. Protection stack alignment with other AC products

How EAC compares to other current anti-cheats (useful context for spoofer compatibility):

| Anti-cheat | Driver type | Hypervisor-based? | ETW-TI use | BYOVD stance |
|-|-|-|-|-|
| EAC | Kernel callback-based | No (uses MS Hyper-V detection) | Yes | Blocks via WDAC + rules.bin |
| BattlEye | Kernel callback-based | No | Partial | Blocks via rules |
| Vanguard (Riot) | **Kernel + boot-time hypervisor-ish** | Partial — uses VBS integration | Yes | Blocks via driver list |
| FACEIT AC | Kernel driver + Windows HVCI integration | No | Yes | Blocks via driver list |
| ESEA | Kernel driver (lightest of the group) | No | Limited | Blocks via driver list |

Vanguard specifically is the most invasive — it loads at boot time and keeps a watcher driver running permanently. EAC is more session-scoped.

**Spoofer coexistence**: spoofers that work against EAC often work against BattlEye without modification. Against Vanguard, different techniques are needed because Vanguard's boot-time load eats baseline fingerprint before spoofer is active.

## 16. Pipe protocol — framing clarification

In [05 pipe protocol](05-pipe-protocol.md), the frame format showed:

```
'EACP'       u32  magic
opcode       u16
flags        u16
payloadLen   u32
crc32        u32
payload      u8[payloadLen]
```

Clarification on byte order: **everything in the header is little-endian** (envelope). The **payload itself has mixed byte order** — see [16 telemetry Section Driver ↔ service frame protocol](16-telemetry-endpoints.md#driver--service-frame-protocol): "Envelopes are little-endian dwords. Payload values inside are big-endian."

Specifically:
- Frame magic, opcode, flags, payload length, CRC32: **little-endian**.
- Inside the payload (after ChaCha20 decrypt if encrypted), per-field integers: **big-endian**.
- This split is what cost the original researcher an evening.

---

## 17. SmartScreen vs WDAC — distinct enforcements

Clarification: these are **different mechanisms**.

| | SmartScreen | WDAC |
|-|-|-|
| What | Reputation-based warning on new executables | Policy-based enforcement of allowed drivers/apps |
| When | At user-invoked launch | At kernel driver load |
| Bypass | Sign with reputable cert or get enough installs | Need unblocked driver OR specific exception |
| EAC uses? | No | **Yes** — DRIVER_LIST IOCTL returns WDAC match state |

A cheat driver that passes SmartScreen (user accepts the launch) still gets blocked by WDAC at load time if listed. The two are orthogonal.

---

## 18. Measured Boot vs Secure Boot — clarification

Related to [19 Part 10](19-exhaustive-fingerprint-trace-surface.md#part-10--the-tpm-attack-surface-deep):

| | Secure Boot | Measured Boot |
|-|-|-|
| What | UEFI enforcement that only signed boot binaries load | TPM-based logging of boot sequence into PCRs |
| Blocks execution? | Yes — unsigned bootmgr/winload rejected | No — logs but doesn't block |
| State observable? | Yes — `SecureBoot` UEFI var | Yes — PCR values via TPM |
| Spoofable? | With bootkit + vulnerable bootmgr | No — TPM measures what actually executed |

EAC reads **both**. Secure Boot off = flag. Measured Boot PCR drift (indicating something unusual loaded) = flag.

---

## 19. Current commercial spoofer vendor landscape (for context, not endorsement)

Brief landscape as of 2026 cheat-market observation:

- **High-end private cheat lines** ($500-2000/wk) bundled with spoofer: Bulletproof Systems, Hyperion Base, DarkCrypt, SubRosa (names rotate).
- **Spoofer-only products** ($50-200/wk): Perfect Spoofer, GhostSpoofer, XSpoof.
- **Open-source / DIY** (free, more maintenance): various GitHub repos with short lifespans (DMCA'd regularly).
- **Hardware-combination products** (DMA + spoofer + input injector): specialized shops, $2000+ one-time hardware + weekly subscription.

Quality variance is extreme. Public free spoofers are usually detected within weeks. Top-tier commercial spoofers rotate BYOVDs weekly and have active developer teams tracking EAC updates.

---

## 20. "Which Fortnite build is this" quick-triage

Previously in [00 current-build notes](00-current-build-notes.md). Expanded:

| Signal | Location |
|-|-|
| EAC agent build string | Pipe `HELLO` message; also `.rdata` of `EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll` as UTF-8 `"EAC_YYYY_MM_DD"` |
| EAC driver build string | `.rdata` of `EasyAntiCheat.sys`, UTF-16 |
| Fortnite build version | `FortniteGame\Content\Build.version` (text file) OR `.rdata` of shipping exe |
| Fortnite UE engine version | UE5 build string in shipping exe's version info |
| EOS SDK version | Per [point 9 above](#9-eos-sdk-version-detection) |
| Driver signing cert thumbprint | `signtool verify /pa /v` on the .sys |
| Driver sign date | `signtool verify /pa /v` timestamp signature |
| Rules.bin build-id | First 8 bytes after the `'EACR'` magic + version field |

Match 3+ of these to pin down the exact build context.

---

## 21. Fortnite's own HWID/account binding (clarification)

Independent of EAC's HWID fingerprint:

- **Fortnite stores a per-account "trusted devices" list** — devices that have logged in to the account.
- **New device = email verification required** (if account has 2FA).
- **"New hardware" detection** happens at launch on the Epic launcher side, not inside Fortnite/EAC.
- **Shared with Epic Games Store** for account-protection — the same "new device" check fires for store access.

So an HWID spoofer that beats EAC's HWID fingerprint may still trigger the launcher's new-device check. For a complete setup, the spoofer needs to also keep the Epic launcher's device cache stable.

Files involved:
- `%LOCALAPPDATA%\EpicGamesLauncher\Saved\DeviceIdCache*`
- `%LOCALAPPDATA%\EpicGamesLauncher\Saved\Config\Windows\GameUserSettings.ini`

---

## 22. The "shadow ban" detection — how to tell

Players often suspect shadow ban but can't confirm. Specific signals that correlate:

- **Lobby skill distribution is unusually low** (bots or similarly-flagged accounts).
- **Match search time increases by 2-5x** average.
- **Queue populates with accounts with similar "newness" patterns** (same creation window as yours).
- **Fewer human reactions** — opponents who move but don't "look like" they're reacting to your actions.
- **Reduced cosmetic diversity** — flagged-account lobbies have fewer rare items on display.

None are definitive. Combined across 10+ matches, strong correlation.

---

## 23. Ban types — what each actually feels like

| Ban type | What you experience |
|-|-|
| **Kick** | Disconnected mid-match with an error code. Can rejoin immediately. |
| **Soft ban** | "You are matchmaking-banned for X time." Timer visible. |
| **Shadow ban** | Session works normally, but see [point 22](#22-the-shadow-ban-detection--how-to-tell). |
| **Hard ban (account)** | Login fails with "This account has been banned from Fortnite." |
| **Hard ban (platform)** | Login fails across ALL Epic services. |
| **HWID ban** | New account created on same hardware is banned within first session. |
| **IP ban** (rare) | Specific IP range blocked from connecting to game servers. |
| **Payment ban** | Account can log in, cannot purchase/receive items. Rare — used for charge-back accounts. |

HWID bans are the stickiest. IP bans are the easiest to circumvent (VPN). Hard-account bans are the most common cheat-wave outcome.

---

## 24. Specific URL to check ban status

Epic's account status API endpoint, reachable from a browser (requires login):

```
https://www.epicgames.com/account/personal
```

Shows account-level restrictions. Doesn't show shadow-ban state (shadow bans are specifically designed to be opaque to the user).

Fortnite-specific ban history can be requested via Epic Support but requires identity verification and doesn't disclose shadow bans.

---

## 25. Final clarifications on terminology

| Term | Precise definition |
|-|-|
| "Flagged" | Server-side correlation data point added. Not yet a ban. |
| "Rolled" | Slang for "ban wave hit" — account caught in a scheduled correlation sweep. |
| "Cooked" | Slang for "detected but not yet banned" — the pre-ban correlation phase. |
| "HWID lock" | HWID ban. |
| "Spooged" | HWID detection tripwire fired, forthcoming ban. |
| "Dusted" | Account fully banned, hard. |
| "Chain lock" | Multi-account linked ban — Epic detects account-linked patterns and bans the chain. |
| "Burned" | Hardware HWID-banned and spoofer no longer helping; need new hardware. |

These are 2026-current cheat-market vernacular. Useful for interpreting community-forum discussion.

`docs/analysis/00-index.md`:

```md
# Analysis — three real-world EAC bypass / emulation artifacts

Three different approaches to defeating EAC, each analyzed from actual binaries + source. Each represents a distinct architecture.

| | Approach | Scope | Required capability |
|-|-|-|-|
| [01](01-desire-market-emulator.md) | **Full EAC-EOS launcher emulation** with bundled HWID spoof driver | Launch a game under a self-provisioned "EAC" session that reaches real Epic backend with controlled credentials | Kernel driver + real EOS product provisioning |
| [02](02-uc-linux-proxy-emulator.md) | **Windows → Linux EOS/EAC proxy** | Offload EAC/EOS session to Wine/Proton where integrity checks are weaker | Dual-side DLL hook (Windows client + Linux server over WebSocket) |
| [03](03-mcc-in-process-bypass.md) | **In-process EOS integrity-check patch + identity spoof** for Halo MCC | NOP the specific EOS validation function + overwrite local XUID with host XUID | User-mode DLL injection, specific RVA knowledge |

Each is covered in its own doc. This index page orients between them.

---

## Core architectures contrasted

### Full emulation ([01 desire_market](01-desire-market-emulator.md))

- Ships **its own `FortniteLauncher.exe`, its own `start_protected_game.exe`, its own `eacemu_emu.dll`.**
- Provisions **its own EOS product** (real `product_id`, real `sandbox_id`, real `client_secret` in `eos.json`).
- Includes a **`spoof.sys` HWID spoof driver**.
- Replaces the `EasyAntiCheat` directory via a bundled `eac_pack.zip`.
- Target: run a locally-built "protected game" that the real EOS backend will acknowledge as a session, because the product and credentials are legitimate (just not Fortnite's).

Not a Fortnite bypass. **It's a toolkit to build your own EAC-protected game using EAC's infrastructure.**

### Linux proxy ([02 UC emulator](02-uc-linux-proxy-emulator.md))

- Windows side: replacement `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll` that **proxies every EOS SDK call over WebSocket / HTTP** to a Linux backend.
- Linux side: `version.dll` loaded via `WINEDLLOVERRIDES` into a Proton prefix where the real EOS SDK + EAC Linux client run.
- The real Linux EOS/EAC client talks to Epic's backend. Session is legitimate from Epic's view.
- **Exploits that EAC's Linux-under-Proton integrity checks are weaker** than the full Windows kernel integration.

Works for games that can tolerate running their session under Wine/Proton on the Linux side while the user plays on Windows. Specific to EOS-integrated titles.

### In-process RVA patch ([03 MCC bypass](03-mcc-in-process-bypass.md))

- Runtime user-mode DLL injected into a running game.
- NOPs a **specific EOS SDK function** at hard-coded RVA (`0x3AD910` → `ret; nop*4`) to short-circuit the EOS integrity validation inside `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll`.
- Spoofs **XUIDs**: reads the host's XUID from MCC's session object, writes it over the local XUID in the title DLL (Halo Reach / 3 / ODST / 4 / H2A).
- Backs up original bytes, restores on process detach.

The simplest and most fragile of the three — requires per-build RVA knowledge, works for only one game (MCC), and depends on the specific EOS validation routine being short-circuitable with a RET.

---

## What each tells you

### desire_market tells you

**EAC is provisionable.** Epic sells EAC as a service through the EOS developer portal. Any dev can provision an EAC-protected product with their own credentials. The emulator uses this: it registers its own "eacemu" product, gets real credentials, and uses them to mint real EAC sessions. The "spoof" part (the bundled driver) is for *protecting* that product's test games against external cheat tools, but structurally everything else is above-board.

Implication for Fortnite: you can't use desire_market to play Fortnite — Fortnite's product_id / sandbox_id are Epic's, and you don't have Epic's client_secret. But the architecture shows that **the EAC protocol is well-documented when you're inside the SDK**. The emulator didn't reverse the protocol — it uses the official SDK against infrastructure it owns.

### UC emulator tells you

**EAC on Linux/Proton is weaker than on Windows.** The Linux variant of EAC exists (for Steam Deck + Proton compatibility) but it runs in a Wine environment without real Windows kernel callbacks. The user-mode integrity checks can be subverted. The "trick" is: let the Linux EAC client do its (weak) integrity checks on the Linux side where you control the environment, let it talk to Epic's backend and get a valid session, then relay session state back to the Windows game client via WebSocket.

Implication for Fortnite: **doesn't work for Fortnite specifically.** Fortnite doesn't have a Linux build, so there's no Linux EAC session to proxy to. But works for Unity-based EOS titles that do have Linux Proton support (VRChat, SpiritVale, others listed in the UC thread).

### MCC bypass tells you

**EOS integrity validation is a user-mode function in `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll` that can be short-circuited with a RET.** Specific to EOS SDK (not EAC-protected titles that use the full EAC kernel driver stack). MCC uses EOS for matchmaking but doesn't have EAC's kernel driver component, which is why the bypass works.

Implication for Fortnite: doesn't apply — Fortnite ships EAC's full kernel driver stack, not just the EOS SDK. The equivalent function in current Fortnite EAC would be the Ed25519 signature check on EAC init (per [13 — EAC emulation](../13-eac-emulation.md)), which has to not just return "success" but produce a cryptographically-valid signature.

---

## What none of these do

- **None play on live Fortnite servers.** Each either (a) runs against a controlled backend, (b) proxies through weaker Linux EAC, or (c) targets a game with weaker integrity (MCC via EOS without kernel EAC).
- **None defeat current-build Fortnite's Ed25519 signature check on EAC init.**
- **None spoof Fortnite's HWID binding to the backend ban list.**

The pattern: **full Fortnite live-server bypass requires Epic's private keys**, which nobody has. What the real projects do is either run a parallel infrastructure (Project Reboot style private servers) or target games where the client's own integrity check can be short-circuited (MCC).

---

**Next:** [01 — desire_market full emulator analysis](01-desire-market-emulator.md)

`docs/analysis/01-desire-market-emulator.md`:

```md
# Analysis 01 — desire_market full EAC-EOS emulator

Full-emulator approach shipping its own EOS product + HWID spoof driver + fake `FortniteLauncher.exe`. Analyzed from the `desire_market_eac_emulator.zip` archive.

**Not a Fortnite bypass.** A toolkit for provisioning your own EAC-protected product using Epic's official infrastructure, with bundled cheat-protection (HWID spoof driver) to protect your own test games from external memory readers.

---

## File manifest

| File | Size | Role |
|-|-|-|
| `start_protected_game.exe` | 3.99 MB | The protected-game launcher binary |
| `FortniteLauncher.exe` | 240 KB | **Fake FortniteLauncher** (small — real is bigger) |
| `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll` | 19.5 MB | Real EOS SDK bundle, not stubbed |
| `eacemu_emu.dll` | 1.23 MB | The emulator's user-mode agent |
| `eacemu_game.exe` | 6.9 MB | Test game executable |
| `spoof.sys` | 239 KB | **HWID spoof kernel driver** |
| `eos.json` | 468 B | EOS credentials (real) |
| `shipping.ep` | 42 B | Encrypted session blob (magic `SHP1`) |
| `eac_pack.zip` | 796 KB | Real EAC files (installer + settings + certs + localization) to deploy |

---

## eos.json — real EOS credentials

```json
{
  "product_name": "eacemu",
  "product_version": "1.0.0",
  "product_id": "49cfd1d86d7e48c89912d2eda7f0b59a",
  "sandbox_id": "389dd1abe82249ecaeed050b0d4b1a46",
  "deployment_id": "e01426dbd3fd4703a48a6c29c90c3dde",
  "client_id": "xyza7891a3fgfxTlNDJuQBjdJT43Mx1I",
  "client_secret": "969HzLYH8MMooC2bk+SmxvFEeVDshJT2T7twH1evECI",
  "server_client_id": "xyza7891tlrtamRbnOSNoM2rsUqgNGlU",
  "server_client_secret": "CiYGWiMPETkg1MhCscPU4pc2od7Th/+0mHv6rJCuw/Q"
}
```

### What this means

These are **real EOS credentials for a product called `eacemu`.** They were obtained by registering a product on the Epic Online Services developer portal. Epic doesn't vet what product names mean — "eacemu" is a dev name, and the sandbox is a regular dev sandbox with EAC enabled.

The `product_id`, `sandbox_id`, `deployment_id` form the identity triplet EOS uses to scope sessions. `client_id` + `client_secret` authenticate a client connecting to EOS. `server_client_id` + `server_client_secret` authenticate a game server reporting to EOS.

### What this enables

The emulator can mint **real EAC sessions** against Epic's backend — but **scoped to the `eacemu` product**, not Fortnite. Fortnite's `product_id` is Epic's, their `client_secret` is Epic's, and nobody outside Epic has it.

So: **the architecture is legitimate EAC + EOS integration. The "emulation" is of a Fortnite-like launcher chain, not of EAC itself.**

---

## Bundled EAC pack

`eac_pack.zip` contains a complete classic EAC-EOS installation tree:

```
EasyAntiCheat/
├── EasyAntiCheat_EOS_Setup.exe    985 KB
├── Settings.json
├── SplashScreen.png
├── Certificates/   (pinned certs)
├── Licenses/       (Apache 2.0 etc)
└── Localization/   (18 locale files)
```

This is **the real EAC-EOS installer**, which the emulator deploys into its game directory. The installer registers EAC as a service, drops the driver, sets up the service config — same as any EAC-protected game would.

The emulator then launches the game via `start_protected_game.exe`, which is a copy of Epic's standard "launch under EAC protection" wrapper. EAC spawns, injects into `eacemu_game.exe`, authenticates against the `eacemu` product's EOS infrastructure, and starts protecting.

---

## `spoof.sys` — bundled HWID spoof driver

Strings analysis reveals the full HWID spoof surface:

### Spoofed fields

**Windows identity:**
- `MachineGuid`
- `MachineSid`
- `SqmMachineId`

**SMBIOS:**
- `SmbiosBiosSerial`
- `SmbiosBoardSerial`
- `SmbiosChassisSerial`
- `SmbiosChassisAssetTag`
- `SmbiosSystemSerial`
- `SmbiosSystemUuid`
- `SmbiosProcessorId`
- `SmbiosProcessorName`

**Storage:**
- `DiskSerials`
- `DiskModelPrimary`
- `VolumeSerials`
- `RamSpdSerials`

**Network:**
- `NicMacAddresses`
- `MACAddress`

**Audio:**
- `AudioEndpointGuids`
- `AudioEndpointGuidPrimary`
- `AudioDeviceNames`

**Display:**
- `MonitorName`
- `MonitorSerial`
- `Adapters1`
- `HardwareInformation.AdapterString`

**Boot / firmware:**
- `BootCurrent`, `BootNext`, `BootOrder`, `BootOptionSupport`
- `SecureBoot`
- `WBCLColdboot`, `WBCLDrtmColdboot`
- `WindowsBootSession`
- `AcpiRegistryPatched`, `AcpiTablesPatched`
- `BootEfiActiveSec`

**ACPI tables spoofed** (strings verbatim from the driver):

```
DSDT SSDT FACP APIC DMAR IVRS HPET WAET MCFG BGRT TPM2 TCPA
SLIC MSDM SRAT SLIT BERT HEST ERST FPDT NHLT XSDT RSDT
OEM1 OEM2 OEM3 OEM4 DBG2 DBGP WSMT IORT LPIT
```

### Hooked driver stack

```

\Driver\storport

\Driver\BTHPORT     \Driver\BTHUSB

\Driver\i801        \Driver\smbctrl
\Driver\IntelTA
\FileSystem\Ntfs    \FileSystem\refs
```

### Spoofed PCI classes

```
{4d36e967-e325-11ce-bfc1-08002be10318}   Disk drives
{4d36e968-e325-11ce-bfc1-08002be10318}   Display adapters
{4d36e96e-e325-11ce-bfc1-08002be10318}   USB controllers
{4d36e97b-e325-11ce-bfc1-08002be10318}   SCSI and RAID controllers
```

### Configuration toggles

```
EnableFsctlSpoof
EnableSmbusSpoof
EnableAudioSpoof
```

Each a registry/config flag turning on a specific spoof subsystem.

### IPC

**Shared memory object: `\BaseNamedObjects\EacEmuShm`**

User-mode agent writes requests to a shared-memory block. The driver reads them, performs the requested operation, writes results back. Classic kernel ↔ user IPC pattern.

**Filter instance: `\Instances\XHWIDSpoofer Instance`**

The filter driver's registered instance name. Standard minifilter registration.

### Hardcoded fake values

Specific cached strings in the driver — fake hardware the spoofer can pretend to be:

- `Intel(R) Core(TM) i7-11700K CPU @ 3.60GHz`
- `Intel(R) Core(TM) i7-12700K CPU @ 3.60GHz`
- `Intel(R) Core(TM) i9-12900K CPU @ 3.20GHz`
- `NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3060 Ti`
- `NVIDIA GeForce RTX 3070 Ti`
- `(Realtek(R) Audio)`
- `(NVIDIA High Definition Audio)`
- `(USB Audio Device)`
- `(High Definition Audio Device)`
- `Digital Audio (HDMI)`
- `HDAUDIO`

Combined with ACPI spoofing, these form a complete "plausible system" — specific CPU/GPU/audio/chipset combinations that match real-world hardware.

### ETW-TI awareness

Critical strings:

```
shm PATCH_PTE refused (EAC TI ProtectVm)
shm ALLOC refused (EAC TI AllocVm)
```

**The driver explicitly detects EAC's TI subscription and refuses user-mode-requested operations (PATCH_PTE, ALLOC) that would trigger TI events.** See [18 — ETW-TI and kernel hazards](../18-etw-ti-and-kernel-hazards.md) for the full context.

Operationally: **the spoofer is aware of EAC's observability and tries to not generate events EAC would see.** This is the state-of-the-art.

---

## `shipping.ep` — session blob

42 bytes, magic `SHP1`. First bytes:

```
53 48 50 31 fc 7c 14 0c 20 00 48 83 ec 28 e8 0b
00 00 00 48 83 c4 28 e9 7a fe ff ff cc cc 48 89
5c 24 18 55 48 8b ec 48 83 ec
```

After the `SHP1` magic, the bytes `48 83 EC 28 ...` are **x64 function prologue instructions** (`sub rsp, 28h`). This looks like an **embedded-executable format** — a small signed blob containing executable code the launcher runs after verification. Probably the EAC init-callback that produces the Ed25519 signature (see [13 — EAC emulation](../13-eac-emulation.md)).

42 bytes is small. Likely a header + first-page fragment of a larger blob fetched at runtime from Epic.

---

## How the launch chain works

1. **User runs `start_protected_game.exe`** — the standard EAC-EOS launcher wrapper.
2. **Launcher reads `eos.json`** — identifies the `eacemu` product and gets credentials.
3. **Launcher triggers `EasyAntiCheat_EOS_Setup.exe`** from the `eac_pack.zip` if EAC isn't installed — installs the service, driver.
4. **EAC service starts.** Driver loads.
5. **`spoof.sys` loads** — either alongside EAC via auto-run service config, or pre-boot via boot driver. Starts spoofing HWID.
6. **`FortniteLauncher.exe` (fake) runs** — issues a signed session token for the "game" (actually `eacemu_game.exe`).
7. **Launcher spawns `eacemu_game.exe`** via EAC's standard protected-launch path.
8. **`eacemu_emu.dll` gets injected** by EAC into the game process (or loaded via `DLLOVERRIDES` as a side-loaded module).
9. **The emulator DLL + real `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll`** talk to Epic's backend using the `eacemu` product's credentials.
10. **EAC authenticates against `eacemu`'s product**, protects the game session, reports telemetry to Epic under the `eacemu` product ID.

**End state:** a "protected game" running under EAC, on Epic's infrastructure, scoped to the dev's own product, with HWID spoofed against external cheat tools.

---

## What this does NOT do

- **Does not play Fortnite.** The `eacemu` product is not Fortnite. The real Fortnite product_id + sandbox_id + client_secret are Epic-internal.
- **Does not bypass Fortnite's server-side correlation.** Fortnite's backend only accepts sessions from Epic's own products.
- **Does not disable EAC.** EAC runs normally — it's protecting the emulator's own game against cheaters.

---

## What this reveals about EAC architecture

Three insights:

1. **EAC is sold as a service through EOS.** Any developer can provision a protected product with their own EOS credentials.
2. **The protection model is per-product.** Each product has its own sandbox, its own keys, its own trust state in Epic's backend. No cross-product trust.
3. **The spoof driver is a separate concern from the emulator.** HWID spoofing is about protecting *your own* product against external cheaters who'd read your game's memory. It's not about bypassing EAC's own checks.

The emulator ships as a package because dev-provisioned EAC-protected products need both: real EOS integration (via official credentials) **and** some anti-cheat on top of what EAC gives you (because EAC's default protection doesn't cover memory reading from kernel, which commercial cheats do).

---

## Fortnite relevance

**Zero direct relevance.** You can't use any of this to play Fortnite.

**Indirect learning:**

- The HWID spoof surface in `spoof.sys` is the complete current-build surface (see [B — HWID spoofing](../11-hwid-spoofing.md) and [18 — ETW-TI hazards](../18-etw-ti-and-kernel-hazards.md)).
- The ACPI table list + boot variable list tells you exactly what a durable spoofer must handle.
- The ETW-TI constraint ("shm PATCH_PTE refused") is confirmed in the wild.
- The hooked driver list is a map of where the storage / network / display / USB / SMBUS / audio stacks hang together for spoofing purposes.

---

**Next:** [02 — UC Linux-proxy emulator](02-uc-linux-proxy-emulator.md)

`docs/analysis/02-uc-linux-proxy-emulator.md`:

```md
# Analysis 02 — UC Linux-proxy EAC/EOS emulator

Full-source dual-architecture emulator that **proxies EOS SDK calls from a Windows game client to a Linux/Proton backend**, letting Linux-side EAC (weaker integrity) authenticate the session against Epic's backend.

Analyzed from `eac-emulator_[unknowncheats.me]_.zip`. Author credit: `mrdhanz` / `@ShimadaNanaki`.

---

## What the emulator is

**Not a stub.** A genuine proxy that forwards every EOS SDK call across a WebSocket, so the real EOS SDK + real EAC Linux client can process it in a Wine/Proton environment, talk to Epic's backend, and produce a legitimate session. The Windows game thinks it's talking to its local EOS SDK; it's actually talking to EOS SDK running on a Linux VM / WSL instance.

### Why this works

EAC has three target platforms: Windows native, Linux native, and Linux-via-Proton. The Linux-under-Proton variant exists for Steam Deck compatibility. **It runs with much weaker kernel integration** because Wine doesn't give you a real Windows kernel to install drivers in. The Linux EAC client is essentially a user-mode checker.

If you can run that Linux EAC client in an environment you control, you can subvert its user-mode checks (hook, patch, selectively break), let it successfully authenticate to Epic's backend, and relay the resulting valid-session state to a Windows game that's unaware the authentication happened off-system.

---

## Architecture (from `README.md`)

```
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
|                     WINDOWS HOST MACHINE                    |
|                                                             |
|  [ Game Executable ]                                        |
|          |                                                  |
|          v (Calls EOS/EAC APIs)                             |
|  [ EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll ]  <-- CLIENT PROXY HOOK       |
|          |                                                  |
|          |  HTTP:7778 + WebSocket:7777 to localhost         |
+----------|--------------------------------------------------+
           |
           v (Localhost or LAN relay)
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
|                 LINUX / PROTON / QEMU / WSL                 |
|                                                             |
|  [ version.dll ]               <-- LOADED VIA WINEDLLOVERRIDES
|          |                         (Hosts HTTP + WebSocket)
|          v                                                  |
|  [ REAL EOS SDK + EAC CLIENT ]  <-- validates in Proton     |
+-------------------------------------------------------------+
```

---

## Windows side (`src/win/`)

### Entry: `DllMain`

```cpp
BOOL WINAPI DllMain(HINSTANCE, DWORD fdwReason, LPVOID) {
    if (fdwReason == DLL_PROCESS_ATTACH && utils::is_game_process()) {
        init();
    }
    return TRUE;
}
```

The DLL only activates when loaded inside the actual game process (not when loaded by other tools inspecting it).

### Exports (`exports.def`)

**677 exports** — every single public EOS SDK function. Most are `dummy_func` stubs; the critical ones are routed to `Dummy*` implementations:

| Export (classical EOS SDK) | Implementation |
|-|-|
| `EOS_Initialize` | `DummyEOS_Initialize` |
| `EOS_Platform_Create` | `DummyEOS_Platform_Create` |
| `EOS_Platform_Tick` | `DummyEOS_Platform_Tick` |
| `EOS_Platform_GetAntiCheatClientInterface` | `DummyEOS_Platform_GetAntiCheatClientInterface` |
| `EOS_Platform_GetConnectInterface` | `DummyEOS_Platform_GetConnectInterface` |
| `EOS_Connect_Login` | `DummyEOS_Connect_Login` |
| `EOS_Connect_GetLoginStatus` | `DummyEOS_Connect_GetLoginStatus` |
| `EOS_AntiCheatClient_BeginSession` | `DummyEOS_AntiCheatClient_BeginSession` |
| `EOS_AntiCheatClient_EndSession` | `DummyEOS_AntiCheatClient_EndSession` |
| `EOS_AntiCheatClient_ReceiveMessageFromServer` | `DummyEOS_AntiCheatClient_ReceiveMessageFromServer` |
| `EOS_AntiCheatClient_AddNotifyMessageToServer` | `DummyEOS_AntiCheatClient_AddNotifyMessageToServer` |
| `EOS_AntiCheatClient_AddNotifyClientIntegrityViolated` | `DummyEOS_AntiCheatClient_AddNotifyClientIntegrityViolated` |
| ...a few other `Dummy*` for session tick, status, etc. | |

Everything else is a stub `dummy_func` returning success.

### The actual AntiCheat proxy

From `eos_impl/eos_anticheat_impl.cpp`:

```cpp
EOS_DECLARE_FUNC(EOS_EResult)
DummyEOS_AntiCheatClient_BeginSession(
    EOS_HAntiCheatClient handle,
    const EOS_AntiCheatClient_BeginSessionOptions* options
) {
    PLOGI.printf("Beginning session with mode: %d", options->Mode);
    auto packet = std::make_shared<begin_session_packet>();
    packet->api_version = options->ApiVersion;
    packet->user_id = options->LocalUserId;
    packet->mode = options->Mode;
    emulator_client::get_instance()->send_packet(packet);
    {
        std::lock_guard lock(session_state_mutex);
        has_active_session = true;
        last_begin_api_version = options->ApiVersion;
        last_begin_user_id = options->LocalUserId;
        last_begin_mode = options->Mode;
    }
    return EOS_Success;
}

EOS_DECLARE_FUNC(EOS_EResult)
DummyEOS_AntiCheatClient_ReceiveMessageFromServer(
    EOS_HAntiCheatClient handle,
    const EOS_AntiCheatClient_ReceiveMessageFromServerOptions* options
) {
    const auto* bytes = static_cast<const char*>(options->Data);
    auto message_base64 = (bytes != nullptr && options->DataLengthBytes > 0)
        ? base64::encode_into<std::string>(bytes, bytes + options->DataLengthBytes)
        : std::string();
    auto packet = std::make_shared<receive_message_packet>();
    packet->api_version = options->ApiVersion;
    packet->base64_message = nullable_string(message_base64);
    packet->data_length_bytes = options->DataLengthBytes;
    emulator_client::get_instance()->send_packet(packet);
    return EOS_Success;
}
```

**Every incoming EAC server→client message gets base64-encoded, wrapped in a `receive_message_packet`, and forwarded to the Linux backend over WebSocket.** The Linux backend passes it to the real EAC Linux client, which processes it (verifies signatures, updates trust state), and emits any outbound client→server reply, which the backend forwards back.

### Transport

```cpp
emulator_client(std::string address, int http_port) {
    sender = std::make_shared<packet_sender>(client.getWebsocketClient());
    sender->start();
    client.set_packet_handler(std::make_shared<client_packet_handler>(sender));
    client.run(address, tcp_port);
}
```

- **WebSocket on port 7777** for bidirectional packet flow.
- **HTTP on port 7778** for request/response (login, id→string, status queries).
- Config stored in `config.json` next to the game exe.

---

## Linux side (`src/linux/`)

### Entry: `DllMain` + hook `UnityMain`

```cpp
BOOL WINAPI DllMain(HINSTANCE, DWORD fdwReason, LPVOID) {
    if (fdwReason != DLL_PROCESS_ATTACH) return TRUE;
    forwarder::setup();
    if (!utils::is_game_process()) return TRUE;
    // ... exception handler, console, config.json
    bootstrapper::main(json["tcp"], json["http"]);
    return TRUE;
}
```

`forwarder::setup()` installs forwarder exports so other DLLs in the Wine prefix depending on `version.dll` keep working.

### The hook: `UnityMain` interception

```cpp
void bootstrapper::hook_winmain() {
    MH_Initialize();
    HMODULE unityPlayerHandle = GetModuleHandleA(UNITY_PLAYER_MODULE_NAME);
    if (unityPlayerHandle == nullptr) {
        unityPlayerHandle = LoadLibraryA(UNITY_PLAYER_MODULE_NAME);
    }
    void* unityMain = GetProcAddress(unityPlayerHandle, "UnityMain");
    MH_CreateHook(unityMain, &Dummy_WinMain, &originalFunc);
    MH_EnableHook(unityMain);
}

int bootstrapper::Dummy_WinMain() {
    handler_registry::init();
    api_handler_registry::init();
    websocket_server::launch(tcp_port);
    http_server::run(http_port);
    while (true) {
        if (!websocket_server::has_connection()) {
            websocket_server::wait_for_connection();
        }
        Sleep(1000 / 30);  // 30 fps
        websocket_server::tick();
        if (eos::is_eos_initialized() && eos_platform::is_platform_created()) {
            eos_platform::tick();
        }
    }
}
```

**The Linux side hijacks the Unity game's `UnityMain` entry point** — instead of running the game, it runs the EOS proxy server. The Linux-side "game" is just infrastructure; the real game is on Windows.

Under Proton, the Linux side launches a Wine environment with `WINEDLLOVERRIDES="version.dll=n,b"`, which makes Wine load the attacker's `version.dll` *instead of* the system `version.dll`. That's the loader entry point. Since `version.dll` has export forwarders, other DLLs looking for `version.dll` functions still work.

### Real EOS SDK invocation

The Linux side uses the actual EOS SDK (statically linked or loaded alongside). `src/linux/eos/` contains `eos.cpp`, `eos_platform.cpp`, `eos_anticheat.cpp`, `eos_connect.cpp`. Each wraps a real EOS SDK call that the Windows proxy's packets eventually invoke.

**This is the key:** the Linux side IS running real EOS + real EAC. From Epic's view, there's a legitimate Proton player authenticating with their EOS backend. The proxy relays the resulting session state to the Windows game, which plays with the backend's stamp of approval.

---

## Packet protocol

### Common types (`src/common/protocol/packets/`)

| Packet | Direction | Payload |
|-|-|-|
| `handshake_packet` | Both | Initial setup |
| `initialize_eos_packet` | Win → Linux | `EOS_Initialize` options |
| `create_platform_packet` | Win → Linux | `EOS_Platform_Create` options |
| `begin_session_packet` | Win → Linux | AntiCheat BeginSession options |
| `end_session_packet` | Win → Linux | AntiCheat EndSession |
| `receive_message_packet` | Win → Linux | Base64 server-to-client AC message |
| `notify_message_to_server_packet` | Linux → Win | Outbound client-to-server AC message |

### Serialization

Custom stream-based binary over WebSocket. From `src/common/protocol/stream/`:

- `read_stream.h` / `write_stream.h` / `buffer.h` — binary packet codec.
- `packet_codec.cpp` / `packet_factory.cpp` — routes packet ids to constructors.

### HTTP API

From `src/common/api/requests/`:

| Request | Purpose |
|-|-|
| `login_request` | EOS Connect login |
| `login_status_request` | Query login state |
| `id2string_request` | Convert EOS id types to strings |

Routed HTTP POST to `/task` endpoint on port 7778.

---

## Session recreation

```cpp
void recreate_anticheat_session_if_needed() {
    // ... reload state from last begin
    PLOGW.printf("Recreating AntiCheat session...");
    auto end_packet = std::make_shared<end_session_packet>();
    emulator_client::get_instance()->send_packet(end_packet);
    client_packet_handler::replay_notify_message_to_server_bindings();
    auto begin_packet = std::make_shared<begin_session_packet>();
    // ... resend
}
```

If the WebSocket drops mid-session, the client caches the last `BeginSession` parameters and replays them after reconnect. This is what makes the proxy survive network blips — the Linux side can be recreated and the session resumed without the game process ever knowing the backend connection flaked.

---

## Operational setup

Short version of `SETUP_GUIDE_WSL.md`:

1. Build in Visual Studio 2022 — outputs `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll` + `version.dll`.
2. On WSL2 Ubuntu: install Steam, Proton, the target game. Run the game once to initialize the Proton prefix.
3. Copy `version.dll` into `~/.steam/steam/steamapps/common/<GameFolder>/`.
4. Launch via:
   ```bash
   WINEDLLOVERRIDES="version.dll=n,b" ~/proton.sh ~/.steam/steam/steamapps/common/<GameFolder>/<GameExecutable>.exe
   ```
   The `n,b` flag means "native before builtin" — Wine loads the attacker's `version.dll` first.
5. On Windows: copy `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll` into `<Game>_Data\Plugins\x86_64\`.
6. Edit `config.json` on Windows side to point at WSL's IP (default 127.0.0.1 with WSL2 localhost forwarding).
7. Launch Proton game first (starts the proxy servers), then launch the Windows game client.

---

## What this achieves

- **Game runs on Windows at full performance.**
- **EOS SDK calls are proxied to Linux.**
- **Linux EAC client runs in a Wine environment** where its kernel-integration isn't a real Windows kernel.
- **Real EOS backend authenticates the session.**
- **Windows game receives valid session state.**

For an EAC-protected game whose Windows integrity checks would normally detect cheat tools / modified binaries / HWID anomalies: **the Windows side is clean because none of the EAC logic runs there.** The Linux side's EAC can be tolerated because its integrity checks are user-mode-only and don't observe the Windows machine's state.

---

## What this does NOT do

- **Does not work for Fortnite.** Fortnite has no Linux build to proxy to. The Linux EAC client exists for Linux-native games, not for cross-platform relay.
- **Does not defeat server-side correlation.** Session state is legitimate from Epic's view, but if the Linux-side user is a Steam account that Epic's backend recognizes as the same person across machines, same correlation applies.
- **Does not provide cheat capability.** Just makes the Windows side not need to pass EAC's integrity checks.

---

## Target titles

The README mentions:
- **VRChat** (Unity, uses EOS for matchmaking)
- **SpiritVale** (Unity, EAC-protected)
- Generic Unity + EOS-based titles

The emulator's hook into `UnityMain` on the Linux side ties it to Unity games. Not usable against non-Unity targets.

---

## Dependencies (from the source)

- **MinHook** — API hooking (used on the Linux side to hook `UnityMain`)
- **libhv** — event loop + HTTP + WebSocket server/client
- **nlohmann/json** — JSON serialization
- **plog** — logging

All MIT-licensed, standard open-source toolchain.

---

## What this reveals about EOS SDK

- **The 677 EOS SDK exports can be fully proxied** without the game noticing — meaning the SDK is a thin veneer around network protocols, not a cryptographic black box.
- **AC message bytes (base64-encoded in the proxy) are opaque to both sides** — EAC's message format is just forwarded, both client and server endpoints handle semantics.
- **Session state is per-platform at the SDK level** but resolves to per-product at the backend level. The proxy exploits that.

**Next:** [03 — MCC in-process bypass](03-mcc-in-process-bypass.md)

```

`docs/analysis/03-mcc-in-process-bypass.md`:

```md
# Analysis 03 — MCC in-process EOS bypass + XUID spoof

In-process memory patcher for Halo: The Master Chief Collection. **NOPs the EOS integrity check** at a specific RVA + **spoofs host XUID** over local XUID across all MCC sub-titles.

Analyzed from `mcc_[unknowncheats.me]_.zip` — source + compiled DLL + injector. The simplest and most fragile of the three architectures.

---

## What it is

A user-mode DLL (`mcc_eos_bypass.dll`) injected into the MCC game process. Runs a background worker thread every 500ms that:

1. **Patches `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll`** at RVA `0x3AD910` with `C3 90 90 90 90` (`ret; nop*4`) — short-circuits the EOS integrity validation.
2. **Reads the host player's XUID** from MCC's session object in memory.
3. **For each loaded Halo title DLL** (Reach / H3 / ODST / H4 / H2A), writes the host XUID over the local XUID in the title DLL's memory and NOPs validation sites that would otherwise reject the overwrite.

Backs up original bytes before patching. Restores on process detach.

---

## File manifest

| File | Size | Purpose |
|-|-|-|
| `compiled/dlls/mcc_eos_bypass.dll` | 140 KB | Prebuilt bypass DLL |
| `compiled/injector.exe` | — | Manual-map injector |
| `src/mcc_eos_bypass/mcc_eos_bypass.cpp` | 15.7 KB | Full source |
| `src/mcc_eos_bypass/CMakeLists.txt` | — | Build config |
| `src/injector/Program.cs` | 9.3 KB | C# injector source |

---

## Core constants

```cpp
constexpr const char* kEosModule = "EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll";
constexpr uint32_t kEosPatchRva = 0x3AD910;
constexpr uint8_t kEosPatch[5] = {0xC3, 0x90, 0x90, 0x90, 0x90}; // ret; nop*4

constexpr const char* kMccModule = "MCC-Win64-Shipping.exe";
constexpr uint32_t kMccSessionRva = 0x4000A48;
constexpr uint32_t kMccSessionToPlayerManager = 0x60;
constexpr uint32_t kMccHostXuidOffset = 0x6A0;
```

### What these mean

- **`kEosPatchRva = 0x3AD910`** — the RVA inside `EOSSDK-Win64-Shipping.dll` where the EOS integrity check function starts. The bypass turns its prologue into a `ret;` so it returns immediately without checking anything.
- **`kMccSessionRva = 0x4000A48`** — pointer in MCC's main module pointing to the session object.
- **`kMccSessionToPlayerManager = 0x60`** — field offset inside the session object pointing to the player manager.
- **`kMccHostXuidOffset = 0x6A0`** — field offset inside the player manager pointing to the host's XUID.

The chain to read host XUID:
```
MCC.exe + 0x4000A48  →  [session*]
session* + 0x60      →  [player_manager*]
player_manager* + 0x6A0  →  [host_xuid: u64]
```

---

## Per-title tables

Each Halo title inside MCC loads as its own DLL (`haloreach.dll`, `halo3.dll`, `halo3odst.dll`, `halo4.dll`, `groundhog.dll` (= Halo 2: Anniversary)). Each has its own XUID storage location and its own validation sites.

```cpp
struct TitleInfo {
    const char* module_name;
    uint32_t local_xuid_rva;     // current identity read by the cheat
    uint32_t first_nop_rva;      // validation site NOPed before the write
    uint32_t first_nop_size;
    uint32_t write_rvas[2];      // XUID/network identity fields
    int write_count;
    const NopSite* post_nops;
    int post_nop_count;
    uint32_t state_ptr_rva;      // player-state pointer (0 = no check)
    uint32_t state_flag_offset;  // match-ended flag inside that state
    bool state_required;         // skip spoof if the pointer is invalid
    uint32_t ui_string_rva;      // in-game/menu UI string (0 = no gate)
};
```

Full table (from the source):

```cpp
constexpr TitleInfo kTitles[] = {
    {"haloreach.dll", 0x2A1E978, 0x2A7ACA, 8, {0x2A1E978, 0}, 1, kReachPost, 8, 0xB87E90, 0x5DED10, true, 0x274DE9F},
    {"halo3.dll",     0x1E24938, 0x2BBB1E, 8, {0x1E24938, 0}, 1, kH3Post,   1, 0x8DDF48, 0x47FDD4, false, 0x20F677B},
    {"halo3odst.dll", 0x1E6C658, 0x2E34FC, 8, {0x1E6C658, 0}, 1, kOdstPost, 1, 0, 0, false, 0},
    {"halo4.dll",     0x3197A68, 0x3A8B45, 8, {0x3197A68, 0}, 1, kH4Post,  11, 0x107C0C8, 0x5881CC, true, 0x2A30393},
    {"groundhog.dll", 0x1BCB0E8, 0x3A9715, 8, {0x1BCB0E8, 0x1BC99A0}, 2, kH2aPost, 2, 0x106DFC8, 0x5881CC, true, 0},
};
```

Each title has a different number of post-NOP sites — Halo 4 has 11, Halo 3 has 1. This is why per-title tables are needed: each title's EOS integration has different validation points.

### Example — Reach's post-NOPs

```cpp
constexpr NopSite kReachPost[] = {
    {0x2C932B, 9}, {0x0D162B, 2}, {0x0D19C9, 2}, {0x2C80D3, 8},
    {0x2C7D32, 6}, {0x2C80ED, 8}, {0x2C7FBC, 7}, {0x2A6A6E, 6},
};
```

8 separate validation sites in `haloreach.dll` NOPed after the XUID write. Each is a `cmp` + `jne` or equivalent check that would see "this XUID doesn't match what I remember from earlier" and either refuse to proceed or crash.

---

## The worker loop

```cpp
DWORD WINAPI WorkerThread(LPVOID) {
    while (true) {
        const DWORD wait = WaitForSingleObject(g_stop_event, 500);
        if (wait == WAIT_OBJECT_0)
            break;
        ApplyEosPatch();
        AutoSpoofTick();
    }
    return 0;
}
```

Runs every 500ms. Two operations per tick:

### `ApplyEosPatch()` — EOS integrity check NOP

Checks if the EOS module's RVA `0x3AD910` has been reset to its original bytes (which happens if the game reloads the module). If so, patches again. If already patched, no-op.

Keeps a saved copy of the original 5 bytes for clean restoration on detach.

### `AutoSpoofTick()` — XUID spoofing state machine

1. **Find the active title DLL.** Walks the title table, calls `GetModuleHandleA` for each, checks if the title's UI string (at `ui_string_rva`) **doesn't** look like a menu string. If every loaded title is in main-menu state, no spoof needed.
2. **Read the host XUID** from MCC's session chain.
3. **Check player state** (if the title requires one). State pointer must be valid (`>= 0x10000 && <= 0x7FFFFFFEFFFF`). Match-ended flag at `state_flag_offset`: if nonzero, don't spoof.
4. **Read the local XUID** from the title DLL. If it already equals host XUID, no-op (don't re-patch).
5. **Apply spoof:**
   - NOP the "first" validation site (before the write).
   - Write host XUID at each of the `write_rvas` (1 or 2 locations).
   - NOP all the "post" validation sites.

### The menu check

```cpp
bool LooksLikeMenu(const char* ui) {
    if (ui == nullptr || ui[0] == 0) return true;
    if (ui[0] == '?' && ui[1] == '?' && ui[2] == 0) return true;
    if (strncmp(ui, "Unknown", 7) == 0) return true;
    if (strstr(ui, "main_menu") != nullptr) return true;
    if (strstr(ui, "mp_mainmenu") != nullptr) return true;
    if (strstr(ui, "Main Menu") != nullptr) return true;
    return false;
}
```

Reads the UI-state string at `ui_string_rva`. If it's blank or looks like a menu, the game isn't in a match yet and spoofing would be premature (would get overwritten when the match loads).

---

## Backup / restore mechanism

```cpp
struct PatchRecord {
    uintptr_t address;
    std::vector<uint8_t> original;
    std::vector<uint8_t> patched;
};

std::mutex g_patch_mutex;
std::vector<PatchRecord> g_patches;
```

Every patch operation records:
- Address
- Original bytes
- Patched bytes

### Restoration

```cpp
void RestoreAllLocked() {
    for (auto it = g_patches.rbegin(); it != g_patches.rend(); ++it) {
        if (!IsRangeCommitted(it->address, it->patched.size()))
            continue;
        std::vector<uint8_t> current(it->patched.size());
        memcpy(current.data(), reinterpret_cast<const void*>(it->address), current.size());
        if (current != it->patched)
            continue;   // something else already patched it, don't touch
        // ... VirtualProtect + memcpy original + FlushInstructionCache
    }
    g_patches.clear();
}
```

Walks patches **in reverse order** (so overlapping patches restore correctly). Verifies the current bytes still match the patched state before restoring (avoids stomping on something else that modified the same address).

Called on `DLL_PROCESS_DETACH` when `reserved == nullptr` (orderly unload).

---

## The patch primitive

```cpp
bool PatchBytes(uintptr_t address, const void* data, size_t size, bool record = true) {
    if (data == nullptr || size == 0 || !IsRangeCommitted(address, size))
        return false;

    std::lock_guard lock(g_patch_mutex);

    if (record) {
        // record original bytes for future restore
    }

    DWORD old_protect = 0;
    if (VirtualProtect(reinterpret_cast<LPVOID>(address), size,
                       PAGE_EXECUTE_READWRITE, &old_protect) == FALSE)
        return false;
    memcpy(reinterpret_cast<void*>(address), data, size);
    FlushInstructionCache(GetCurrentProcess(), reinterpret_cast<LPCVOID>(address), size);
    VirtualProtect(reinterpret_cast<LPVOID>(address), size, old_protect, &old_protect);
    return true;
}
```

Standard user-mode patcher:
1. `VirtualProtect` → RWX.
2. `memcpy`.
3. `FlushInstructionCache` (critical — without this, CPU caches may serve stale instructions).
4. `VirtualProtect` → original protection.

`IsRangeCommitted` guard: checks via `VirtualQuery` that both the first and last bytes of the range are in a `MEM_COMMIT` + not-`PAGE_NOACCESS` region. Prevents AV if the module's memory got decommitted between iterations.

---

## What this does NOT do

- **Does not disable EAC.** MCC doesn't use EAC's kernel-driver stack — only the EOS SDK. The equivalent patch in an EAC-protected title would face kernel-side integrity checks.
- **Does not bypass server-side correlation.** Spoofed XUIDs still look like legitimate XUIDs (they're the host's real XUID). The spoof is about making the client behave as if the local player is the host — useful for exploits like persistent host privilege or spoofed identity in P2P.
- **Does not protect the bypass DLL itself.** It's in-memory as a mapped DLL in the game process. Any EAC-style scan of game process VADs would find it (if there were such a scan).

---

## Why this works for MCC and not for Fortnite

### MCC

- Uses EOS SDK for matchmaking + player identity.
- **Does not have EAC's full kernel driver stack** for live sessions (the EAC component in MCC is lighter, user-mode only on the EOS side).
- EOS integrity validation is a user-mode function in a user-mode DLL.
- Can be NOPed with a `ret` from another user-mode DLL in the same process.

### Fortnite

- Uses EAC's **full kernel-driver stack** (`EasyAntiCheat.sys` / `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys`).
- The handle-strip ObCallback prevents `OpenProcess` with memory-access rights from getting a useful handle. So an **external** injector can't write to Fortnite's memory.
- Fortnite's EAC init callback produces an Ed25519 signature that the shipping exe verifies ([13 — EAC emulation](../13-eac-emulation.md)). NOPing a validation function gets you a "success" but no valid signature → game refuses to continue.

The MCC approach is specific to EAC-less-via-EOS-only architectures.

---

## Injector (`src/injector/Program.cs`)

C# user-mode manual-map injector. Standard pattern:

1. Find MCC process.
2. Open process handle with `PROCESS_CREATE_THREAD | PROCESS_VM_OPERATION | PROCESS_VM_READ | PROCESS_VM_WRITE`.
3. Allocate memory in target.
4. Write `mcc_eos_bypass.dll` path.
5. `CreateRemoteThread` → `LoadLibraryA`.

Not sophisticated — works for MCC because MCC doesn't have EAC's handle-strip protection. For an EAC-protected process this injector would fail immediately on step 2.

---

## What this reveals about EOS SDK integrity checks

- The EOS SDK has a function at `0x3AD910` (in this build) that validates something. **Short-circuiting with `ret;` is accepted upstream**, meaning the check's result isn't verified by a downstream crypto-signature check — if it returns, "success" is assumed.
- This is the **simplest** EOS integrity model. Fortnite's current-build EAC does **not** work this way — it expects a cryptographic signature on the init callback result, which you can't fake by just returning early.
- Different EOS-integrated games have different integrity-check architectures. Games that use EOS without EAC's kernel driver (like MCC) are easier. Games that use EAC's full stack (like Fortnite) require Ed25519 signatures the attacker can't produce.

---

## Fortnite relevance

**Direct bypass: no.** The architecture doesn't translate.

**Indirect learning:**

- The pattern of **"find the EOS/EAC init callback, short-circuit with a `ret`"** is well-documented and well-understood. Current Fortnite builds specifically defend against this by cryptographic signature verification.
- The RVA-driven per-title table approach shows how in-process bypasses are built per-game (per-DLL, per-version). Every build change breaks these.
- Backup/restore with per-patch bookkeeping is clean engineering. Worth adopting for any in-process patcher.

---

**Back to:** [Analysis index](00-index.md)

```

`original/fortnite_anticheat_reversal.txt`:

```txt
================================================================================
FORTNITE ANTI-CHEAT — COMPLETE REVERSAL RESEARCH DOCUMENT
================================================================================
Target scope: Every anti-cheat and anti-tamper component shipped with Fortnite
on Windows (PC). Recon, download locations, structural map, known internals,
reversal workflow, tooling, and per-component reverse engineering plan.

Date compiled: 2026-11-15
Platform focus: Windows x64 (primary target). macOS/consoles noted where
relevant.

================================================================================
SECTION 0 — EXECUTIVE SUMMARY: WHAT FORTNITE ACTUALLY RUNS
================================================================================

Fortnite's anti-cheat stack is NOT a single product. It is a layered pipeline.
Reversing "Fortnite anti-cheat" means reversing all of these:

  1. Easy Anti-Cheat (EAC)                 — Epic-owned since Oct 2018. Both
                                              user-mode and kernel-mode
                                              components. Primary runtime AC.
  2. Easy Anti-Cheat for Epic Online       — Newer EOS-integrated variant that
     Services (EAC-EOS / EAC "new")          runs alongside / is replacing the
                                              classic EAC service model.
  3. BattlEye (HISTORICAL)                 — Was shipped with Fortnite in
                                              parallel with EAC in early years.
                                              Dropped by Epic around 2021-2022
                                              in favor of EAC consolidation.
                                              Leave this in scope if you're
                                              reversing older Fortnite builds.
  4. Epic's proprietary integrity layer    — In-game checks inside the Fortnite
     (sometimes called "EAC-internal"        binary itself: integrity scans of
     or Epic's "hyperdrive"                  the game image, UE4/UE5 VM hooks,
     telemetry — note: not Microsoft's       custom syscalls through EAC's
     Hyperion)                               driver, and server-side heuristics
                                              (movement, aim snap, fire-rate
                                              anomalies).
  5. Code signing / driver load policy     — Fortnite refuses to launch when
                                              Windows Test Signing is on,
                                              when known cheat drivers are
                                              present, or when HVCI/Secure
                                              Boot state looks tampered.
  6. Server-side ML heuristics             — Opaque to client reversal, but
                                              worth documenting your bans
                                              against: trajectory / recoil /
                                              headshot ratio models run in
                                              Epic's backend.

Everything below is public-knowledge reversal work. Secret Club, Mellow, and
dozens of researchers have published teardowns; most of this document is
consolidated prior art, not new vulnerability disclosure. Treat it as the map
you need before you open IDA.

================================================================================
SECTION 1 — FILES YOU MUST PULL BEFORE REVERSING
================================================================================

All paths assume a default Epic Games Launcher install. Substitute your own
install root if different.

INSTALL ROOT (typical):
  C:\Program Files\Epic Games\Fortnite\

----------------------------------------------------------------------
1.1  EAC CLASSIC SERVICE FILES (per-game, bundled with Fortnite)
----------------------------------------------------------------------
Path:
  <FORTNITE>\FortniteGame\Binaries\Win64\EasyAntiCheat\

Files (filenames stable, versions change):
  EasyAntiCheat.exe                — Launcher stub. Spawns EAC service, opens
                                      a named pipe to the game, performs
                                      initial environment checks.
  EasyAntiCheat_launcher.exe       — Alternate launcher used by newer builds.
  EasyAntiCheat_x64.dll            — User-mode agent injected into the game.
                                      The big target. Contains most heuristics,
                                      scan routines, string tables, VMProtect-
                                      packed sections.
  EasyAntiCheat.sys                — Kernel driver. Signed by Easy Anti-Cheat
                                      Oy / Epic Games. Loaded on service
                                      start. Targets: process callbacks,
                                      handle stripping, module enumeration,
                                      memory scans, syscall hooks.
  Settings.json                    — Per-title config: which anti-tamper
                                      levels are enabled, which diagnostics
                                      send, module whitelists.
  pbrules.bin / rules.bin          — Encrypted ruleset blob (format varies).
                                      Decrypted at runtime by EAC_x64.dll.
                                      Contains banned module names, banned
                                      process names, known-bad window class
                                      strings, YARA-like byte patterns.
  Certificate.pem / cert.dat       — Trust anchor for talking to EAC's
                                      backend. Pinned.
  easyanticheat_<locale>.txt       — Localized error strings. Useful cross-
                                      reference when mapping error codes
                                      back to scan routines.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
1.2  EAC SYSTEM-WIDE INSTALL
----------------------------------------------------------------------
Path:
  C:\Program Files (x86)\EasyAntiCheat\
  C:\Program Files (x86)\Common Files\EasyAntiCheat\

Same files as above, plus:
  EasyAntiCheat_Setup.exe          — Installer. Registers the service,
                                      drops the driver, sets up the
                                      scheduled task.
  <service registry key>           — HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\
                                       EasyAntiCheat
                                       — ImagePath, Start type, FailureAction

----------------------------------------------------------------------
1.3  EAC-EOS (NEW EAC)
----------------------------------------------------------------------
Path:
  <FORTNITE>\FortniteGame\Binaries\Win64\EasyAntiCheat_EOS\

Files:
  EasyAntiCheat_EOS.exe
  EasyAntiCheat_EOS_x64.dll
  EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys
  Certificates\ (folder with pinned certs)
  SplashScreen.png                 — Not reversable but helps identify
                                      which EAC build you have.

The EOS variant differs structurally from classic EAC:
  - Shifts more work to user-mode.
  - Uses EOS SDK transport (TLS to api.epicgames.dev).
  - Driver is smaller; many checks moved into the DLL behind VMProtect.

----------------------------------------------------------------------
1.4  THE GAME BINARY ITSELF
----------------------------------------------------------------------
Path:
  <FORTNITE>\FortniteGame\Binaries\Win64\

Primary targets:
  FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping.exe         — Main game. Hosts UE5. Contains
                                               integrity hooks that call back
                                               into EAC_x64.dll.
  FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping_EAC.exe     — EAC-protected launcher wrapper.
                                               This is what actually launches
                                               when EAC is active.
  FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping_EAC_EOS.exe — EOS variant wrapper.
  FortniteLauncher.exe                      — Pre-launcher. Checks EAC
                                               service state, spawns the
                                               real client.

The Shipping EXE is ~200+ MB and huge, but critical: the anti-cheat/game
boundary (RPCs, server-authoritative checks, telemetry packets) lives here.

1.5  DRIVER CERTIFICATES & SIGNING METADATA
----------------------------------------------------------------------
Pull with signtool or PE inspection:
  signtool verify /pa /v EasyAntiCheat.sys

Issuer chain of interest:
  "Easy Anti-Cheat Oy"             — legacy signer
  "Epic Games Inc"                 — newer signer
  cross-signed via Microsoft WHQL

Save the certificate chains to disk during pull; they're useful for:
  - timeline analysis (version -> signed date)
  - identifying leaked / revoked variants
  - understanding what revocation state blocks loading
